# Interlude: Miagao, 1966 {.unnumbered}

[T]{.dropcap}he fiesta ran three days that year, the way it did every year, the whole town spilling out of its houses into the plaza in front of the old stone church, the one built so long ago out of coral rock and egg whites, according to every version of the story anyone ever told her, that the church itself had outlasted three separate town names. Perla Villanueva was eighteen years old, home for the fiesta from her second year of nursing school in Iloilo City, and she was standing at her Tita Pilar's food stall stacking bibingka into a wobbling tower when a young man in a shirt two sizes too big for him told her, with total confidence, that she was doing it wrong.

"You have to alternate," he said. "Big one, small one, big one. Otherwise it falls."

"It's not going to fall."

It fell. Six bibingka rolled off the table and into the dirt, and the young man, instead of looking smug about being right, dropped immediately to his knees and started rescuing what he could, brushing dust off the ones that had a chance, laughing the whole time in a way that made it impossible for Perla to stay properly annoyed with him.

"I'm Ramon," he said, still crouched in the dirt. "I teach the little ones, over at the elementary school. Second year."

"You teach children and you can't stack cake."

"I never claimed to be good at everything." He handed her back a bibingka that had survived, mostly, holding it out like an offering. "Only the things that actually matter."

She would tell this story for years afterward, quietly, to no one but herself, the exact shape of it worn smooth from private retelling the way a coin goes smooth from being carried in a pocket too long: the dust, the wobbling tower, a schoolteacher two years into his career with flour somehow already on his sleeve though he'd been nowhere near the cooking, laughing at himself before anyone else had the chance to laugh at him first.

By the second day of the fiesta he'd found an excuse to walk past her family's stall four separate times. By the third, he'd asked her Tita Pilar for permission to walk Perla home, and Tita Pilar, who liked to say she could read a man's whole character off the way he apologized for something, said yes before Perla had finished deciding whether she wanted him to.

There is a photograph from that fiesta, taken by somebody's cousin, in which both of them appear at the edge of the frame without being the subject: a young woman rescuing a stack of bibingka and a young man on his knees in the dirt, both of them laughing at something the photograph does not contain.

Neither of them ever knew the photograph existed. It is in a box in a house in Miagao and has never been looked at closely by anybody.

They were engaged two years later, in a manner that felt, at the time, entirely unremarkable: no dramatic proposal, no single moment either of them could later point to as the beginning, only a long, ordinary accumulation of Sunday walks and letters passed between Miagao and Iloilo City during the school terms, until one day it simply became understood between both families that a wedding would happen, the only real question left being when. Ramon told her, the week it became official, sitting on the low sea wall where they'd taken to talking in the evenings, that he'd never once in his life been more certain of anything, including his own name.

"You say that now," Perla told him, echoing, without either of them noticing it, the exact rhythm of the argument that had started the whole thing two years earlier.

"I'll say it every day for the rest of my life if that's what it takes to convince you."

She believed him completely. She had no reason, that evening, sitting on a sea wall with the tide going out and a whole ordinary future laid out in front of her exactly the way she'd always assumed one would be, to believe anything else.

# Interlude: Iloilo City, 1965 {.unnumbered}

[T]{.dropcap}he dormitory at the nursing school held forty-two girls in rooms built comfortably for half that number, and Perla Villanueva shared hers with three others, including, from the very first week, a sharp-tongued girl from Janiuay named Nora Bacalso who announced within an hour of meeting her that she intended to be the best nurse in their entire batch and did not particularly care who found that ambition unattractive.

They were not friends immediately. Perla found Nora abrasive; Nora found Perla soft, in the way ambitious young women sometimes misread kindness as a lack of spine. What changed it, midway through the first year, was a night shift observation rotation on the men's ward where a patient went into distress at two in the morning and the two of them, alone together in a corridor with no supervising nurse in sight, had roughly forty seconds to decide what to do and did it correctly, at the same time, without either of them needing to say a word to the other.

They walked back to the dormitory at dawn, shaking, and Nora said, staring straight ahead, "You're better under pressure than you look."

"You're kinder under pressure than you look."

"Don't tell anyone."

"Wouldn't dream of it."

They were inseparable after that, in the specific way of two people who have been frightened together at two in the morning and have discovered they can rely on each other's hands. Three years of a training that was, by any modern standard, brutal: twelve-hour clinical rotations layered on top of coursework, inspections where a supervising sister ran a white glove along a bed frame and could send a girl home for a week over dust, an English-language requirement enforced with a severity that had less to do with medicine than with the colonial inheritance of the entire curriculum, which had been built by Americans, taught in their language, and aimed, from its founding, at producing nurses who could work anywhere in the world.

Nobody said that last part out loud to the students. They didn't need to. Every girl in that dormitory understood, by the second year, what the credential in their hands was actually for, and understood equally that the ones who eventually left would be envied and the ones who stayed would be, quietly, a little bit pitied, and that neither reaction had much to do with what any individual girl actually wanted from her own life.

"Would you go?" Perla asked Nora once, near the end. "If they offered."

"In a heartbeat. Nothing here I'd be sorry to leave." Nora had said it flatly, without self-pity, a girl whose family situation Perla knew enough about not to press on. Then, after a moment: "You wouldn't. You've got the schoolteacher."

"I could go and come back. Two years is two years."

Nora had looked at her for a long moment with an expression Perla would remember for the rest of her life, decades later, in a country neither of them had seen yet on the day of that conversation.

"Sure," Nora said. "Two years is two years."

She said it in Hiligaynon, which is worth recording, because sixty years later, in a locked thrift shop in Daly City, she would say almost the same sentence to Perla's granddaughter in English and it would not carry any of the same weight, and she would notice that it didn't, and would let it go.

Neither of them cried at the graduation. Nora's family did not come; Perla's mother came and wept through the entire ceremony and then complained about the heat for the whole journey home, which is the correct way for a mother to attend a graduation and which Perla would find herself doing, decades later, at her own granddaughter's.

# Chapter 1

### The Lamay

[T]{.dropcap}he funeral home on Hickey Boulevard had a room they used for exactly this purpose, and Perla Bautista lay in it for three nights running, in the pale blue dress Liza had chosen because it was the one her mother had worn to Maya's college graduation, the closest thing in the whole closet to an outfit Perla had ever loved wearing rather than merely owned.

The dress had been a fight. Not a large one, and not one anybody would have called a fight out loud, but Maya had watched her mother stand in front of the open closet for eleven minutes on the Tuesday after, pulling things out and putting them back, and had understood by minute four that this was not really about the dress.

"The green one," Liza had said, finally, holding it up.

"She hated the green one."

"She wore it to church."

"She wore it to church because Tita Fe gave it to her and she didn't want to hurt her feelings. She complained about it every single time she put it on." Maya reached past her mother and took down the pale blue, the one with the small covered buttons at the cuff. "This one. She wore this to my graduation and she kept the picture on the credenza for five years."

Liza had looked at the blue dress for a long moment. Then she'd taken it out of Maya's hands, laid it over her arm the way you'd carry something asleep, and said, "Fine," in a tone that had nothing to do with agreement and everything to do with not being able to keep standing in that closet another minute.

Filipino wakes do not run quickly, whatever the funeral home prints on its visiting-hours card. The manager, a young man in a suit that fit him badly across the shoulders, had said seven to nine on the phone. By quarter past nine on the first night there were still thirty people in the room and a fresh tray of pancit arriving through the side door, and by the second night he had stopped mentioning the card at all and started simply unlocking the coffee machine at six and going home at ten, which Maya thought was the single most graceful piece of professional judgment she'd witnessed all year.

The first night brought maybe forty people through in polite waves. Parish women in cardigans. Two of Perla's former colleagues, both retired now, both of whom told Maya the same story about a supervisor in 1994 without either of them knowing the other had told it. Father Santos led the rosary in his unhurried baritone, five decades of Hail Marys that Maya found herself mouthing along to out of pure muscle memory, the words arriving from somewhere much further back than her adult brain, though she could not have told anyone when she'd last sat through a full rosary before this week.

The second night brought closer to eighty. Somebody located a stack of folding chairs in a closet down the hall and the room absorbed them without ever quite feeling less full. By the third night the whole thing had turned into something closer to an exhausted family reunion, grief and gossip and foil trays sharing the same crowded space with nobody finding that arrangement strange, because it wasn't strange, because this was simply what it looked like when a woman had fed several hundred people over fifty years and all of them showed up at once to say so.

She learned, over three nights, that a receiving line has a rhythm and that you can get good at it, which is a horrible thing to become good at.

Maya stood receiving-line duty beside her mother all three evenings, accepting the same condolences phrased forty different ways.

*She's with God now.*

*She's with your Lolo now.*

*She lived such a full life, apo, such a full life.*

Every one of them kindly meant. Every one of them true. And each one landing, by the third repetition, with slightly less weight than the one before it, the way any true thing said enough times in a row starts to feel like something recited rather than something felt. By the second night Maya had caught herself answering on autopilot, *thank you, thank you for coming, she would have loved that you came,* and had gone briefly cold with the horror of realizing she'd said it to four people in a row without looking at any of their faces.

She made herself look after that. It was harder and it was better.

Roughly a third of them cried. About a fifth said something Maya could tell they had prepared in the car. Two people told her, unprompted and in almost identical language, that her grandmother had lent them money and refused repayment, and neither of them appeared to know about the other.

A woman of about sixty introduced herself as somebody Perla had trained in 1991 and said, twice, that she had been terrifying, and appeared to mean it as the highest available compliment, which Maya eventually understood that it was.

An old man she didn't recognize held her hand in both of his for a long time and told her, with total conviction, that her grandmother had once talked him out of leaving his wife.

"When?"

"Nineteen eighty-six." He was maybe eighty-five, and his eyes were wet, and he seemed unembarrassed about any of it. "I was standing outside the church having a cigarette and feeling very sorry for myself. She came out and she said, Boy, are you going to smoke that or propose to it. And then she made me tell her the whole thing." He laughed, wheezing slightly. "She said I was being an idiot. Not gently. She said it like that. We were married another twenty-nine years."

He moved on down the line before Maya could ask his name, and she stood there with the vertigo of a woman learning, on night two of three, that there had been an entire population of people her grandmother had bossed into better lives without ever once mentioning it at a family dinner.

Liza held herself upright through all three nights with a brittle competence that worried Maya considerably more than open weeping would have. She greeted every guest by name. She thanked every casserole by the name of the dish itself, which struck Maya as both absurd and exactly correct, the way *thank you for the kaldereta, Tita* said something *thank you for coming* never could. She arranged the flower deliveries along the back wall by size, largest to smallest, and then rearranged them twice more over the course of the evening as new ones arrived, with the same brisk efficiency she would later bring to a hall closet.

Only once did Maya catch her doing nothing at all.

It was the second night, during the lull when most people had gone down the hall for coffee, and Liza was standing alone at the casket with one hand resting on the polished edge of it. Not praying, as far as Maya could tell. Not crying. Simply standing there, the way a person stands guard over something she is not ready to stop protecting.

Maya came up beside her and didn't say anything for a while, which she suspected was the correct move and which she had learned, she realized, from the woman lying in front of them.

"You okay, Ma?"

Liza didn't answer right away.

"She looks like herself," she said finally. "I was worried she wouldn't. Some people don't, after. You go in and it's a wax model of somebody you knew." She adjusted nothing. Her hand stayed exactly where it was. "But she looks like herself."

It was, Maya understood only much later, the truest thing her mother said out loud across all three nights, and Maya had missed it entirely at the time, because at the time it had sounded like a woman talking about a mortician's work.

# Chapter 2

### The Burial

[T]{.dropcap}he funeral Mass ran at St. Augustine's on a Saturday morning thick with the kind of fog that doesn't burn off by noon or by two or at all, so that the short procession from the church to the cemetery in Colma drove the whole distance through a gray so complete it felt, Maya thought, watching it slide past the funeral car's window, like driving through the inside of a held breath.

Colma has more dead than living, a thousand to one by most counts, a whole town of cemeteries built when San Francisco decided in 1900 that it had no more room for its own and moved the ones already there. It is an arithmetic everybody local knows and nobody says out loud at a funeral.

Maya had grown up ten minutes from it and had never found it morbid, only ordinary, the way you find anything ordinary that has always simply been there. It struck her differently that morning. Every window on the drive held another field of stones going gray into the fog, and she thought, unhelpfully, that her grandmother was about to move into one of its seventeen cemeteries, and then made herself stop thinking it, because her mother was sitting eight inches away and her mother did not need that particular thought spoken aloud in a car.

Father Santos kept the homily short and specific rather than grand, which Maya suspected he'd made deliberately, having known Perla long enough to know she'd have found anything longer embarrassing.

He talked about forty years of nursing. He talked about a woman who fed everybody within reach of her kitchen whether or not they'd been invited, which got a laugh from about a third of the church, the damp laugh of people who had all personally been fed. He said, and this was the part that got Maya, that Perla Bautista had a particular brand of dry, unimpressed humor that had gotten at least three parish priests through their own hardest weeks over the decades, himself very much included, and that he had never once in twenty-two years managed to get the last word in a conversation with her and had stopped trying somewhere around 2009.

He did not mention, because he did not know, because nobody in the family had thought to tell him, that there was an other version of her life running underneath the one two hundred people had gathered to celebrate.

Rico, her cousin, flew in from Sacramento that morning, arriving with twenty minutes to spare in a suit that had clearly been changed into in an airport bathroom, the creases still folded across the jacket where it had been packed flat.

"Don't say anything about the suit," he said, sliding into the pew.

"I wasn't going to say anything about the suit."

"You were looking at the suit."

"I was looking at you. You happened to be inside the suit."

He snorted, and Liza turned around from the pew ahead and gave both of them a look that had disciplined children in this exact building for thirty years and worked instantly on two adults in their twenties and forties, and they both sat up straighter and did not speak again until the recessional.

Tita Fe and Aunty Baby sat together in the second pew, both dry-eyed, both holding their faces very still in the way of women who have buried enough people to know precisely how much expression a face can carry before it stops being manageable. Nora sat further back, alone, three rows from the exit. Maya noticed her at the time only as one more familiar face in a long blur of familiar faces, sitting by herself at the back with her hands folded, sixty years and one ocean removed from a dormitory room in Iloilo City.

At the cemetery the fog thinned just enough for a weak, watery sun to come through, right at the end, in the way that always feels like it means something and never does.

Liza threw the first handful of dirt because tradition asked her to, and did it competently, without hesitating, so that nobody standing there had to endure the awful pause of watching someone find the courage. Then she stepped back and made room, and Maya crouched and took her own handful, and was surprised by the temperature of it, cold and slightly damp, heavier than a handful of anything ought to be.

The sound it made hitting the lid was the part she was not ready for. Nobody warns you about the sound.

There was a hold-up afterward about the cars.

Somebody had parked across the access lane and there was a fifteen-minute negotiation involving three cousins and a groundskeeper, conducted mostly in gestures at a distance, while forty people in dark clothes stood on wet grass with nothing to do.

What she remembered most clearly, years later, was not the lowering. The standing around afterward in a light rain that had started without anybody noticing, everybody being very slightly bored, waiting for a car to be moved.

They stood shoulder to shoulder at the edge of it afterward, the two of them, at the rim of a hole in the ground that contained, as far as either of them understood at that particular moment on that particular Saturday, the entire complete sum of everything their mother and grandmother had ever been. Forty years a nurse. Forty-three years a wife. Seventy-eight years a woman who fed people and bossed them into better lives and never let anybody else have the last word.

# Chapter 3

### Nine Days

[F]{.dropcap}ilipino Catholic tradition asks for nine consecutive nights of prayer after a burial, the pasiyam, and Liza kept every single one of them without exception, hosting at her own house each evening, rosary at seven and food at half past, the specific structure of it being, Maya suspected by about night three, the only thing keeping her mother upright through a loss that had not yet found anywhere to land.

The first night, twenty-six people came. Liza had prepared for forty and said so, twice, in the kitchen, to nobody.

By the fourth night it was fourteen. By the sixth, nine. It thinned exactly the way these things thin, not from anybody's failure but from the plain arithmetic of other people's lives resuming: a work trip, a sick kid, a woman in her seventies who genuinely could not drive at night anymore and had stopped pretending otherwise. Nobody said anything about the shrinking. Everybody noticed it.

Maya came every night. She was there when the rosary began at seven and there when the last person left, and she did the dishes on six of the nine nights, which she came to understand around night five was not helpfulness so much as a way of having a job.

Rico stayed the whole week, sleeping on the couch in the front room like a teenager, which at forty-one produced a running joke about his back that got funnier each night as it got less clearly a joke.

"I'm going to need a chiropractor and a priest," he announced on night five, standing up in stages.

"You're going to need to stop eating three plates," said Tita Fe.

"That's a separate issue and I've made peace with it."

Nora came twice. Both times she arrived without being specifically invited, stood at the back of the room for all five decades, and left immediately after the final Glory Be without staying for food, thanking Liza at the door with a hand on her arm and a word or two nobody else heard. Both times she was gone before Maya thought to look for her.

"She's not staying," Maya said, the second time, watching the door close.

Liza was already turning back toward the kitchen. "Nora's never stayed for anything in her life. Forty years of parish funerals, she prays and she goes." A pause, a rearrangement of trays that didn't need rearranging. "Your grandmother used to say Nora treats company like a medical procedure. Necessary, finite, and best concluded promptly."

That got a real laugh out of Maya, the first one of the whole week.

The rosary itself she found harder than she expected, and not for the reason she'd braced for. It was the repetition. The same words fifty times over, the same cadence, and by the third or fourth decade her mind would come unmoored from the meaning entirely and she'd be simply making the sounds, and then some ordinary thing would ambush her sideways in the middle of it. On night two, it was noticing that her mother had put out the good glasses, the ones Perla had given her in 1994 and which had been used, to Maya's certain knowledge, four times in thirty years. On night six, it was Aunty Baby's voice on the responses, slightly flat and slightly loud, which Maya realized, mid-decade, sounded almost exactly like her grandmother's had.

On the seventh night the power went out on the whole block for about forty minutes.

It was, in retrospect, the best night of the nine. Liza produced candles from somewhere with the immediate unfazed competence of a woman who has always known where the candles are, and the rosary went ahead by candlelight with everybody's face lit from underneath, and Rico said the room looked like a séance and Tita Fe told him to hush and then laughed so hard at her own hushing that she had to stop praying for a full decade. The lights came back on partway through the fourth mystery and everybody groaned, disappointed, and Aunty Baby said, "Turn them off again," and somebody did, and they finished in the dark on purpose.

On the sixth night, an argument broke out about whether Perla had ever actually liked the pancit at St. Augustine's or had merely been polite about it for four decades, and it ran for nearly twenty minutes and involved four adults, and was not resolved.

On the ninth night the tradition asks for a final, slightly larger meal to close the mourning period properly, and Liza cooked as she cooked for everything that actually mattered, which is to say for roughly twice the number of people expected. Kaldereta. Pancit. A leche flan she had visibly stress-tested at least twice earlier in the week, because Maya had eaten two failed versions of it out of the fridge on nights six and eight and had said nothing about either.

The rosary finished. The plates went around. And partway through the meal Aunty Baby raised a small glass of the cheap sweet wine somebody had brought and said, without standing up, in a voice pitched to carry across a table rather than a room:

"To Perla, who fed all of us for fifty years and is probably furious right now that she's not the one in the kitchen tonight."

The table went up. Not a polite laugh. The real kind, the kind with grief riding directly underneath it, the kind you can only get to through nine consecutive nights of praying the same fifty sentences in the same living room with the same nine people.

"She would be furious," Liza said, wiping her eyes with the back of her wrist. "She'd have opinions about this kaldereta."

"She'd have opinions about the flan," said Tita Fe.

"The flan is fine."

"The flan is *fine*," Tita Fe agreed, with enormous emphasis, and the table went up again.

Maya watched her mother laugh until she had to put her fork down, and understood, sitting there with a plate she'd barely touched, what the nine nights had been for. Not to finish anything. Nothing finishes grief in nine days, and anybody who has been through it knows better than to expect that. Only to give it nine specific nights of shape and company and a job to do, before everybody had to go back to their own houses and carry it alone on their own ordinary Tuesdays with nobody keeping count.

It would be three more weeks before either Maya or Liza set foot inside Perla's apartment to begin the actual work of closing it out. Three weeks that both of them would later describe, if pressed, as simply needing the time. Neither one ever said out loud that the delay was its own kind of grief, the reluctance to open a door on a life that still smelled, on the other side of it, as it always had.

# Chapter 4

### The Recipe Box

[F]{.dropcap}og sat over the hill the whole drive down, the kind that didn't burn off until noon most days in Daly City, softening the strip malls on Mission into pale rectangles of color, blurring the row of identical stucco duplexes into something almost pretty if you didn't know better. Maya had grown up inside that fog and stopped seeing it as unusual sometime around eighth grade, the way you stopped seeing your own kitchen wallpaper, and it wasn't until she'd left for college in a city that got actual seasons that she understood other people found it strange, this town that stayed the same temperature and the same color for eleven months out of twelve.

She parked two doors down from her grandmother's building because the spot in front was already taken by her mother's Camry, nose in, the way Liza always parked when she meant to be somewhere fast and leave faster. Maya sat in her own car for longer than she needed to, engine off, radio still playing a podcast she hadn't followed a word of for the last six minutes, telling herself this was just what traffic on 280 looked like on a Saturday, though there'd been no traffic at all, and she'd left with plenty of time, and the ten minutes she was about to lose sitting in a parked car outside her dead grandmother's apartment were ten minutes she was choosing, not ten minutes anyone was taking from her.

She had a key. She had had a key since she was nineteen and had used it perhaps four hundred times, and she stood outside the door with it in her hand for a second longer than necessary, because a key to a dead woman's apartment is a different object than it was five weeks ago and nobody warns you that it will feel different in your hand.

The apartment smelled as it always had. She had it before she'd gotten her key fully turned in the lock, before she'd said a single word to her mother. Garlic gone soft in oil, years of it worked into the walls the way smell works itself into a place that's cooked the same food for forty years. A trace of Vicks VapoRub, unmistakable, medicinal and sweet at once. The waxy smell of a votive candle burned down past its last inch, still sitting on the little shelf under the Santo Niño where it had probably guttered out on its own sometime in the five weeks since anyone had been there to relight it.

Maya stood in the doorway with a roll of packing tape looped over two fingers and let all of it settle into her chest at once before she made herself walk the rest of the way in.

Her mother was already three boxes deep into the hall closet, sleeves shoved up past her elbows, a Sharpie clamped sideways between her teeth like a woman defusing something delicate. She had a system going, Maya could tell at a glance, three piles arranged with military precision on the carpet: KEEP, closest to the door and smallest by half; DONATE, already spilling its first box; and a third pile, unlabeled, that Maya understood without being told was the maybe pile, the one neither of them would admit existed out loud.

"You're late," Liza said around the Sharpie, not turning around.

"Traffic on 280."

It wasn't true, and Liza probably knew it wasn't true, the way mothers generally knew when their daughters were shaving the truth to avoid a longer conversation, but she let it go, which from Liza counted as an act of real restraint. She pulled the marker out of her mouth, capped it, uncapped it again, and wrote KEEP in letters two inches tall across the top of a box before actually looking at what was inside it. Whatever she saw made her sigh, short and put-upon, and cross the word out with two decisive lines and write DONATE beneath it instead.

"Grab the other tape gun. It's on the kitchen counter, next to the coffee maker nobody's going to want because it's from 1994 and takes those little paper filters they don't even sell anymore."

"I might want it."

"You have a Keurig, apo. You told me so yourself, you were very proud of it." Liza waved a hand without looking up, dismissing both the coffee maker and any sentimental claim Maya might try to stake on it in the same gesture. "Go get the tape."

The living room hadn't changed in Maya's whole adult memory, walking past a room and understanding all at once that it would never again look exactly like this, cluttered and lived-in and slightly dusty in the particular way of a room whose owner had intended to clean it thoroughly sometime very soon and simply never gotten around to it. The maroon sofa still wore its decades-old plastic slipcover, cracking now at the seams, permanently indented with the shape of wherever Perla had sat most evenings watching her Filipino channel with the volume up too loud, claiming she couldn't hear otherwise, though everyone in the family suspected she just liked the noise filling the apartment. The gold-framed photograph of Ernesto in his one good suit, taken sometime in the seventies judging by the lapels, hung slightly crooked above the television as it had for as long as Maya could remember, and she had a sudden, absurd urge to straighten it now, as if that single correction might undo everything else that had gone wrong in the last five weeks.

The wooden Santo Niño stood on top of the television where it always had, a ring of rosary beads looped twice around its little upraised hand, the paint on its tiny crown chipped down to bare wood in one spot from decades of being dusted a little too vigorously. Someone, sometime in the last five weeks, had turned every framed photograph on the credenza face down, a whole row of them lying flat like dominoes mid-fall, and Maya didn't know if her mother had done it because she couldn't bear to be looked at by all those frozen faces yet, or because dust settled worse on glass left standing upright, and she suspected, from the careful, deliberate way each frame had been laid down rather than simply knocked over, that it was the first reason dressed up as the second.

She turned her own kindergarten photo back over on her way past, propping it against a stack of church directories so it would stay upright. Left the rest lying down. It felt, obscurely, like the correct amount of intervention.

"Aunty Baby wants the rice cooker," Liza called from the hallway. "The good one, not the little one, she was very specific. And Tita Fe already called dibs on the caldero, the big pot, so if you had your eye on that, speak up now or lose it forever to a woman who's had three of her own since 1998 and doesn't need a fourth."

"I don't want a pot, Ma."

"You say that now. Wait till you're forty and suddenly cooking for real."

"I cook for real now."

"You air fry things and call it cooking." Liza came out of the closet with an armful of church coats nobody had worn since the nineties, dropped them unceremoniously into the DONATE box, and looked at her daughter with an expression that was almost, but not quite, fond. "There's a difference, apo, and one day you'll understand it, probably around the same time you understand why your grandmother kept eleven years of Easter programs in a shoebox like they were going to be worth something someday."

They worked through the closet in a rhythm that felt older than either of them, the choreography of two women who'd cleared out closets before, for other relatives, other decades, though never one that mattered quite this much. A coat held up for inspection. A beat of silence that meant no before anyone said the actual word. A faster motion into the donate box once the decision, unspoken, had been reached. The shoebox of church bulletins, exactly as advertised, going back to 1994, kept apparently for no reason except that Perla Bautista had never once in her adult life thrown away a piece of paper with a saint's name printed at the top of it. A drawer of expired coupons organized by expiration date, which made Liza laugh out loud, a real laugh, sharp and surprised, before she could stop herself.

"She was still clipping these. Right up until the end. I found a whole stack in her purse at the hospital, coupons for a store that closed two years ago." Liza held one up, faded newsprint advertising forty cents off a can of Spam. "I used to tell her, Ma, nobody's giving you forty cents off anything anymore, this store doesn't exist. She said it didn't matter. She said cutting them out was the part she liked, not using them."

"That tracks," Maya said, and meant it, and for a second the two of them stood in the ruins of a closet laughing at a coupon for a defunct grocery store the way you laugh at the specific, useless habits of somebody you loved, the kind of laugh that had grief folded up somewhere inside it but didn't announce that fact, just let itself be funny for as long as it was allowed to be.

Further back, behind a stack of decades-old phone books nobody had the heart to be the one who threw out, Maya found a small zippered coin purse, heavy in the specific way old coins are heavy, cool and dense against her palm. She unzipped it expecting American change and found instead a fistful of centavo coins from a currency that had been out of circulation for longer than Maya had been alive, worn nearly smooth, a few of them so old the design had rubbed away to almost nothing.

"What's this," she said, holding it out.

Liza took it, weighed it in her hand exactly the way Maya had, and something crossed her face that wasn't quite sadness and wasn't quite fondness, some third thing that lived in between.

"She used to say she was saving them," Liza said finally. "For bus fare, if she ever went home." She turned the purse over once, watching the coins shift and settle. "She said it as a joke, mostly. I used to remind her the ocean was in the way, that a coin purse full of centavos wasn't going to get her very far past San Francisco Bay, and she'd laugh and say something like, you never know, apo, maybe the ocean will dry up one of these days and I'll just walk."

She dropped the purse into the KEEP box without writing a single label on it, which struck Maya as more decisive, somehow, than anything her mother had done all morning.

"Did she ever go back? After she came here?"

"A few times. The one I remember is when I was nine. Six weeks, the whole summer, I stayed with your Lolo Ernesto's sister because your grandmother said a child that age didn't need to be dragged through humidity and mosquitoes for something that was really about her, not about me." Liza set the coin purse gently into its box, arranging it like something breakable, though it wasn't. "She came back different that time. Quieter, for about a month. Then she went back to being exactly who she'd always been, and I never thought much more about it, since you don't think much about your own mother having an inner life until you're old enough to realize she absolutely did, the whole time, and you were mostly too busy being a child to notice."

It was the closest thing to an actual memory Liza had offered all morning, more real than the inventory of coats and Tupperware they'd been trading for the past two hours, and Maya wanted to follow it somewhere, wanted to ask what quieter had looked like, what a month of it had felt like to a nine-year-old watching her mother from across a kitchen table without understanding what she was watching. But Liza had already turned back to the closet, pulling out a stack of Tupperware with no matching lids, muttering something about a woman who'd never in her life managed to keep a lid with its container, and the door that had cracked open for a second swung shut again just as fast, as it kept doing, all morning, every time Maya thought she'd gotten close to something real.

By eleven the donate pile had reached the front door and Maya had begun making trips to the car in a rotation that would, by the end of the day, total nine.

# Chapter 5

### The Second Load

[A]{.dropcap}t noon they stopped and ate standing up, because there was nowhere left to sit that was not already holding something.

Liza had brought food, of course. There had never been any possibility that Liza would arrive at a task like this without food. Two containers, a bag of pandesal, and a thermos of coffee that turned out to be about forty percent condensed milk and which Maya drank all of.

"She'd have made more than this," Liza said, of her own catering.

"She'd have made enough for nine people and then apologized for it."

"She'd have said there wasn't enough and then produced a second bag from somewhere." Liza wiped her hands. "Where did she keep it, do you think? There was always a second bag. I never once saw the second bag before it appeared."

Neither of them knew. It was, Maya thought, one of maybe four hundred small operational secrets that had left the world five weeks ago and would not be coming back.

Her phone buzzed against her hip, insistent, the double-buzz she'd set for Marcus months ago and had never bothered to change even after they'd had the conversation about maybe not needing a special buzz for one person anymore, since by then they'd been together long enough that the joke had become just a fact.

She let it go twice before she gave in.

*Just need a yes or no by the 30th. I know it's a lot to ask. I'm not trying to pressure you, I just have to tell them something.*

The fellowship. Two years in Chicago, a lab that had wanted him specifically, apparently, based on a paper he'd published that Maya had skimmed and mostly not understood, full of words like differentiation and knockout that meant something very different in his field than they did in hers. An offer with a question folded inside it that he'd been too careful to ask outright, not yet, though she could feel its shape in every conversation they'd had for the last two weeks: *come with me, or don't, but tell me which, because I can't keep holding both possibilities open forever and I shouldn't have to.*

She typed *talking to my mom, promise I'll call tonight* and looked at it for a second before sending, because it wasn't a lie exactly, they were in fact standing four feet apart in a dead woman's living room, but it also wasn't a plan, and Marcus had gotten good, in the last two weeks, at hearing the silence underneath a text that sounded like an answer and wasn't one. She sent it anyway. Turned the phone face down on top of a box marked MISC and went back to sorting spoons.

"Who's that," Liza said, without much real interest, taping a box shut with three decisive strips.

"Nobody. Work thing."

"On a Saturday."

"It's a whole thing, Ma."

Liza gave her a look over the top of a box that suggested she knew exactly how much of a whole thing it probably was, and had decided, for now, out of some rare reserve of mercy, not to make Maya say any more of it out loud. It was unusual enough that Maya noticed it. Liza Bautista-Reyes did not typically deal in mercy where her daughter's avoidance tactics were concerned; she ran the world, generally, the way she ran a closet, quickly, efficiently, with the donate box always a little larger than the keep box, on the theory, often stated, that keeping things mostly just meant paying storage on grief you were going to feel eventually anyway, so you might as well feel it now and be done.

By two the closet stood empty except for wire hangers and a single winter coat neither of them could explain, since it never dropped below fifty degrees in Daly City in living memory and Perla had never once, as far as anyone knew, traveled anywhere colder. They carried the boxes out to the cars in fog that had thickened sometime around eleven into the kind that didn't fall so much as simply arrive, wrapping itself fully around the carport, the row of identical two-story buildings, the bus grinding its way up Mission toward the Colma BART station with its headlights on at noon. By the time they came back inside for the second load the whole street had gone soft and gray at every edge.

"Kitchen tomorrow," Liza said, locking the front door behind them, her keys jingling on a ring that still held a laminated photograph of Ernesto, cracked down one edge, that she'd apparently never considered removing. "I don't have it in me today. Closet was enough for one Saturday."

"I could stay and start it now. Get a head start."

"No." Liza said it fast, fast enough that Maya looked over, and her mother softened it half a beat too late, as she always did, reaching out to squeeze Maya's arm once, briefly, an unusual gesture from a woman who mostly showed affection through logistics rather than touch. "Let's just do it together tomorrow. It's not going anywhere. None of it's going anywhere."

Through the kitchen doorway, visible from where they stood locking up, sat the recipe box on top of the refrigerator, exactly where it had always sat, so long in the same spot it had left behind a pale rectangle of unfaded paint. Beside it, wedged between a chipped ceramic rooster nobody remembered the origin of and a leaning stack of Sunday missalettes going back over a decade, was the fat green spiral-bound cookbook Perla had kept since before Maya was born, its cover gone soft and gray at every corner from forty years of hands reaching for it.

Neither of them looked at it directly. Neither of them mentioned it at all.

They drove home separately through the thickening fog, taillights disappearing into it within half a block, and Maya thought about Marcus's unanswered text the whole way, replaying two years and Chicago and a lab that wanted him specifically.

# Interlude: Maya Remembers Her Father {.unnumbered}

[S]{.dropcap}he was twenty-two when it happened, old enough to be genuinely angry at how young that still felt afterward, and the memory she kept coming back to, in the weeks after her grandmother's own funeral, wasn't the hospital or the funeral itself but something smaller and stranger: her father teaching her, at maybe eight years old, how to properly check the oil in a car, crouched together beside his old Corolla in the driveway on a Saturday morning, his hand guiding hers on the dipstick with a patience she hadn't fully appreciated until she was old enough to recognize patience as a thing a person had to actively choose rather than simply possess.

Danilo Reyes had not been a man given to long speeches or dramatic declarations of feeling. He'd shown love the way he'd shown her the dipstick, hands-on, practical, more interested in making sure she knew how to do a thing herself than in being thanked for showing her. He'd taught her to check her own oil, change her own tire, argue calmly with a mechanic who was trying to overcharge her, all before she'd turned sixteen, on the theory, stated plainly and often, that he wasn't always going to be around to do it for her, a statement that had struck her at the time as merely practical and struck her now, at twenty-seven, as something closer to a man quietly preparing his daughter for a loss he had no actual way of knowing was coming as early as it did.

She had cried harder at his funeral than she remembered crying at almost anything since, a physical grief that had frightened her a little with its size, and had watched her mother, in the weeks after, do what Liza was apparently still doing five years later with an entirely different loss: stand very straight, manage every logistical detail with brisk, unimpeachable competence, and never once, in front of Maya, let the full weight of it actually show.

Maya wondered, driving home through the fog with her grandmother's cookbook still on top of a refrigerator she had not been allowed to start on, whether that particular inheritance ran further back than her mother, whether Perla had taught Liza the exact same lesson Liza had apparently absorbed and passed down without ever meaning to: that love looked like showing up, like competence, like never once making anyone else carry the actual weight of what you were feeling. It was, she was only just beginning to understand, both a gift and a cost, handed down through three generations of women who had each, in her own specific way, decided that being steady mattered more than being seen.

She missed her father in a low, ordinary way most days, the missing that didn't announce itself dramatically anymore so much as simply lived alongside everything else, the way an old injury lives in a joint long after the initial pain is gone, flaring only occasionally, unpredictably, usually over something small. Tonight it was a cookbook on top of a refrigerator. Some other night it would be something else entirely. That, she was coming to understand, was simply what grief eventually became, once enough years had passed: not an absence you kept trying to fill, but a low, familiar weather system you learned to live your whole life inside of, the same way you learned to live inside actual weather, dressing for it rather than waiting for it to pass.

She had one voicemail from him still, on an old phone in a box, ninety seconds long and entirely about a dentist appointment.

She had never played it. She had moved that phone through two apartments and had checked, twice over five years, that it still held a charge, and had not played it, and understood perfectly well what that was and had made her peace with doing it anyway.

# Interlude: Maya and Marcus, Five Years Earlier {.unnumbered}

[T]{.dropcap}hey'd met at a mutual friend's barbecue in a backyard in Berkeley, both of them standing near the cooler for different reasons, Maya because she didn't know many people there yet and a cooler was at least something to lean against, Marcus because he'd been assigned to keep the ice from running out and was taking the job with a seriousness that struck her, from three feet away, as either deeply endearing or deeply strange.

"You're really committed to that ice," she'd said.

"Someone has to be. Do you know what happens to a potato salad left out in this heat without proper ice management? Nothing good, is what."

It wasn't a particularly funny line. She'd laughed anyway, the surprised kind of laugh that came out before she'd decided to allow it, and he'd looked so genuinely pleased with himself for landing it that she found herself staying by that cooler for the rest of the party instead of circulating as she'd planned to.

He was finishing his doctorate then, immunology, a field that meant almost nothing to her beyond the fact that he clearly loved it in a way she found unexpectedly attractive, the glow of a person who'd found the one thing he wanted to spend his whole working life doing and had simply never had to negotiate himself down to something more practical. She envied that a little, she admitted to him eventually, months into dating, admitted that she'd landed in teaching partly because it was steady and good and helped people and partly because she'd never quite let herself want anything specific enough to risk not getting it.

"Teaching is specific," he told her. "You wanted it specifically enough to actually do it, every single day, for people who mostly can't say thank you properly yet. That counts."

She'd fallen for him slowly and then, it felt afterward, all at once, the way most real things seemed to happen, and five years in she still hadn't tired of watching him get excited about experimental results he tried, valiantly and mostly unsuccessfully, to explain to her over dinner, still hadn't tired of the exact quality of his attention when she talked about her own actual days, the small disasters of Room 14, listening like the details genuinely mattered to him and not just because she mattered to him, though she suspected, charitably, it was probably both.

There was a lake up in Mendocino they always said they'd get to, near a cabin a colleague of his kept and offered them every summer, and they had been saying it for three years running, always in the spring, always derailed by something small and ordinary before either of them booked a single night of it. It was the kind of plan two people keep precisely by never quite keeping it, a someday neither of them had ever felt the need to put a date on.

He'd never once pressured her about marriage, about moving in together faster than she wanted to, about any of the usual timeline anxieties her married friends kept gently warning her she'd eventually face from someone. He asked for what he needed plainly and gave her room to answer honestly, and that, more than any single romantic gesture in five years, was the thing she'd come to trust most completely about him: that he wanted an actual answer, not a comfortable evasion dressed up as one.

Which was, she would only understand much later, exactly what made the fellowship so hard. Not that he was leaving. That he was finally asking her, after five years of never pressuring her about anything, to give him the one kind of honesty she'd spent her entire adult life quietly avoiding giving to anyone at all.

They had talked about children exactly twice in five years, both times obliquely, both times in the register of two people establishing that the other was not opposed in principle.

There was a stretch in the third year when she had thought he was going to ask her something, over about two weeks, in the manner of a man circling.

He never did. She has never established what it was and has never asked, and by the time she thought to, those two weeks were two years gone and the question, whatever it had been, had presumably expired.

Neither of them had ever moved it past that, and both of them had privately noticed the other not moving it past that, and had drawn, from the not-moving, an entirely unfounded reassurance.

# Interlude: Before Marcus {.unnumbered}

[T]{.dropcap}here had been someone before him, in college, a student named Jake, two years ahead of her in the credential program, who by the end was teaching fifth grade at a school across town, and the relationship had run two and a half years, long enough that both of them had started saying things like eventually and someday without either one quite specifying what those words were meant to lead to.

It had ended not in any single dramatic argument but in the particular, unglamorous way Maya was only now, at twenty-seven, starting to fully understand as her own specific pattern: Jake had gotten an offer to move to Portland, a good position at a school he'd been trying to get into for years, and had asked her, plainly, whether she'd consider coming with him. She'd said she needed time to think about it. She'd taken that time for nearly four months, through a slow, mutual cooling that neither of them ever formally addressed, until Jake had finally, gently, stopped asking, and the relationship had ended not with a single conversation but with an accumulation of unanswered questions that eventually simply stopped getting asked at all.

She'd told her friends, afterward, that it had ended because the timing wasn't right, because Portland wasn't somewhere she'd ever pictured herself, because Jake, in the end, hadn't been quite right for her anyway. All of that had felt true at the time. It was only later, sitting with a different deadline from a different man, that she recognized the actual shape of what had happened: she had let indecision do the deciding for her, the exact same way, though she hadn't yet made the connection explicitly, that a woman she'd never properly known had let a two-year clock make an enormous decision on her behalf sixty years earlier.

Jake had married someone else two years after moving to Portland, she'd learned eventually through the passive surveillance of old mutual friends' social media, and looked, in the handful of photos she'd allowed herself to look at exactly once before never looking again, genuinely happy, which she'd been glad of, mostly, with only the faintest, private sting of wondering what her own life might have looked like if she'd simply answered the question when he'd first asked it instead of letting four months of silence answer it for her.

Jake had a habit, near the end, of asking her what she wanted for dinner and then, when she said she didn't mind, choosing, and being faintly irritated about having had to. She had thought at the time that this was a small incompatibility.

It was not a small incompatibility. It was the entire thing in miniature, performed nightly for about eight months, and neither of them ever named it.

She had never told Marcus this whole story in detail, only the broad outline: a college-era relationship, a job offer, a slow ending. It was only that autumn, watching her own grandmother's exact same pattern surface from sixty years in the past, that she understood how much of the specific shape of it she'd been quietly repeating, waiting for circumstances to make her decisions for her rather than risking the plainer, harder work of simply deciding out loud and living with whatever came of it.

Jake had emailed her once, nearly three years after Portland, a short and entirely friendly message occasioned by a mutual friend's news.

She had drafted a reply and had not sent it, and had then felt, for about a week afterward, an unearned sense of having handled something.

The draft is still in the folder. Four sentences, warm and slightly too careful, dated on a Tuesday in March, addressed to a man who by then had been married eight months and did not require it.

# Chapter 6

### What the Recipe Doesn't Say

[T]{.dropcap}he kitchen took longer than the closet had, the way kitchens always did, because every drawer held something with a story wrapped around it, and Liza wanted to relitigate roughly half of those stories before she'd let anything land in a box.

"The good knife goes to whoever cooks," she announced, holding up a chef's knife with a taped handle, its blade worn thin and silver-bright from decades against a whetstone Perla had kept in a drawer specifically for that purpose and no other. "Which is neither of us, as established yesterday, so it goes to Tita Fe, who at least uses a knife for its intended purpose and not just to open packages."

"I cook."

"You air fry, apo. We covered this."

Maya let it go, mostly because it wasn't wrong, and got down on her knees in front of the low cabinet beside the stove, working through a graveyard of mismatched Tupperware lids that matched nothing currently in the house, a rice cooker with its cord wrapped in three separate colors of electrical tape from three separate repairs over what must have been twenty years, a stack of paper bags folded flat and saved because Perla had never in her life thrown away a paper bag that still had structural integrity.

The drawer beside the stove was the worst of it, in the way that drawers are.

Six kinds of takeout chopsticks. A church key. Nine twist ties. A tape measure. Two batteries of a size nothing in the apartment took, which meant either that something had left the apartment years ago or that Perla had at some point bought the wrong batteries and had declined, on principle, to accept that.

"Throw all of it away," Liza said, without looking.

"There's a tape measure."

"Do you want a tape measure?"

"I don't have a tape measure."

"Then take the tape measure and throw the rest away."

Maya still has that tape measure. It lives in a kitchen drawer with a broadly similar population of objects around it.

Behind the bags, shoved to the very back where the cabinet met the wall, she found a cast iron pan so seasoned it was closer to black than gray, heavy enough that she had to brace her wrist to get it out one-handed.

"Ma. The pan."

Liza looked down from the step stool and went briefly, visibly still.

"That's the pan," she said.

"I know it's the pan."

"That pan is older than you. That pan is older than *me*, I think. She brought it up from the old apartment on Guttenberg and I have never once seen her wash it with soap." Liza came down two steps to look at it properly. "She'd hit me with a spoon if she saw you holding it under the tap."

"I wasn't going to."

"I know you weren't. I'm telling you in case the thought crossed."

Maya set it on the stovetop rather than in any box, and neither of them said anything about that, and it stayed there through the entire rest of the afternoon while everything around it got sorted, packed, or thrown out.

Her mother worked the upper cabinets, handing things down one at a time, narrating as she went in what she'd apparently decided, somewhere overnight, was the correct approach to this particular Sunday.

"Rooster's chipped, that can go. The missalettes, please, nobody on this earth needs eleven years of Easter programs, I don't care what she thought she was saving them for." A pause, a rustle, the scrape of a heavy object being pulled across a shelf. "The cookbook. I don't know. You still want it?"

Maya was up on her feet before she'd fully decided to be. "Yeah. I want it."

She said it faster than she meant to, faster than the question probably warranted, and her mother glanced down at her from the step stool with the particular look she gave when she suspected Maya of getting sentimental over something that was, strictly speaking, a spiral-bound binder with a stained cover and a cartoon chicken smiling on the front, a fundraiser cookbook from some church group decades gone that Perla had barely used as intended. But Liza handed it down without argument, which from a woman who'd fought about the fate of a chipped ceramic rooster for four full minutes counted as something close to tenderness.

The cookbook wasn't really a cookbook anymore, hadn't been for years. It had started as one, a mass-market spiral binder of American potluck recipes some parish group must have assembled and sold to raise money for something, back when the cover's cartoon chicken had still been a selling point rather than a curiosity. Perla had used maybe a third of its original pages exactly as printed, things like a Jell-O salad and a seven-layer dip that Maya had never seen her grandmother make. The rest of the book she'd colonized entirely, taping in recipes on whatever paper happened to be nearby: the backs of Manila envelopes gone brittle and yellow, a torn sheet from what looked like a school notebook in handwriting too careful to be Perla's own, once, memorably, the back of a phone bill from a year Maya could tell just from the logo design was sometime in the late nineties.

Every recipe carried her grandmother's own small, forward-leaning hand in the margins, notes that read less like cooking instructions and more like an ongoing argument with the page itself. *Too much vinegar, next time half.* *Ernesto says needs more garlic. Ernesto is wrong, as usual.* Beside a recipe for leche flan: *Do not trust the oven at Fe's house, runs hot, learned this the hard way in front of everybody at the reunion, 2003.*

Maya sat down at the kitchen table with it while her mother kept working the upper cabinets, laughing quietly at a note beside a rice recipe that simply read *DO NOT let Liza make this, 1987, never again, ask her why if you want a whole story,* laughing again, harder, at the margin beside a kaldereta recipe, *Tastes better the second day, don't let anyone rush you into eating it fresh, patience is a virtue nobody in this family has except me.*

"What," Liza said, from inside a cabinet.

"Nineteen eighty-seven. Rice. What did you do."

There was a pause of the wrong length.

"I don't remember."

"Ma."

"It was a very long time ago and I have chosen to release it."

"She wrote *ask her if you want a whole story.*"

"Then she should have written the story down herself instead of leaving me holding it thirty-nine years later." Liza came out of the cabinet with a stack of chipped saucers and did not make eye contact. "I put the water in twice. That's all. I put it in, I got distracted, I came back and I couldn't remember whether I'd done it, so I did it again. It came out like wallpaper paste and she served it to eleven people because there wasn't time to make more."

Maya laughed so hard she had to put the book down flat on the table.

"She never let it go," Liza said, stacking saucers with unnecessary force. "Twenty years. Every single time rice came up."

It was, Maya thought, still grinning, the most relaxed her mother had looked in five weeks.

She turned back to the book. Turned another page, then another, still smiling, actually smiling, moving through her grandmother's small opinionated handwriting like moving through rooms of a house she'd only ever seen the front hall of.

Then she reached the recipe for torta and stopped smiling entirely.

The recipe itself looked ordinary at first glance, the same brisk economy as every other page, a list of ingredients for the egg-and-ground-meat dish Filipino families fried in rounds for special occasions, no different in form from a dozen recipes around it. But underneath the ingredient list, in the margin, in ink the same color as the recipe above it but pressed harder into the paper, at a different speed entirely, as if it had been written in one motion, all at once, rather than jotted down over the course of several cooking sessions as most of her margin notes clearly had been, sat a line that wasn't cooking instruction at all.

Maya put her thumb on the page without meaning to, the way you touch a thing to confirm it's there.

She couldn't read the words themselves. She recognized the shape of the language without knowing more than a scattered handful of actual words in it, the Hiligaynon her grandmother had spoken fast and low into the phone with her own sisters back home and had never once tried to teach her grandchildren, on the stated theory, repeated often enough over the years to have become a kind of family scripture, that English would get them further in this country and there was no sense confusing a child's tongue with two languages when one would do the job. But she could read the shape of the sentence even without the words. Two short words, then a longer phrase that ran the width of the margin and curved slightly where the paper ran out, then, standing alone the way an initial stands alone when it's meant to be understood without further explanation, a single letter, underlined once, hard enough that the pen had nearly torn straight through the paper.

She looked at it for long enough that the sounds of her mother working went briefly out of the room.

"Ma." She didn't look up from the page. "What's this say?"

Liza came over from the cabinet, dish towel still looped over one shoulder, and looked down at the page for exactly as long as it took to read four short words of unfamiliar handwriting, which was not very long at all, not nearly as long as a person needed if the words were genuinely new to them.

"I don't know," Liza said, and turned back to the cabinet before the sentence had even fully left her mouth.

Maya stayed at the table. Behind her, a cabinet door opened and closed, then opened again. The saucers went into a box with more noise than saucers require.

It was, Maya would think much later, lying awake with the whole day replaying itself behind her eyes, the fastest her mother had ever in her life answered a question she actually knew the answer to.

# Chapter 7

### The Rest of the Book

[S]{.dropcap}he woke at five-forty on Saturday without an alarm and knew immediately she wasn't getting back to sleep, so she made coffee and sat down at her own kitchen table with the cookbook and went through it from the front.

Not hunting, this time. She'd already found the thing. She wanted, after six days of thinking about four words she couldn't read, to know who had written the other nine hundred she could.

Most of it was exactly what it appeared to be. Recipes. Adjustments. The practical accumulation of fifty years of feeding people.

A kaldereta with three crossed-out sauce ratios, each in a different pen, the last one dated 2011, which meant her grandmother had still been revising a dish she'd made a thousand times, at sixty-three, because she had apparently never in her life considered anything finished.

Beside a rice recipe: *Ernesto's mother's version. Better than mine. I will never admit this to anyone.*

Maya laughed out loud, alone, at six-fifteen in the morning, in an empty apartment.

A page for pancit Molo carried an entire small essay in the margin concerning a brand of wonton wrapper that had gone out of business in 1995 and the vastly inferior substitute that had replaced it, a grievance Maya could personally confirm her grandmother had continued to air at family gatherings for at least the next twenty years, usually while serving the dish, usually to people who had heard it before.

There were recipes copied from other women and credited by name, in the way of a woman who kept score:

*Nora's leche flan. She won't share the exact recipe. This is my best guess after three attempts.*

*Fe's lumpia. Far too much filling for one wrapper. Corrected here.*

*Baby's adobo, which is not hers, it is her mother's, and she knows it.*

A whole social history of one immigrant parish sitting inside a fundraiser cookbook: five decades of women trading recipes and small rivalries and real affection across the same folding tables, competing pans of pancit, nobody's version ever officially declared best.

And threaded through all of it, in the same forward-leaning hand, things that had nothing to do with cooking at all.

*Liza cried the whole way through her first day of kindergarten and so did I, in the car, alone, before I went back in to work my shift.*

*Ernesto snores worse every year. I have made my peace with this.*

*Maya asked today why the sky doesn't fall down. I did not have a good answer. Neither did the encyclopedia.*

That one stopped her for a while. She had no memory of asking it. She had no memory of the encyclopedia, though she could picture exactly where it had lived, the bottom shelf, volumes A through C missing for reasons nobody ever explained.

Further on, in pencil, undated, wedged beside a recipe for something called *quick chicken:*

*Bad week. Making this because it takes eleven minutes.*

There was nothing else on the page. No indication of which week, which year, what had made it bad. Just a woman who had needed dinner to take eleven minutes and had written down why, for nobody, and then gone on to make quick chicken at least four more times based on the wear on the page.

Maya sat with the book open on her lap for the better part of two hours, coffee going cold, laughing at some of it, going quiet at other parts.

And somewhere in the second hour she understood something that had not occurred to her once in six days of turning the margin note over.

Her grandmother was not a secretive woman.

It was sitting there in front of her, nine hundred entries deep. This was a woman who wrote down that she'd cried in a car. Who recorded a grievance about wonton wrappers for posterity. Who put *Baby's adobo is not Baby's* in permanent ink where anyone in the family might one day read it. Perla Bautista had spent fifty years documenting herself with almost no self-editing whatsoever, generously, funnily, sometimes unflatteringly, in a book she kept on top of the refrigerator where every single person in that apartment could reach it.

Which meant the one page where she'd stopped, where an entire something had been compressed down to four words and one underlined letter instead of the paragraph she'd have given a chicken recipe, was not a woman being private.

It was a woman who had gone to real effort, over decades, to keep one thing very small.

Maya closed the book. Outside, the fog was doing what it did.

# Chapter 8

### Someone Else's Dish

[S]{.dropcap}he was eight, maybe nine, small enough to still need the kitchen chair dragged over to the counter if she wanted any real view into the pan, and Perla was showing her, with the patient, repetitive good humor she brought to almost everything domestic, how to crack an egg one-handed, a skill Maya would go on to never fully master despite years of trying, cracking shell fragments into more bowls of batter than either of them ever bothered to count.

"Not this one," her grandmother had said, that particular afternoon, when Maya went into the recipe box hunting the torta recipe they made almost every other Sunday, the same one Maya had watched her mother and grandmother argue over the correct thickness of for as long as she could remember, and came out with the wrong card. It was a version with slightly different proportions, more garlic by the smell of the ingredient list alone, and a name faintly visible at the top of the card that Maya, even squinting, hadn't been able to make out. Perla took it out of her hand and gave her the usual one.

"Whose is it?"

"Nobody's business, apo." Perla had said it lightly, as she said most things that were actually, underneath the lightness, not up for discussion, ruffling Maya's hair with one hand still damp from rinsing rice. "Some recipes are just old. Like me."

"You're not old, Lola."

"I am extremely old, and don't let anybody tell you different, especially not me, because I like to complain about it." Perla had laughed, tucked the card back into the box, further back than the others, and moved on to showing Maya the egg trick again, and an eight-year-old had let the whole exchange go, the way an eight-year-old let almost everything go that didn't involve dessert or bedtime, and had not thought about it again for something like nineteen years, until she was twenty-seven and sitting at her grandmother's kitchen table with the cookbook open in front of her and a line of unreadable handwriting sitting underneath a recipe for the exact dish her grandmother had once, just that once, told her not to make.

What she remembered next, and had not thought about in nineteen years, was that her grandmother had put the card back in a different place.

Not with the others. Further back in the box, behind the divider, in the section where Perla kept things that were not recipes: a coupon, a mass card, a slip of paper with a phone number on it.

An eight-year-old registers that kind of thing without being able to do anything with it. Maya had watched the card go into the wrong section and had thought, in the unbothered way of a child, *that's not where that goes,* and had then been handed an egg.

Back in her grandmother's kitchen that Sunday, she didn't say any of this to her mother, not yet, not while the day's work was still half finished and Liza was already three cabinets deep into a fight with a set of nesting bowls that had lost most of their matching lids sometime during the Clinton administration. Instead Maya photographed the page on her phone, angling it twice to catch the light right, once for the recipe itself and once close in on the margin note, and spent the rest of the afternoon doing entirely ordinary things with an unordinary hum sitting underneath them: sorting spoons into a box, wrapping the good china plates in yesterday's newspaper, laughing out loud, a real laugh, when her mother unearthed a Tupperware container that had clearly held rice so long ago and so thoroughly that it had fused a permanent yellow stain directly into the plastic in the exact shape of the lid's rubber seal.

"Some things you don't get back," Liza said, holding the container up like courtroom evidence, and threw it in the trash without ceremony, and Maya laughed for real, because it was genuinely funny, because grief had this specific and disorienting way of sitting right alongside the stupidest possible things and making them funnier instead of sadder, at least for as long as anyone in the room was willing to let it.

She thought about the note again that night, alone in her own apartment fifteen minutes from the one they'd spent two full days emptying, Marcus's unanswered text still sitting unresolved at the top of her messages like something she hadn't yet earned the right to ignore. Opening the photo on her phone, she tried the obvious thing first, typing a rough guess at the letters into an online translator and getting back three completely different results depending on how she'd guessed the spacing, none of which resembled a coherent sentence in any language she recognized. She gave up on that after twenty minutes, annoyed at herself for expecting a free app to do what apparently required an actual human who'd grown up hearing the language spoken.

She called Priya, a friend from college, not about the note specifically, just to hear another voice that wasn't asking her anything, and talked for forty minutes about nothing in particular, a show they were both watching, a mutual friend's engagement, carefully avoiding both her grandmother and Marcus's deadline the entire time, which she understood, hanging up, had been the actual point of the call all along.

She looked at the photo one more time before bed, zoomed in until the pen strokes blurred into pure texture, then zoomed back out until they resolved again into letters she still couldn't read.

Not a spice. Not a measurement. A name, or the beginning of one, written by a woman who had apparently never thrown away a church bulletin from three decades prior but who had also, somehow, across seventy-eight years of an otherwise thoroughly documented life, never once mentioned that a version of an ordinary Sunday dish existed that belonged, by her own admission, to somebody who wasn't family.

Maya set the phone face down on her nightstand and typed out, finally, an actual answer to Marcus, something better than a promise to call. *Can we talk tomorrow instead of tonight? Not avoiding you, I promise. Just a lot going on with my grandma's stuff this week.* She looked at the message for a long moment before sending it, deciding it was true enough to count as honest even though it left out most of what was actually true, and sent it, and put the phone face down on the nightstand beside the cookbook, an unanswered question from a man who loved her and a stranger's underlined initial pressed hard enough into old paper to nearly tear through it, lying eight inches apart in the dark.

# Chapter 9

### The Forty Days

[T]{.dropcap}he fortieth day fell on a Thursday, so the misa was held on the Sunday after it, which everyone in the family agreed was fitting, though nobody could quite explain why beyond the fact that Perla had gone to nine o'clock Mass at St. Augustine's every Sunday of her adult life without missing more than a small handful of times in fifty years, once for childbirth, once for a hip replacement she'd complained about more, by every account, than the actual surgery.

The parish hall smelled like every parish hall Maya had ever set foot in across a lifetime of confirmations and funerals and fundraiser dinners: industrial coffee from three urns nobody had ever successfully gotten to brew at the same strength, folding tables draped in plastic cloths printed with a pattern nobody had chosen so much as inherited, the particular acoustic of a room built for hymn-singing that didn't quite know what to do with forty adults murmuring over paper plates of pancit and lumpia and rice. Aunties she saw perhaps twice a year hugged her hard enough to leave a faint, not unpleasant ache along her ribs. A cousin she'd played with as a child, now trailing two kids of her own, one clinging to each leg like burrs, told her she looked tired, in the specific way only family could say a thing like that and have it land as kindness rather than criticism.

Aunty Baby held court near the dessert table for most of the hour, working through the full saga of Perla and the Delacruz fence, which had begun in 2011 over eight inches of disputed property line and had escalated, across the following four years, into something that could only accurately be described as a cold war conducted entirely through potted plants.

"She moved them at night," Aunty Baby said, to a growing audience. "Not far. Two inches. Three inches. Every week, a little further onto their side, and every week they'd move them back, and neither house ever once said a single word about it to the other."

"That is not what happened," said Tita Fe.

"That is exactly what happened."

"The plants were *hers*. She was moving her own plants on her own property, and if the Delacruz family wanted to take issue with where a woman put her own bougainvillea, that was their business, not hers." Tita Fe delivered this with the total conviction of a woman who had not been present for any of it.

"Fe. She had a tape measure."

"She had a tape measure because she was a *precise woman*."

"She had a tape measure because it was a *war*," said Aunty Baby, delighted, and half the room went up, loudly enough to draw looks from the four older ladies still working through a rosary in the corner, who did not stop praying but did, collectively and without discussion, turn their chairs about fifteen degrees away.
