For a few minutes the hall held two separate versions of the same argument running simultaneously, which struck Maya, watching from beside the coffee urns, as precisely the kind of chaos her grandmother would have found enormously satisfying to have caused from inside a casket forty days in the ground.

Tita Nora found her at the dessert table after the second retelling had wound down. Maya had been standing there a while by then with a slice of leche flan she had no intention of eating, holding it mostly to have something to do with her hands.

"You look like your Lola did when she was worried about something."

Nora said it without preamble, as she said most things, a woman who had apparently decided somewhere in her seventies that small talk was a young person's inefficiency she no longer had the time for. She'd been Perla's classmate at the nursing school back in Iloilo, sixty years and an ocean removed from that fact, and she still stood the way Maya imagined you stood if you had once been inspected daily by a woman with a white glove and had never fully unlearned it.

"What is it."

Maya hesitated. It was, objectively, the wrong room. Forty people, three coffee urns, an argument about a fence still audibly in progress behind her.

She pulled out her phone anyway.

"I found this in her cookbook. Under a torta recipe. I can't read it, it's Hiligaynon I think, but I wondered if you could tell me what it says."

Nora took the phone with both hands, the careful way an older woman takes something she intends to examine properly rather than glance at. She tilted it slightly toward the light from the window. She held it, Maya noticed, a beat longer than reading four short words could possibly require.

Something moved across her face. Maya had seen it once before, seven days earlier, on her own mother's face, over this exact page.

"Where did you find this."

"In the cookbook. Why, what does it say?"

"Old handwriting."

"Tita, that's not..."

"You should eat something, apo." Nora handed the phone back and patted Maya's forearm twice, briskly, a gesture Maya would come to recognize over the following months as the woman's universal signal that a conversation had concluded whether or not the other party agreed. "You're thin. Your grandmother would say the same thing and she'd say it louder."

"Would she."

"She'd have said it three times by now and put a plate in your hand." Nora was already half turned away. Then she stopped, and for a second something in the set of her shoulders went less certain than anything else about her that afternoon. "It's an old page, apo. Some pages are just old."

It was, almost exactly, what Perla herself had said to an eight-year-old standing on a kitchen chair nineteen years earlier, and Maya recognized it at once and did not say so.

Nora moved off toward the coffee urns without looking back, and Maya stood there holding a slice of flan she still had no intention of eating, watching a woman who had known her grandmother since they were both seventeen years old cross a crowded room with considerable purpose to be somewhere else.

Two people now. Two women who had looked at four unfamiliar words and gone, visibly, somewhere else entirely in their own faces before they'd even finished reading them through.

# Chapter 10

### Not Yours to Open

[M]{.dropcap}aya drove over on a Tuesday, unannounced, with a container of Sunday's leftover pancit riding on the passenger seat as her stated reason for coming.

Neither of them mentioned the pancit once.

Her mother was at the stove when she let herself in, stirring something that did not need stirring, which Maya had understood since roughly age fourteen was what her mother did with her hands when she suspected a conversation was inbound and wanted a reason not to be looking at anybody during it. The kitchen smelled like onions and the radio was on low, tuned to the station that played standards, which Liza claimed to find boring and had never changed.

Maya set the folder on the counter between them.

Inside it: the photograph of the page, printed out and blown up to twice its actual size at the copy shop on Mission, and beneath that, three separate Google Translate attempts at the phrase, each producing a different result, none of them producing a sentence.

"I just want to know what it says, Ma. That's all I'm asking."

"It says nothing." The spoon kept moving. "It's an old recipe with an old woman's chicken scratch written next to it. You're building a whole story out of a grocery list."

"It's not a grocery list. It's a name. Half a name."

Maya nudged the folder an inch closer. Her mother's eyes went to it and away again, fast, in less than a second, and that was the whole answer right there, sitting in the room with both of them, unacknowledged.

"You knew something in that kitchen." Maya heard her own voice go up and made herself bring it back down. "You looked at that page for four seconds and told me you didn't know. Tita Nora looked at it Sunday and told me it was old handwriting and then walked across an entire room to get away from me. Everybody in that parish hall who's over seventy looked at four words and made the exact same face. Except me. I'm the only person in this family who doesn't already know whatever this is, and I'm the one holding the book."

"There's nothing to know."

Liza's voice had gone flat. Not raised. Flat, in the particular way it went right before it cracked, a warning Maya had learned to read by the time she was twelve and had, in fifteen years, never once learned to heed.

She turned the burner off. The spoon went down against the counter harder than the moment required, a small crack of metal on tile that startled both of them.

"Your grandmother has been in the ground six weeks," Liza said, "and you are going through her things looking for a scandal. Is that what you want out of this? A scandal? Something to tell people?"

"That's not fair."

"I didn't ask if it was fair, I asked if it's what you want."

"I want to understand her."

"You understood her fine for twenty-seven years."

That one landed. Maya felt it land, physically, the way you feel a step that isn't there.

Liza picked up the folder. Not to open it, not to read a word of it. Only to move it, sliding it several inches away from herself along the counter, the way you'd move something you'd rather not have near the food.

"Some things are not yours to open," she said, "just because she isn't here anymore to tell you no."

"Ma, please..."

"Let her keep what she kept, Maya."

Which was, Maya thought afterward, driving home through fog thick enough to slow her to twenty on a road she'd driven ten thousand times, the trouble with the whole argument. There was nothing in it she could get a grip on. Her mother was not wrong. A person does get to keep things, from her family, from her children, even after she's gone, maybe especially then. It was a decent principle and Liza had stated it well.

She'd also, across two entire conversations now, never said the sentence *there is nothing there.*

She'd said *there's nothing to know,* which was a different sentence entirely, and Maya, who spent every working day teaching seven-year-olds the difference between what a sentence said and what it meant, had heard the gap in it as clearly as if her mother had drawn a circle around it in red pen.

At the door Liza said her name.

Maya stopped with her hand on the latch.

For about two seconds it seemed as though her mother was going to say the thing, whatever the thing was, and the whole shape of the following two months would have been different.

"Drive carefully," Liza said. "It's thick out there."

She left without pushing further. The pancit stayed on the counter, unmentioned, unrefrigerated, still in its container.

The folder stayed where her mother had shoved it. Three careful inches closer to the trash can than to either one of them, and not, Maya noticed on her way out, actually in it.

# Chapter 11

### Two Deadlines

[S]{.dropcap}he had ordered two copies at the shop on Mission without examining why she had wanted a spare.

She got home a little after nine, put the second printout on her own kitchen table, and sat looking at it for a while with her coat still on, a thing she'd started doing lately and had not yet noticed she'd started doing.

Then she got up, found an index card in the drawer where index cards had lived unused since roughly her second year of teaching, and copied the phrase out by hand.

It took longer than it should have. She was copying letters in a language she couldn't read, which meant she couldn't rely on the shape of a word to tell her whether she'd gotten it right, only on matching curve to curve like a child tracing. Twice she got most of the way through and saw she'd dropped a letter and started over. The third card came out clean.

She looked at what she'd made. Her own handwriting, careful and slightly cramped, holding a sentence she didn't understand, written by a woman who'd been dead six weeks.

Her phone rang while she was still holding it.

"Hey." Marcus, at the other end, with the evening sound of a man on a couch. "You sound like you're standing up."

"I am standing up."

"Are you eating?"

"I had pancit at my mother's." She hadn't. She'd left it on the counter. She didn't correct it. "How was the thing with your advisor?"

He told her about the thing with his advisor. It took about six minutes and involved a scheduling conflict, a piece of equipment that had been broken since February, and a colleague she'd met twice and had opinions about. She made the correct noises in the correct places and did not hear most of it.

"Maya."

"Mm."

"You've said 'mm' four times."

"Sorry." She sat down, finally, coat still on. "Long night. I went to my mom's."

"How is she?"

There was a version of the next ten minutes that was available to her right there. She could feel it sitting in the room, reachable: *I found something in my grandmother's cookbook and my mother lied to my face about it twice and I've been thinking about nothing else for nine days.* Marcus would have listened. Marcus would have asked good questions. That was, demonstrably, what he was for.

"She's fine," Maya said. "You know. Sorting through everything. It's a lot."

"Yeah." A pause. She could hear him deciding not to push, which he did well and which she had always found restful and which, that night, for the first time, felt slightly like something she was getting away with. "Well. I'm around, if it stops being fine."

After they hung up she took the index card and put it in the inside pocket of the planner she carried to school every day.

The planner had a laminated month-at-a-glance in the front, and on the thirtieth, in her own quick hand, seven weeks out, she had written: *M: answer by 30th.*

She hadn't meant to put the two things in the same pocket. There was no other pocket. It simply happened, the way things happen when a person isn't paying much attention to the shape of her own life, and it wasn't until she'd zipped the planner shut, the card and the calendar resting against each other in the dark, that something in her chest gave a small, unpleasant tug and let go again before she could name it.

She stood in her kitchen a minute longer, still in her coat, in an apartment fifteen minutes from one that had smelled her whole life of garlic and Vicks and burned-down votive wax.

Then she hung the coat up and went to bed, and did not think about it again for eleven days.

# Chapter 12

### The Last Room

[T]{.dropcap}hey saved the bedroom for last, both of them, without either one ever saying so out loud, as people always save the hardest room for last and then pretend afterward the order was arbitrary.

It took two full Saturdays.

The closet ate most of the first one. Forty years of dresses and cardigans, most of them in the same four colors, arranged by a system neither of them could work out and both of them dismantled anyway. Three winter coats for a city that has never once required one. A garment bag containing what turned out to be Liza's own wedding dress, which produced a silence of about fifteen seconds and then a decision, made entirely by eyebrow, to deal with that another day.

And, near the back, on a hanger by itself, one truly indefensible sequined jacket.

Liza held it up. The sequins caught the overhead light and threw it around the room like a small disaster.

"Nineteen eighty-seven," she said.

"That is not a real jacket."

"It is a real jacket. She wore it. In public. To your Tita Fe's cousin's wedding, and she wore it *proudly*, Maya, she got compliments, she talked about the compliments for a year." Liza's voice had gone up into a register Maya hadn't heard from her in months. "I have photographs. There are photographs of this jacket in this house."

They both went at the same time. It wasn't even funny, particularly, and they laughed until Maya had to sit down on the edge of the bare mattress and her mother had to hold on to the closet door.

"You have to keep it," Maya said, when she could.

"I absolutely do not have to keep it. There is no law. Nobody is legally required to keep a sequined jacket."

She folded it into the KEEP box anyway, carefully, sequins in, and neither of them said a single word about it, because some decisions made in a dead woman's bedroom do not require defending to anyone.

The dresser was harder.

The top drawer held what top drawers of that generation always hold, which is a graveyard of small objects with no obvious value and enormous unexplained significance. A watch that had stopped at 4:20 on some unknown day of some unknown year. Three pairs of reading glasses in three different prescriptions, none of them the pair Perla had actually been using at the end. A rosary missing two beads. A ticket stub, soft with age, from something at the Cow Palace in 1979. A Mass card from a funeral in 1994 for a woman named Remedios Punzalan, a name neither Maya nor her mother had ever heard in their lives.

"Do you know her?"

Liza turned the card over. Nothing on the back. "No."

"She kept it for thirty-two years."

"She kept eleven years of Easter programs, apo. She kept coupons for a store that closed."

"She kept those in a *shoebox*. This was in the top drawer." Maya set it on the dresser rather than in any box. "Top drawer's different. Everything in here was something she touched."

Liza didn't argue with that.

They worked through the rest of it in a rhythm that had gone quiet. A jewelry box with nothing valuable in it and one pair of gold earrings Liza put directly into her own pocket without comment. A hospital ID badge from 1988, Perla's face on it at forty, unsmiling, hair pulled back hard. An envelope of photographs of people nobody could identify, which was, Maya thought, the single most common object in the world and the one nobody ever knows what to do with.

"Do you ever get used to that part?" she asked, at some point, holding up two strangers on a beach in what looked like the sixties. "The not being able to ask anymore."

Her mother took the photograph, looked at it, handed it back.

"No," Liza said. "But you get better at not needing to. Which is not the same thing, and it takes longer than anybody tells you it will."

In the last hour they found the shoes.

Fourteen pairs, at the bottom of the closet, most of them unworn in a decade, several of them a size Perla had not been for twenty years. Two pairs still in boxes. One pair of white nursing shoes, worn down on the outside edge of each heel, which told you exactly how she had stood for forty years and which neither of them could throw away and which went, in the end, into the KEEP box with the sequined jacket.

They finished just before dark on the second Saturday.

Maya took the crucifix down last, because it was the last thing on any wall, and underneath it the paint was a shade lighter in a small clean rectangle where forty years of sun hadn't reached.

They stood together in a room with a bare mattress and a nail hole in it, and Maya thought that this was the strangest part of the entire summer, stranger than the wake, stranger than the burial: how fast a room stops belonging to somebody once you take the objects out. Two months ago this had been unmistakably her grandmother's bedroom. You would have known it from the doorway, blindfolded, by the smell alone.

Now it was a room. In a building. That somebody else would rent by October, and paint, probably, and never wonder about the small pale rectangle under the fresh coat.

# Chapter 13

### Handing Back the Keys

[T]{.dropcap}hey got there an hour before the landlord to do the last of it, which turned out to mean standing in an empty apartment with a broom and very little to sweep.

Maya had not been prepared for how it sounded. Nobody warns you about that either. With the furniture gone and the carpets rolled up, her own footsteps came back off the walls, and when her mother said something from the kitchen it arrived with a faint hollow ring on the end of it, and the apartment that had absorbed forty years of noise had apparently been doing that entirely by means of a sofa and some curtains.

The pale rectangle on top of the refrigerator was still there. So was the one on the bedroom wall. So was a small constellation of tape marks beside the kitchen door frame where a calendar had hung, replaced annually, since some year before Maya was born.

"She measured me on that frame," Maya said.

Liza came and looked. The pencil marks were still faintly there, low down, four of them, none higher than a seven-year-old.

"She stopped after you got tall enough to argue about whether you were standing straight." Liza ran her thumb across the lowest one without appearing to notice she was doing it. "You accused her of cheating. You were six."

"She *was* cheating. She pressed down on my head."

"She pressed down on your head because you were up on your toes, apo."

Neither of them suggested doing anything about the marks. There was nothing to be done about them. They'd be under primer inside a month.

Maya swept the kitchen twice, which did not need it.

Her mother stood in the middle of the living room for a while with her arms folded, not doing anything, so unlike her that Maya stopped sweeping to watch.

"Ma."

"I'm looking at the room."

"Okay."

"I lived here," Liza said. "That's all. Nobody ever thinks about that part. This was my house from nine years old. I did homework on that floor." She unfolded her arms. "And then it was hers for another thirty years after I left, and it stopped being mine so gradually that I never noticed the day it happened."

She picked up the broom Maya had put down and finished the corner Maya had missed.

Mr. Ibarra arrived at ten, a man in his sixties with a clipboard, and did the least thorough walkthrough Maya had ever witnessed. He looked into the bathroom without entering it. He tried one light switch. Then he stood in the middle of the empty kitchen holding the clipboard down at his side, not looking at it, not looking at anything in particular.

"She used to bring me pancit," he said.

Neither of them had asked.

"Every New Year. Thirty years running." He shifted his weight. "She'd knock on my office door on the thirty-first with a foil tray and she'd say the same thing every single time, in exactly the same tone, like a joke we'd agreed on years ago. *This is not a bribe. But do remember it in February.*"

Liza laughed, startled and wet-eyed at once. "That sounds exactly like her."

"I always did remember it in February." He said this seriously, as though the record needed correcting. "Twice I forgot other people. Never her."

He glanced around the kitchen one more time.

"She was the easiest tenant I ever had, and I've been doing this thirty years. Never once late. Complained about exactly one thing the entire thirty years, some business with the water pressure back in 2009, and she apologized twice for mentioning it. Twice." He shook his head slightly. "I had a man on the second floor call me at eleven at night about a light bulb."

He signed the form without inspecting anything further, produced the deposit refund envelope from an inside pocket and handed it to Liza directly rather than mailing it, and then stood in the doorway a moment longer than the transaction required.

"Tell your family she was a good neighbor," he said. "People don't say that enough about anybody. She was a good neighbor for thirty years."

It got Maya more thoroughly than the wake had, more thoroughly than the burial. Not *she was a good woman* or *she'll be missed*, both of which she'd heard two hundred times since June and neither of which meant anything anymore. A good neighbor. Specific, small, and provable, from a man who'd had thirty years of evidence.

They locked the door. Liza dropped the keys into his palm.

And that was the end of it. A month of work concluded in a hallway in under four minutes, no ceremony of any kind. Walking back down to the car through fog that hadn't lifted all morning, Maya was fairly sure her grandmother would have preferred it that way.

# Chapter 14

### What Marcus Actually Asked

[H]{.dropcap}e came over that same night, unprompted.

She'd texted him three words about the keys, *it's done, keys back,* and forty minutes later he was at her door with Thai food and no particular agenda, which was, she thought later, a small demonstration of the entire case for him: he had read a whole week accurately off three words and had then done nothing about it except show up with dinner.

They ate on the couch. Behind them, against the wall, stood the boxes she'd brought back from the apartment, six of them, a small architecture of a dead woman's remaining belongings that Maya had not yet decided anything about and had been walking past for four days.

Marcus did not ask about the boxes. He talked instead about a colleague who had, that week, attempted to microwave a hard-boiled egg in the shared break room, with predictable results and one genuinely surprising one involving the ceiling. He was good at this. It was a specific and underrated skill, being able to fill a room with something light on a night when a person could not manage anything heavier, and he had never once, in five years, made it feel like a strategy.

"Can I ask you something," he said eventually, "and can you not answer it tonight if tonight's the wrong night?"

"That's a very ominous way to open."

"It's not ominous."

"It's a little ominous."

"It's badly timed, is what it is, and I know it's badly timed, which is why I'm doing it this way instead of just letting it arrive." He set his container down on the coffee table and turned to face her properly, which he did rarely, and which meant something when he did. "The fellowship is real. I have to give them an answer, and the answer has a date attached to it. I'm not asking you tonight. I'm telling you the date exists."

"Marcus."

"Let me finish, because I've been rehearsing this and if you interrupt me I'll lose the middle of it."

She let him finish.

"I'm telling you now so you're not surprised by it later," he said. "So you don't find out in four weeks that I've been sitting on a deadline the whole time and doing that thing where I hope you'll work out what I want without me having to say it. I've done that before, to somebody else, years ago, and it went badly and I was the reason it went badly. So. The date exists. You know about it now. That's the whole thing I came to say."

The room was quiet for a second. On the wall behind them, six boxes.

"Thirtieth," Maya said. "You already told me. I've had it in my planner for a month."

"You have?"

"In the same pocket as something else, actually."

And there it was.

It sat right there behind her teeth, the whole of it, entirely sayable: *I found something in her cookbook. There's a name in the margin in a language I can't read and my mother lied to my face about it twice and I have thought about almost nothing else since, and the card I copied it onto is sitting eight inches from your deadline in a zippered pocket, and I noticed, and I didn't like what I noticed.*

He was right there. He had just, at some visible cost to himself, modeled exactly how a person says a difficult thing out loud in advance.

"It's fine," Maya said. "I know the date. I'll have an answer."

Marcus looked at her a beat longer than the sentence needed.

Then he nodded, and picked his container back up, and said something about the egg again, and let it go.

He shouldn't have had to. Some part of her knew that before he'd even finished nodding. She let him do it anyway, and lay awake for a while that night working out why it bothered her, and got as far as understanding that it was the first dishonest thing she had done in five years of a relationship that had been built, almost entirely, on not doing that.

She got no further than that, then.

# Interlude: Miagao, 1968 {.unnumbered}

[T]{.dropcap}he recruiter came to Iloilo Mission Hospital in the spring, a brisk American woman from a hospital in San Francisco with a folder of paperwork and a way of talking that made the whole enormous undertaking sound like filling out a simple form. Two years, she explained, standing in front of a room of graduating nurses in their white caps. Two years of clinical experience at a real American hospital, exceptional pay by local standards, room and board arranged, and afterward, an open road home with a credential that would open every door in the province for the rest of a career.

Perla raised her hand and asked the only question that mattered to her. "And after the two years?"

"After two years, you come home," the recruiter said. "That's the whole arrangement. We need you here for those two years, and then you belong right back to your own country, no different than before, except considerably more qualified."

It sounded, put that way, like the safest kind of adventure there was, a door that opened and then closed itself again right on schedule, and Perla's mother wept with pride for a full week after the acceptance letter came, telling every relative and neighbor and the parish priest himself that her daughter was going to America, as though the going itself were the entire achievement, the two years afterward barely worth mentioning.

Ramon said less about it than anyone. He helped her practice her English phrases in the evenings on the sea wall, corrected her pronunciation with real patience, and only once, the week before she left, let something else show through.

"Two years is a long time," he said, not looking at her, watching the water instead.

"It's not so long. You'll write. I'll write. We'll set the date for right after I'm back, maybe even before, so I have something certain to come home to."

"I'd marry you tomorrow if your mother would let us skip all this."

"My mother would never let us skip all this." She laughed, and after a moment he laughed too, and they went ahead, that same week, and had the invitations printed anyway, on the theory, practical and entirely hers, that ordering them now meant one less thing to arrange once she came home, everything ready and waiting except the single date, which they'd fill in together the moment she was back on solid ground. Aguilar. Villanueva. A blank line where a number would eventually go.

The paperwork took until after the new year, which nobody had warned her about and which she spent working her first eight months on a ward in Iloilo City, so that by the time the letter with the flight on it came she had already stopped believing in it slightly.

She packed the invitations at the very bottom of her one suitcase, underneath her uniforms, where they wouldn't crease, and did not think, standing at the airport in Manila the following spring with her mother crying into a handkerchief and Ramon standing a little apart from the rest of the family, hands in his pockets, trying visibly not to be the reason anyone else started crying harder, that two years was actually a very long time at all. It only became a long time, she would understand much later, once she was standing on the other side of it, with no one there to tell her exactly when it had quietly turned into forever.

Her mother had given her a scapular at the gate, pressed into her hand without ceremony, and Perla wore it for eleven years and then stopped, and kept it in a drawer for another forty-six, and it was in the top drawer of the dresser in August when her own granddaughter emptied it, and went into the donate box, because nobody left alive knew what it was.

# Interlude: Perla's Mother {.unnumbered}

[S]{.dropcap}he was not a warm woman and would have been surprised to learn anyone thought so.

Consolacion Villanueva had raised five children on a schoolteacher's salary and her own considerable force of will, and she had wanted, for as long as she could remember wanting anything specific for her eldest daughter, this: a credential, a door out of the particular narrowness of small-town expectation that had closed around her own life before she'd fully finished deciding what she wanted from it.

She had married young herself, at nineteen, a match her own parents had arranged with more attention to the family's land than to any question of what she personally hoped for, and while the marriage had turned out well enough, steady and companionable in the manner most marriages of her generation eventually settled into, she had never once stopped privately wondering what she might have become given a different set of doors to choose from. She saw, in Perla, precisely the version of herself she'd never gotten the chance to be: quick, capable, unafraid of hard work, with an actual credential in hand and an actual world outside Miagao willing to pay her for it.

So when the recruiter came through Iloilo with her folder of American paperwork, Consolacion had pushed harder than anyone else in the family for Perla to go, harder even than Perla herself, brushing aside every hesitation her daughter voiced about Ramon and the wedding and the sheer distance of it with the same brisk, practical confidence she brought to running a household on very little money. Two years, she told her daughter, was nothing. Two years was the blink of an eye against an entire life, and a woman who came home from it with an American nursing credential could write her own future rather than accepting whatever future got handed to her by circumstance, the way Consolacion's own had been handed to her.

She never learned the full truth of what happened afterward. Perla's letters home grew steadily shorter over the years, the gradual thinning that happens to correspondence between two people separated by an ocean and, increasingly, by an unspoken thing neither wants to be the one to name in writing, and by the time Consolacion passed, in 1988, arthritis and a bad heart taking her quietly in her sleep at seventy-one, she believed, as far as anyone in the family could tell, exactly the story everyone else eventually came to believe: that her daughter had gone to America, fallen in love with a good man there, and simply built a new life, the way ambitious daughters sometimes did, with no tragedy attached to any part of it at all.

Perla flew home for the funeral, her third trip back in nineteen years, and stood at her mother's grave for a long time after everyone else had gone ahead to the reception, thinking not about Ramon at all, for once, but about a woman who had wanted so badly for her daughter to have the world that she'd never considered what it might cost that daughter to take it.

She did not say any of it. She stood there with her hands folded in front of her the way she stood on a ward, and at some point she straightened a wreath that had gone crooked in the wind and did not straighten the one beside it, and then she went in to the reception and ate, and was told twice that she looked well, and agreed both times that she did.

It joined, that same afternoon, a list that was by then quite long and that nobody else in the world had ever seen any part of. The long private list of things Perla Villanueva Bautista had decided, over the years, were simply hers to carry alone.

# Interlude: Miagao, 1988 {.unnumbered}

[S]{.dropcap}he had three days in Miagao and she spent most of the first one in a kitchen that was not hers, being told where things were kept by women who had known where things were kept for longer than she had been gone.

Her mother was to be buried on the third morning. The house was full in the way a house is full before a funeral, which is to say full of people eating and of food arriving faster than anyone could put it away, and Perla had come in from the airport and washed her hands and started working before anybody could decide whether she was a guest.

"You do it different now," her cousin Merced said, watching her cut.

"I do it the same."

"You do it faster. That's America." Merced said it without any weight at all, moving a bowl. "Or that's twenty years of a hospital. One of the two."

On the second morning she went out for calamansi because they had run out and because she wanted, more than she wanted to admit to anybody in that house, twenty minutes in which nobody was going to look at her face.

The market was where it had always been. Two of the roads had been paved since her last visit and one had not, and the church stood at the end of the plaza doing what it had done since before anyone's grandmother, the stone worked over edge to edge, patient about the whole thing.

The elementary school was four streets up from the market.

She knew that the way you know where a thing is in the town you were a child in, which is to say not as information but as a direction your feet have. It was a Tuesday and it was half past nine and there would be children in it.

She bought the calamansi. She bought more than she needed, because the woman selling them had been at the wake and there is no version of that transaction that ends with the correct amount.

Then she stood at the edge of the plaza with a bag in each hand and did the arithmetic she had come six thousand miles and nineteen years to avoid doing, which took about four seconds and produced nothing she could use. Four streets. A working morning. A man who would be somewhere in a room with a blackboard, and who would look up.

She did not go.

She would not have been able to say, afterward, that she decided anything. She stood there long enough for the heat to start on the back of her neck, and then she turned and went back down toward her mother's house by the road that does not pass the school, which was the longer way, and which she had not taken once in the whole of her childhood.

At the wake that evening one of her aunts said his name in the middle of something else entirely, the way a small town says a name, as scenery rather than news. *Ramon's eldest is at the high school now, wants to do engineering, and his wife has opinions about it.*

"That's good," Perla said.

"It's very good. She has *opinions*." The aunt was already three sentences into somebody else's daughter.

Nobody in that room thought there was anything to tell her. Twenty years is long enough that a thing stops being a thing and becomes only the arrangement of the town, and the arrangement of the town was that Ramon Aguilar taught at the school and had a wife with opinions and a boy who wanted to be an engineer, and Perla Bautista had gone to America and married there and come home for her mother.

She went back into the kitchen and finished the pancit, which was not enough for the number of people who came, and she apologized for it twice, and it was gone by nine.

On the flight back she had a window seat and a bag on her knees with three of her mother's spoons in it, wrapped in a shirt, and she looked down at the water for a while and then stopped looking.

She never told anyone she had stood at the edge of that plaza.

There was nothing to tell. She had gone out for calamansi and had come back with calamansi, and everything else that happened that morning happened inside four seconds and left no evidence anywhere, which is the condition most of a life is in.

# Chapter 15

### R.

[R]{.dropcap}oz Tan taught fourth grade two doors down and ate lunch standing at the copier, one hand on the machine and the other holding a sandwich, on a theory she stated at least twice a week and clearly believed: sitting down to eat during a school day was a luxury available only to people who did not also have to run forty copies of a spelling test before recess.

Maya found her there on Thursday and did not bother with any preamble.

"Your mom's from Iloilo."

"My mom's from Iloilo," Roz agreed, around the sandwich. "Why do you look like that."

Maya held out her phone.

Roz set the sandwich down on top of the copier, which Maya had watched her do a hundred times and had never once seen her clean up after, and took the phone with both hands.

"Okay. So I can read some of this. Not all. My Hiligaynon is basically whatever I absorbed eavesdropping on my mother's phone calls, which means I'm excellent at gossip and useless at anything formal." She turned the phone sideways. Turned it back. "This part's the recipe. Egg, ground pork, that's a measurement. This part..."

She stopped.

"This part's not the recipe. This is somebody talking."

"Talking how? Talking to who?"

"Hang on."

Roz was already dialing, phone wedged between shoulder and ear, using both hands to pull a warm stack of spelling tests off the tray and square them against the machine.

"Ma. Ma, quick question, don't ask why yet."

A burst of Hiligaynon came back, fast enough that Maya caught nothing but its rhythm, which rose and fell like something being sung even though, as Maya would learn much later, Mrs. Tan was at that moment complaining about the volume of the downstairs television.

Roz read the phrase back slowly, letter by letter, spelling the parts she wasn't sure of, checking the screen twice.

There was a pause on the other end.

Then a small, sharp sound that came through the phone and across three feet of hallway and was, even without a word of translation, unmistakably surprise.

Roz's eyebrows went up. She listened. She said something short. She listened longer.

Then she covered the speaker with her palm.

"Okay. She says it's not a full sentence. She says it's the kind of note somebody leaves for herself, not the kind you write meaning anyone else to read it." Roz was watching Maya's face now rather than the phone. "It's something like, *not this one anymore.* Or *no more of this one.* And then a name. Or the start of one."

She uncovered the speaker. There was another exchange, brief, and whatever Roz said made her own mother laugh audibly on the other end of the line. Then she hung up.

"She wants to know if this is gossip."

"What did you tell her?"

"That I genuinely didn't know yet. Which, for the record, was the truth."

Maya took the phone back and looked at it again, at four words she now half understood, which was worse than not understanding them at all.

"An R," she said. "That's all it is. One letter, underlined."

"Could be Ramon. Rodel. Romy. Ramil." Roz picked her sandwich back up off the copier. "My mom says there were about six R names in the entire town when she was growing up. It was basically half the boys her age."

"That's not narrowing it much."

"No." Roz chewed. Swallowed. Her voice changed a little. "Hey. You okay? This is your grandma who passed, right?"

"Yeah."

"You want the honest read, teacher to teacher?"

The bell was two minutes out. Fourth grade waits for nobody's family history. Roz said it anyway, gathering her stack, already half turned toward the door.

"Nobody underlines a name that hard, in the margin of a recipe, for no reason. That's not a slip of the pen. That's somebody pressing down." She paused in the doorway. "So somebody, at some point in your grandmother's life, was somebody. Which isn't nothing. But it also isn't automatically something you need to be afraid of finding out."

Maya taught two-digit subtraction for the next two hours with the word sitting in her back pocket like a stone she kept forgetting was there and then finding again, sharper each time.

*Somebody.*

Not a spice. Not a measurement. Not an argument about vinegar. A person, cut off mid-sentence sixty years ago and never mentioned since, not at a single Sunday dinner or wake or wedding in the whole span of Maya's life, and she had eaten at that woman's table probably two thousand times.

Dario got three problems wrong in a row and she caught it late, and she crouched by his desk and walked him back through the second one and he told her, unprompted, that she seemed tired again and that his lola said tired people should drink more water. She thanked him for the medical advice. He accepted the thanks as his due.

Walking out to her car after the last bell, through a parking lot half swallowed in late-afternoon fog, she found herself doing arithmetic she hadn't meant to start.

Perla, born 1948. So: early twenties in the late sixties. Nursing school, then America, then a wedding to her grandfather in 1972.

Young enough for an R to matter enormously.

Young enough that whatever this was, it hadn't been an acquaintance or a cousin or some minor figure at the edge of a long life. It had been near the center of one, at the exact moment that life was deciding what shape it intended to take.

# Chapter 16

### Rico's Two Cents

[R]{.dropcap}ico called on Wednesday about a serving dish.

"The white one. With the blue around the rim."

"I don't know what that is."

"Maya. The white one. It's been at every family event since before either of us was born. Your mother thinks I took it home after the misa and I'm telling you I did not take a serving dish onto a *plane*."

"Did you drive?"

There was a pause.

"I drove," Rico admitted. "But I still didn't take it."

She could hear his house behind him: a television, and under that, two of his kids conducting what sounded like a formal legal dispute over a video game controller, one of them making an argument involving the word *technically*.

They talked about the dish for six minutes. Then they talked about his back, and his oldest starting middle school, and a thing at his work that he described as ninety percent politics and ten percent actual engineering.

And then, somewhere around minute twenty, without particularly deciding to, Maya told him the whole of it. The margin note. Roz's mother on the phone. The initial pressed hard enough to nearly go through. Her own mother, twice now, answering too fast.

Rico listened without interrupting, which she had not expected.

Rico had always occupied a certain position in the family: the cousin who'd moved furthest away, and who had therefore somehow acquired standing to say the blunter things nobody living within twenty minutes of Liza was willing to say out loud.

"You know nobody's going to thank you for this," he said, when she finished.

"That's not fair, I'm not..."

"I'm not telling you to stop. I'm telling you what's going to happen, because I don't think anybody else is going to." His voice had no edge in it. It was the tone of a man giving an accurate weather report. "Whatever you find. Auntie Liza's going to be upset you went looking. The rest of them are going to be upset you found something. Tita Fe will tell four people within a week and get two of the details wrong. And you'll be the one holding all of it, because you're the one who wouldn't leave it alone."

Maya sat down on the edge of her couch.

"So you think I should stop."

"I didn't say that. I said nobody's going to thank you. Those are two different sentences." Somebody in the background lost the controller argument, loudly. Rico did not react at all, which suggested this was ambient. "For what it's worth, I think you should keep going."

"Why?"

"Because from what you're describing, Lola never got to decide anything out loud." He said it slowly, working it out as he went. "Not once. Not about this. Somebody in this family ought to eventually get to look at the whole picture, even if it can't be her. Might as well be the one who can't sleep until she knows things. That's been you since you were nine."

"That's not true."

"You made me help you find out what my own birthday present was in two thousand six. You were *seven*. You had a plan."

"That was one time."

"It was not one time." He laughed, and then didn't, and there was a stretch of a few seconds where neither of them said anything and the television murmured on his end.

"Since when did you get wise about all this," Maya said.

"Since I turned forty and started thinking about what I'm leaving my own kids to untangle." He said it lightly. It did not land lightly. "Everybody in this family's got something folded up somewhere, Maya. I've got mine. Believe me."

She waited.

He didn't fill it.

"You want to tell me?"

"Not tonight." A beat. "Maybe not for a while. Ask me again in a few years, when I've worked up to it."

"Is that a real maybe or a family maybe?"

"It's a real maybe." He cleared his throat, and the whole register of the call changed back, deliberately. "Anyway. Find my serving dish. That's the actual crisis here. Your mother's building a case against me."

She never did learn, that year, what Rico's own folded-up thing was.

He never brought it up again and she never asked a second time, and the not-asking felt, at the time, like courtesy, and only much later like something she'd inherited.

But the call stayed with her, mostly for one reason: it was the first time it occurred to her that her grandmother might not be the exception in this family.

She might just be the one whose version had surfaced.

# Chapter 17

### The Yearbook

[S]{.dropcap}he went back to her mother's garage on Saturday without asking, on the sound principle that it is considerably easier to apologize for opening a box than to obtain advance permission to open one.

Liza had a standing hair appointment every third Saturday, ten to noon, which she had kept since roughly the Clinton administration and which Maya had been quietly waiting for since Tuesday without admitting to herself that she was waiting for anything.

The spare key still lived under the fake plastic rock beside the side door, a rock which had never once fooled anybody, least of all the family members it was ostensibly hidden from, and which had survived three separate conversations about how it was not actually a security measure.

The garage was cold and smelled of concrete and old motor oil and, faintly, of the cardboard itself. Twenty-some boxes stood stacked three deep along the far wall, most still wearing the tape Maya had wound around them herself two weekends earlier.

Four of them were open.

She noticed that immediately and stood looking at it for a moment. Not opened and repacked. Open, flaps up, one with its contents visibly disarranged, in a garage nobody but her mother had entered in two weeks.

So Liza had been out here. Alone. More than once, from the look of it, and had not mentioned it to anybody.

Maya filed that and kept moving.

The box she wanted was marked PAPERS, PERLA in her mother's hasty capitals. She sat cross-legged on the concrete and went through it properly, which took the better part of forty minutes.

Expired passports, four of them, rubber-banded together, the rubber gone brittle and snapping when she touched it. A decade of Christmas cards nobody had ever thrown out, several still in envelopes. A folder of tax documents from the 1990s. Two funeral programs. A certificate from the hospital marking twenty-five years of service, still in its cardboard sleeve, never framed.

Her hands went gray with garage dust. She wiped them on her jeans twice and gave up after that.

Near the bottom, under everything, she found a thin cardboard folder, soft and slightly warped at the corners.

Inside it, a photograph.

Fifty young women in three stiff rows on the steps of a building she didn't recognize, all of them squinting into a sun that must have been genuinely punishing that morning. Identical white caps. Identical uniforms. Fifty pairs of hands folded in the same position, which suggested someone had told them where to put their hands.

On the back, in pencil gone soft with age: *Iloilo Mission Hospital School of Nursing, Batch of 1968.* Beneath that, two neat columns of names.

Maya turned it over and found her.

Third row, second from the left. Nineteen years old, and unmistakably, immediately her, because whatever else six decades had changed, they had not changed the set of that jaw. Maya had spent an entire childhood watching that exact expression chop vegetables at a frankly dangerous speed, negotiate a swap-meet vendor down forty percent, and, on one memorable afternoon in 2009, dismantle a parking attendant in three languages inside ninety seconds.

Nineteen. Squinting into the sun. No idea whatsoever what was coming.

Maya looked at her a long time.

Then she turned the folder over to slide the photograph back in, and something else came loose and dropped onto her knee.

A second photograph. Smaller. Not mounted, not taped, not preserved in any way at all. Bent along one edge, softly, the way paper bends when it has spent time folded in a pocket.

A young man stood in front of a church, one hand raised to shade his eyes.

He was laughing.

Not posed and laughing. Caught. Mid-motion, mid-laugh, at whoever was holding the camera, with the unguarded expression of a person who trusts the photographer enough to laugh *at* them rather than perform for them.

The church behind him was extraordinary, even out of focus. Stone, and carved over every visible surface, the facade crowded with shapes she couldn't make out at that resolution.

She turned it over.

Nothing.

No name. No date. No pencil, no ink, no note in either language. A blank white rectangle on the back of the only photograph in this entire box that had not been labeled, sleeved, dated, or filed.

Maya sat on the concrete floor of her mother's garage with both photographs balanced on her knees and did not move for a long time. Long enough that the cold came up through the floor. Long enough that her left leg went numb and she noticed it distantly, the way you notice weather.

This was a woman who had kept eleven years of Easter programs.

Who had labeled the back of a class photograph from 1968 with the full name of the institution.

Who had recorded, in permanent ink, that Baby's adobo was not actually Baby's.

And this one photograph, the only one in fifty-some years of documentation with nothing at all written on the back of it, sat loose in a folder behind everything else, unlabeled, as though at some specific point she had decided that this was the one item in her entire carefully annotated life she was not going to leave any instructions about.

Maya photographed both pictures with her phone, twice each, adjusting for the bad light from the small window above the workbench.

Then she put the folder back.

She was careful about it, and she noticed herself being careful: sliding it in at the same depth, at the same slight tilt against the cardboard, so that if her mother came out here again, at night, alone, as she apparently had at least four times in two weeks, she would find nothing disturbed.

Maya locked the side door behind her and put the fake rock back exactly where it lived.

She was already in the car with the engine running and the heater working against the afternoon chill before it caught up with her: that she had just spent forty careful minutes making sure her mother would never know what she had found in her own garage.

Folded quietly out of sight. Undisturbed. Waiting.

She sat in the driveway a while before she pulled out.

# Chapter 18

### An Ordinary Tuesday

[R]{.dropcap}oom 14 smelled the way it smelled most mornings, which was glue sticks and orange slices. The orange slices arrived every single day in a plastic container packed by Amelia's mother, regardless of whether it was Amelia's turn for snack, a devotion nobody had asked for and everyone had long since accepted as weather.

By nine-forty Maya had already refereed a dispute over whose turn it was to feed the classroom fish, a matter complicated by the fact that the fish, whose name was Kevin, had been fed twice on Monday by two children operating in good faith and had spent all of Tuesday morning looking, in the collective view of the class, unwell.

"He looks fat," said Marisol.

"He looks the same as always."

"He looks *fat*, Ms. B."

"Kevin is fine. Kevin is a fish. Kevin has no opinions about his appearance." This was not a fully honest answer, since Maya had privately consulted the internet about Kevin at eleven o'clock the previous night and had learned more about overfeeding than she wanted to know.

She confiscated a rubber-band slingshot at nine-fifty with the full grave ceremony the moment demanded, which meant walking it to her desk drawer slowly while the entire class watched, because in second grade the ceremony is the deterrent and the confiscation is merely paperwork.

At ten she told a child, for approximately the four-hundredth time in five years of teaching, that a headache which arrived precisely at the start of handwriting practice and would, she felt confident, resolve precisely at the start of recess, did not require the nurse.

"Ms. B, is it true old people can't get married anymore?"

Dario. No runway whatsoever, as usual, a habit Maya had stopped trying to correct out of him in the first week of school because it was, honestly, the best thing about him.

"Where'd you hear that?"

"My lola says nobody wants her anymore because she's eighty."

He delivered it flat, entirely without distress, in the way children report facts from the adult world before anybody has taught them which ones are supposed to have weight attached.

"Is that true?"

"No." Maya crouched by his desk and straightened his worksheet, which had somehow rotated forty-five degrees in the ten minutes since she'd handed it out, a persistent mystery of second grade she had never solved. "That's not true at all. People can fall in love at any age. Your lola is probably being a little dramatic."

Dario considered this.

"She IS dramatic," he said, with enormous enthusiasm, satisfied, and went back to his worksheet, crisis resolved, requiring nothing further from anyone.

Maya laughed out loud into a pocket of the room's ordinary noise, a real one, the first that had come easily in about two weeks.

She would think about that exchange for a surprisingly long time afterward. Not the question. The confidence. A seven-year-old had encountered a piece of information that troubled him, had brought it directly to the nearest adult, had received one clear factual correction, and had accepted it and moved on with his entire day. No circling. No waiting for a better moment. No deciding privately that it was probably nothing.

She would have liked to borrow that. She was, she was beginning to suspect, constitutionally incapable of it.

At lunch she found Roz in the staff room, and the two of them ate standing at the window over the yard rather than at the sticky round table where the fifth-grade teachers had entered day three of a conflict about whose turn it was to run the book fair.

"Any progress on your mystery man?"

"Found a photo. No name on it, nothing on the back." Maya aimed for casual and landed nearer to tired. "Nursing school classmate, maybe. Maybe more than that. I don't know. It's like collecting puzzle pieces out of a box with no picture on the lid."

"That's most family history, honestly." Roz worked on her sandwich. "My grandmother's got a whole branch of the family nobody talks about. Some falling-out in the seventies. All I've ever been able to establish is that it involved a pig, a land dispute, and one memorable slap at a christening."

"A slap at a christening."

"At a *christening*, Maya. In front of the priest, from what I can piece together, though nobody will confirm the priest." Roz shook her head. "Fifteen years I've been assembling this out of overheard fragments at funerals. Every time I ask directly, somebody offers me food."

"That's the whole strategy, isn't it. Just feeding the question until it goes away."

"It works, is the thing. It has a really good success rate." Roz bumped her shoulder against Maya's. "Point is, you're not out here alone doing detective work on people who'd rather you didn't."

Roz went to referee the book fair. Maya stayed at the window a few minutes longer.

Down in the yard, Dario's grandmother had already arrived for pickup, a full hour early, as she did most days, walking the length of the fence line and waving enthusiastically at every car that pulled through whether or not it had any connection to her.

This was the actual shape of her life, Maya thought. Not the thing in the cookbook. This. Kevin the fish and the orange slices and a confiscated slingshot and a woman waving at strangers' cars. A job that asked her to show up steady for other people's children five days a week and asked nothing else of her at all.

It was a good life. She had always known that and had never once resented it.

# Chapter 19

### Parent Conferences

[T]{.dropcap}wo hundred and twelve minutes of scheduled conferencing across two nights, plus, in practice, another forty of overrun that nobody accounts for and everybody absorbs.

Maya had a system. Folder open, work samples on the left, one genuine specific compliment ready before the parent sat down, because a conference that opens with a real observation about a real child goes differently than one that opens with a number. By the second night she had said the phrase *reading at grade level* enough times that the words had come apart into sounds.

Twenty-minute slots, four until eight, two nights running, on a schedule that survived first contact with roughly sixty percent of the families.

Marisol's parents came at four-forty and stayed the full twenty minutes without asking a single question about reading, because what they wanted to discuss was Kevin.

"She talks about this fish constantly."

"She's very invested in Kevin."

"She has *theories* about the fish," Marisol's father said. "She has told me the fish is being overfed. She has told me this many times."

"Marisol is correct that the fish is being overfed," Maya said. "We're addressing it as a class."

Dario's mother came at six, worried, as she came to every conference, convinced that her son's constant questions signaled some deficit rather than what Maya considered his single best quality.

"He doesn't stop. He doesn't stop, Ms. Bautista. In the car, at dinner, at church especially. The priest knows him by name now and it is not for a good reason."

Maya spent ten of their twenty minutes on this, and did not rush it, because it was plainly the only item on the agenda.

"Can I tell you the three questions he's asked me this month?"

"I'm afraid to hear them."

"Whether old people can still fall in love. Whether ghosts have to eat vegetables. And whether it gets cold enough anywhere on earth to freeze a person's eyeballs, which he asked with real scientific seriousness and which I have since had to look up."

Dario's mother put her face in her hands, laughing.

"That's not a child failing to pay attention," Maya said. "That's a child paying enormous attention to a world most adults stopped questioning about thirty years ago. He notices something, he brings it to somebody, he asks. That's the entire skill. I have adults in my life who cannot do that."

"I just don't want him to be seen as difficult."

"He isn't difficult. He's specific."

She heard herself say it, and heard the half-beat behind it, and did not follow the thought into the room where it wanted to go: that there had been an entire specific woman in her own life whose particular quiet had been read, by a whole family across fifty years, as something simpler and considerably less interesting than what it was.

"Those aren't the same thing," Maya finished. "And I think the difference matters more than people give it credit for."

Between slots she ate a granola bar standing at the window and watched the parking lot fill and empty and fill.

Roz came past at six-forty with two coffees and put one on Maya's desk without stopping or speaking, which is a form of collegial love available only in schools.

The last conference of the second night ran long.

He was new to the district, a father, recently divorced, and he arrived with a folder of his own. Inside it: printed reading logs, a spreadsheet, two articles about second-grade benchmarks.

His daughter was doing fine. Maya told him so in the first four minutes, plainly, and then watched him not be able to accept it.

He circled instead. What about spelling. What about the fact that she'd been quieter since school started. Should he be doing flash cards, was twenty minutes of reading enough, was it too late to have caught something.

Maya let him go for a while. It was eight-fifteen. She was the only teacher left in the hallway with a light on.

"Can I say something that isn't about reading levels?"

He stopped.

"She's okay," Maya said. "She's genuinely okay. She laughs in my classroom every day. She has two good friends. She has been quieter since school started, and that's real, and I've been watching it, and my honest read is that it's a kid whose house changed and who is working out what that means. Which is exactly the thing you'd expect and exactly the thing you can't flash-card your way out of."

He looked at the spreadsheet in his hands for a long moment.

"I'm not doing a great job of hiding it," he said.

"You brought a spreadsheet to a second-grade conference."

"Yeah." He laughed, badly, and put the folder down on the little desk. "Yeah, I did."

She stayed twenty minutes past her end time. He talked, mostly, about a marriage he had not wanted to end and the way he had come to believe that if he simply managed hard enough at everything else, his daughter would come through it unmarked.

Maya drove home a little past nine, wrung out in the particular way of a day spent entirely inside other people's fears, and thought, pulling into her own driveway, that nobody in her credential program had said anything about this part.

Not the lesson plans. This. Two nights a year in a room with adults who came in holding printed spreadsheets about spelling and were, without exception and without ever once saying so directly, asking whether they were failing the person they loved most in the world.

She sat in the car a minute before she went in. Above the dashboard the fog had come down as far as the streetlight.

Everybody was trying so hard. You could not say it to any of them, because saying it out loud would sound like consolation and it wasn't consolation, it was just the fact of the matter. Everybody was trying extraordinarily hard, in almost total privacy, and telling nobody.

# Chapter 20

### The Shoebox

[T]{.dropcap}he second trip to the garage came a week later, and this time she brought a space heater from her own apartment, which turned out to be an act of pure optimism in a structure with no insulation and a gap under the door you could see daylight through.

The box was labeled DOCS, in her mother's hand, hastily. Liza's handwriting told you things. The careful capitals on PAPERS, PERLA meant a job done properly. DOCS meant a woman who had wanted to be finished.

Maya sat down on the concrete and went through it slowly.

Bank statements, decades of them, several still sealed in their original envelopes, which raised a question she noted and did not pursue: why keep, unopened, a statement you did not want to read. There were eleven of them, all from the same eighteen-month stretch in the early nineties.

A hospital pay stub from 1977. Maya did the arithmetic and had to do it twice, and then sat with the result, that her grandmother had raised a child in San Francisco on an amount of money that no longer sounded like a salary.

A folded church program from a wedding in 1981, which Maya set aside.

A marriage certificate. Perla Villanueva and Ernesto Bautista, 1972, the ink still dark, barely faded at all despite fifty-four years inside a manila envelope. Two witness signatures, neither of them a name Maya recognized. She photographed it and moved on and only much later thought to wonder why nobody in the family had witnessed it.

A green card, laminated, the lamination gone yellow and lifting at one corner. The photograph on it showed a woman so young that Maya's first reaction was that this was somebody else. Then she found the jaw, and it was unmistakable. Twenty-five, maybe. Unsmiling for the camera, but not blank: the expression Perla wore in every photograph ever taken of her across seven decades, which was the expression of a woman about half a second away from deciding something.

There was also, near the middle, a small stack of photographs held together with a paperclip that had rusted into the top one.

Maya worked it free carefully and lost a corner anyway.

They were all of the same three women, over what looked like a decade: her grandmother and two others, in a kitchen, at a table, once on a beach in coats. Nora she recognized immediately. The third woman she had never seen in her life, and there was nothing written on any of them, and there was nobody left to ask.

She photographed all seven and put them back with the ruined paperclip on top.

Underneath all of it, flat against the bottom of the box, a single sheet of onionskin.

She knew before she picked it up that it mattered, and could not have said how. Something about the way it had been stored: not in an envelope, not in a folder, not with anything else. Underneath everything, alone, at the very bottom.

It had been folded and refolded so many times over so many decades that the creases had gone soft and gray and furred at the edges, and when she lifted it the whole page sagged in her hands and threatened to come apart into four unequal pieces.

She unfolded it slowly, on her knees, using both hands.

Government letterhead. A department she didn't recognize by name. The dense, deliberately flattened prose of an official form built to communicate as little as legally possible:

*exchange visitor status. non-immigrant classification. two-year foreign residence requirement.*

Dated 1971.

Addressed to Perla Villanueva, care of a hospital in San Francisco whose name Maya recognized immediately, because she had driven past that building probably a hundred times in her life, on the way to other places, without once knowing that her own grandmother had walked its corridors in a uniform two decades before Maya was born.

There was a stamped date in one corner and a signature under it, looping and confident and completely illegible.

Maya read the whole page twice before any of it resolved into meaning, and even then what she had was not really meaning. It was shape.

Her grandmother had come to this country on a clock.

The clock had a date on it, an actual official date, printed on a document a government had mailed to her.

And whatever had happened next in Perla Villanueva's life had happened right at that date, either when this piece of paper said it should or exactly when it said she was meant to go home instead. One or the other. Maya could not yet tell which, and she sat on a cold concrete floor for a long time trying to work out whether it made a difference, and could not.

The space heater ran the entire time and accomplished nothing whatsoever.

She photographed the document three times, adjusting the angle against the glare from the window, then folded it back along its own soft creases exactly as she'd found it, and slid it under everything else at the bottom of the box.

Second time in two weeks, sitting on that floor, holding a piece of her grandmother's actual life that nobody in the entire family had ever once mentioned, in her presence, existed.

She thought about calling her mother.

She did the calculation right there on the garage floor: what she would have to admit to ask. That she had been in here. That she had a key and had used it during a hair appointment. That she had photographed things and put them back at the correct angle so nobody would know.

She put her phone back in her pocket, and turned off the space heater, and went home.

# Chapter 21

### What Liza Admits

[S]{.dropcap}he brought the folder to her mother's kitchen table that Sunday and laid it flat between them without a word of preamble, on the theory, which turned out to be correct, that her mother would rather fill an uncomfortable silence than sit inside one for very long.

Liza was cooking when Maya arrived, something simple, adobo simmering low on the back burner though nobody had asked for adobo and no company was expected, the kind of unnecessary cooking Maya recognized as her mother's version of pacing a room. She glanced at the folder Maya set down, went still for exactly a beat too long, then reached over and turned the burner down another notch before she said anything at all.

"Where was this."

"Bottom of the box marked DOCS. Underneath everything else in it."

Liza looked at the notice for a long moment, considerably longer than she'd looked at the recipe margin note back in August, long enough that Maya understood, watching her mother's face carefully, that this document was not new information to her. It was, instead, a document Liza had clearly hoped never to have to look at again in her life.

She wiped her hands on the dish towel at her hip, twice, though they weren't wet, and finally sat down across from her daughter, the adobo forgotten behind her.

"There was somebody," Liza said, slowly, the words coming out like something she was translating from a much older, more guarded version of herself. "Before your grandfather. Back home. I don't know much about it, apo, she never talked about it directly, and I learned a long time ago that asking twice never got me anywhere with your grandmother, only got the subject changed faster than the first time." She reached out and pushed the paper an inch across the table, away from herself rather than toward Maya, a gesture Maya was beginning to recognize as her mother's way of physically distancing herself from something she couldn't yet fully face. "It didn't work out. That's the entire sum of what I actually know, and honestly, I'd like that to remain the entire sum of what I know. Some things your grandmother earned the right to keep quiet about for the rest of her life, and I'm not sure I want to be the one who digs them up not three months after we put her in the ground."

"How long have you known?"

"I told you. I don't know anything."

"You knew what that page said the second you looked at it. You knew what this letter was before you finished reading the first line." Maya kept her voice level, which took effort. "You didn't ask me what an exchange visitor was. Most people would have asked."

Liza's jaw did something.

"Ma. There's a wedding invitation somewhere, isn't there."

She didn't actually know that yet. It was a guess, the kind a person makes out of a deadline and the word *somebody*; she hadn't found it, and wouldn't for another seven weeks. But she said it with the full confidence of fact, and watched her mother's face confirm it before a single additional word left Liza's mouth, a flash of something caught and recognized crossing her expression too fast to hide.

"I don't know what you think you're going to find by digging through everything she owned," Liza said, standing up abruptly, carrying her coffee cup to the sink though it remained half full. She dumped it out, ran the tap, turned it off again without washing the cup. "But whatever it is, it isn't going to change a single thing that matters. She still married your grandfather. She still stayed here, with us, and built an entire life out of nothing for fifty straight years." Her voice cracked exactly where Maya had learned to expect it would. "Doesn't that count for something more than one name written in the margin of a recipe?"

"I'm not trying to take anything away from what she built, Ma."

"Then leave it alone." Liza turned the faucet on again, off, on once more, a woman with nowhere obvious to put her own hands. "Please, apo. Just leave it alone."

Maya left the folder on the table when she finally went, and her mother didn't throw it away that night.

# Interlude: Liza, That Same Night {.unnumbered}

[S]{.dropcap}he didn't go straight to bed after Maya left. She sat at the kitchen table with the folder still where her daughter had left it, and stared at it for a long time before she got up, went to her own bedroom closet, and pulled down a shoebox from the top shelf that she had not opened herself in something close to twenty years.

She'd found the invitation once before. Fifteen years old, snooping through her mother's dresser drawer for a stray dollar to borrow without asking, the casual theft of a teenager who assumed a mother's drawers held nothing more interesting than spare buttons and old receipts. She'd found instead a folded piece of gold-bordered paper with a name on it that wasn't her father's, and had stood frozen in her parents' bedroom for a full minute, fifteen years old, trying to make the two facts sit together in any arrangement that made sense.

She'd never told her mother she'd seen it. She'd put it back exactly where she'd found it, gone back to the kitchen, and said nothing for two full years, until the night she was seventeen and something, she could no longer even remember what, some argument about curfew or a boy or nothing at all, had cracked the question loose almost against her own will. *Who's Ramon.* She remembered her mother's face going very still, remembered the short, flat answer that followed, the one she'd repeated almost verbatim to Maya just an hour ago. *Someone before your father. It didn't work out. That's all there is to know.*

She had believed that, mostly, for years. Not entirely. A daughter always kept some small private percentage of doubt tucked away from even her own mother's most convincing denials. But mostly. Because believing it made her own childhood make sense, made her parents' marriage into the simple, solid thing she needed it to be to trust her own eventual marriage, her own choices, her own long practice of staying steady through hard years rather than looking for an exit. She had been given a little more of it only once, years later, over a bowl of peas, and she had not mentioned that to Maya an hour ago either.

Now there was a granddaughter digging through boxes with a photograph and a translated phrase, and Liza sat on her bedroom floor with her own much older shoebox in her lap, the one she'd kept without ever fully deciding to keep it, containing nothing more dramatic than a handful of photographs from her own childhood and, tucked at the very bottom, a single loose Polaroid of her mother at what must have been about twenty-five, laughing at something outside the frame, younger and lighter in the face than Liza had ever known her to be in person.

She had never shown this photograph to anyone. She wasn't entirely sure, sitting there at fifty-one with the shoebox open on her knees, why she'd kept it so carefully hidden for so long, except that some part of her had always understood, without ever articulating it even to herself, that this photograph belonged to a version of her mother that existed before Liza did, a woman with her own private losses and choices that had nothing whatsoever to do with being anybody's mother yet.

She thought about calling Maya right then, telling her about the Polaroid, closing some of the distance that had opened up between them over the last month. She didn't. Not yet. She sat with it a while longer instead, the way, she realized, her own mother must have sat with her folded invitation countless times over fifty-five years, and understood, for the first time with any real clarity, what that kind of solitary keeping cost a person, and what it protected them from having to explain out loud.

# Chapter 22

### Tita Fe's Kitchen

[T]{.dropcap}ita Fe lived twenty minutes south in Pacifica, in a house that smelled permanently of whatever she had cooked three days previously, and Maya got in the door on a Wednesday evening using a borrowed serving spoon as her stated reason, with the photograph already open on her phone before she'd finished parking the car.

Fe was not related to Perla by blood in any traceable way. She was family by the long elastic definition that covers any woman of roughly the right age who knew your mother back home and was therefore called Tita by two subsequent generations without anyone ever formally deciding it.

She had come over herself in 1983, more than a decade behind Perla, sponsored eventually by a cousin twice removed, and she had absorbed across forty years of parish potlucks and long-distance phone calls an enormous quantity of secondhand family history, which she dispensed with total and unshakable confidence regardless of how many decades old the story was or how many mouths it had passed through on the way to hers.

The kitchen had a dozen framed photographs in it, which Maya counted while eating, and every one of them was of somebody else's children.

Tita Fe had none. Maya had known this her entire life in the way you know a fact about a person without ever once turning it over, and she turned it over that evening for the first time, at twenty-seven, at that woman's table, being fed.

She fed Maya first. There was no version of this visit in which she did not feed Maya first.

"You're thin."

"Everyone keeps saying that."

"Everyone is correct." Fe put a plate down. "Eat that and then show me whatever's on the phone. You've had that face since you walked in. Your Lola had the same face. Something behind the eyes, planning."

Maya ate about half of it, then showed her the photograph of the margin note.

Fe looked at it for perhaps four seconds.

"Rodel," she said, and handed the phone back.

"Sorry?"

"Rodel Fernandez. That's who that is." She said it the way you'd confirm a bus route. "Everybody knew about Rodel and your Lola. My own mother told me the whole story years ago. There was an understanding between them before she left for America, and then nothing came of it because his family didn't approve of a nurse marrying into their line. Thought it was beneath them, imagine that, a *nurse*." She made a small dismissive noise and picked up her own fork. "So she went, and that was the end of it."

Maya sat very still for a second.

"You're certain about the name."

"Rodel Fernandez."

"You're certain, though. Because that's a whole family name. That's something I could actually go looking for."

"Apo." Fe put the fork down and gave her a look of enormous patience. "I have known this story since before you were born."

"From your mother."

"From my mother."

"Who heard it from."

"From people in the town, Maya, that's how anybody knows anything." A flicker of something crossed Fe's face and was gone. "There is a whole branch of the Fernandez family out in Vallejo now. You should ask them. Tell them you're Perla's granddaughter, they'll know the name."

She said it with the absolute authority of a woman who has never allowed an absence of firsthand knowledge to slow down a confident assertion.
