Maya drove home over the hill with a low ache starting behind her right eye.

She got most of the way to the freeway before she started actually doing the arithmetic, and by the time she was on it she had a sinking suspicion that she recognized the shape of what had just happened. Fe had not given her a second thread. Fe had given her a different rumor entirely, one that had been sanded smooth by forty years of retelling in church halls until it resembled her grandmother's story only in outline, the way two unrelated rivers look identical from high enough up.

An understanding. A departure. A family that disapproved.

Half the young women who left that province in that decade had a version of that story attached to them, and half of the people telling those versions had never been in the room for any of it.

She sat in her own driveway for a while with the engine off.

She had a name now, and it was worse than not having one, because it was a name she was fairly sure was wrong and she could feel how much she wanted it to be right. Rodel Fernandez, of the disapproving family, out in Vallejo. It would be so easy. She could start tomorrow.

She almost didn't go see Nora the following Wednesday at all, out of an embarrassment she couldn't quite name: the fear of having spent a Wednesday evening chasing a piece of gossip that belonged to somebody else's grandmother entirely.

# Chapter 23

### Tita Nora, Alone

[S]{.dropcap}he caught Nora at the parish thrift shop on a Wednesday afternoon, volunteering as she volunteered every single Wednesday of the calendar year as far back as Maya could remember, sorting a rolling rack of donated winter coats with the same brisk, unsentimental economy Liza brought to her own closets.

"You again." Nora didn't look up right away, didn't sound surprised either, the tone of a woman who'd clearly been expecting this particular conversation to eventually find her on some Wednesday when her mother wasn't around to run interference on her behalf. "Your mother know you're here?"

"No."

Nora considered that for a moment, hands still on a coat's collar, then set it down entirely, walked over to the shop's front door, and locked it, flipping the little cardboard sign hanging in the window to CLOSED with the unhurried authority of a woman who had made a decision and had no intention of being rushed through the execution of it.

"Tita Fe says his name was Rodel Fernandez," Maya said, to her back. "From a family who thought a nurse was beneath them."

The older woman's answer took under two seconds.

"No."

"You're sure?"

"There were Fernandezes in Miagao. There are Fernandezes everywhere, apo, it's like saying Smith." Nora shook her head, unbothered. "Nothing to do with your grandmother. Whatever your Tita Fe's mother was passing around, it belonged to some other girl in some other part of that town. Small towns don't run out of scandal. They recycle it. Generation to generation, until nobody remembers whose it was originally, and everybody's absolutely certain they've got it right."

She came back and stood behind the second folding chair with both hands on it.

"I could be kinder about saying so," she said. "I am not going to be, because being kind about a wrong name would cost you another month."

"His name was Ramon," she said, before Maya had even managed to fully sit down in the folding chair Nora gestured toward. "Ramon Aguilar. From Miagao, your grandmother's town. I was from Janiuay, but that whole province was one small town in those days, everyone thoroughly in everyone else's business, no privacy to speak of for anybody under the age of thirty." She lowered herself into the second folding chair with a small grunt, hands folded in her lap. "They were engaged before she came here. There was a date set, or nearly set. There was an invitation, I believe, though I never actually saw it with my own eyes, only heard about it secondhand from a cousin of mine who was closer to the family than I was. Then she left for America, and the two years everyone expected her back within simply passed, and by the time anyone back home fully understood that she wasn't coming, it had already been decided, in effect, without anybody sitting down and deciding it out loud. Do you understand what I mean by that?"

"I think so." Maya looked at the coats. "It was only supposed to be two years."

"Two years is two years," Nora said. She said it flatly, in English, the way a person repeats something she once heard somewhere, and did not explain it.

"What happened to him? After."

"I don't know." Nora shook her head slowly, and for the first time in the whole conversation something crossed her face that looked sorry rather than simply cautious. "We all lost touch with people from home in those years, letters slowing to a trickle and then stopping altogether, no calls, no way to easily follow up the way you could now with a phone in your pocket. I couldn't tell you if he ever married, if he's even still alive, whether he ever forgave her or simply stopped thinking about it at all somewhere along the way. I only know the shape of what happened at the time. Not what came after it."

She reached over and patted Maya's knee twice, the same gesture from the parish hall in August, though this time it landed as something closer to genuine comfort than dismissal.

"Your grandmother was not a bad woman, apo. I want you to understand that clearly, whatever else you find. She was a very young woman, very far from everything she knew, with a deadline in front of her that she apparently had no idea how to face directly. That is not the same thing as being cruel."

Nora looked at the rack of coats for a moment rather than at Maya.

"She didn't go home when she was supposed to. That is the whole of it and I have never said it out loud to anybody before now." Her hands stayed folded. "She meant to ask for the time off. She told me so, in the kitchen, more than once, in the way a person tells you a thing so that saying it counts as most of the work. And then she didn't ask, and then it was three months late, and then it was six, and every single week there was an actual reason, apo, that is the part nobody understands about it afterward. The floor was short. A woman on her ward died. Somebody had the flu. Not one of those weeks was a lie. They simply added up into one."

"And you never said anything."

"I never said anything."

"Can I ask you something else."

"You will regardless."

"Did she talk about him? That first year. When you were both here and it hadn't happened yet."

Nora was quiet for a while.

"Constantly," she said. "For about eight months. She would read me his letters, or parts of them, out loud, in the kitchen at the dormitory, whether or not I wanted to hear them, which I generally did not." A short breath that was almost a laugh. "He was very funny in his letters. I remember being surprised by that. I had met him twice in my life and I would not have said he was a funny man."

"And then?"

"And then she stopped." Nora smoothed her skirt across her knees. "I noticed it. I didn't say anything. That is my share of this, apo, since you're collecting everybody's. I noticed my friend stop mentioning a man she had mentioned every day for eight months, and I decided it was not my business, and I said nothing, and I have had about fifty-five years to think about whether that was respect or cowardice."

"What did you decide?"

"That it was both," Nora said. "That it usually is."

"Why does everyone in this family want to protect her from me finding this out?"

"Because we are not protecting her from you." Nora stood, unlocked the shop's door, flipped the sign back to OPEN, though no customers had come by the entire time it had been closed. "We are protecting her from becoming, in your mind, the villain of her own story. She isn't one. She's simply a woman who let a deadline answer a question she couldn't bring herself to answer for herself, and that is not a scandal, apo. That is only what it looks like to be twenty-three years old and genuinely terrified, further from home than you have ever been in your entire life."

# Interlude: Nora {.unnumbered}

[W]{.dropcap}hat Maya never asked, and what Nora never once volunteered in all their conversations that year, was why a woman who understood Perla's particular kind of far-from-home fear so completely had never once, in fifty-odd years in this country, married herself.

There had been someone, early on, a fellow nurse's brother who'd courted her properly for the better part of a year, flowers and Sunday dinners and an eventual, expected proposal that Nora had turned down for reasons she'd never fully explained to anyone, including, some nights, herself. She'd told Perla at the time only that it hadn't felt like the right shape for her life, a phrase vague enough to end most follow-up questions, and Perla, newly married to Ernesto herself and deep in the busy fog of a first pregnancy, hadn't pushed.

The truth, which Nora had carried privately for decades the same unshowy way she carried most things, was considerably less romantic and considerably harder to say out loud: she had watched Perla's unraveling that first hard year, the letters slowing, the deadline quietly passing, the private, wordless grief her friend carried around like a stone in her uniform pocket, and had decided, watching it, that she did not trust herself to make a choice that large without a version of the same quiet cowardice creeping into it somewhere. Better, she'd reasoned at twenty-six, to simply not choose at all, and spend a life answerable to no one but herself, than to risk becoming a woman with her own folded invitation hidden at the bottom of a box.

It had cost her things, over the years, a life without children, a house always a little too quiet, holidays spent as the honorary aunt at other women's tables rather than the mother at her own. She did not regret it, mostly, on the ordinary Wednesdays that made up most of her life, sorting donated coats at the parish thrift shop with the same brisk competence she'd once brought to a hospital floor. But she thought of Perla, more often than she ever admitted, as the one person in her whole life who had actually understood, without needing it explained, what it cost a woman to be that far from home and that afraid, all at once, with no good option in front of her that didn't require giving something up.

She went to the funeral, of course. She sat three rows from the back and left before the reception, which several people noticed and nobody remarked on, because that is what Nora does and has always done.

What nobody in that church knew, including the woman's own daughter and granddaughter, was that Nora Bacalso was the last living person who had heard Perla Villanueva say Ramon Aguilar's name out loud in a dormitory kitchen in 1969, reading a letter, laughing.

She had never told Maya any of this, and didn't plan to. Some kinds of keeping, she'd come to understand over nearly eight decades of a full and generally satisfied life, were not wounds at all. They were simply the shape a particular life had taken, chosen or not fully chosen, and a woman was allowed to hold that shape quietly, without needing anyone else's understanding attached to it.

She did tell one person, once, in 1994, at the parish, the week Remedios Punzalan died, to a woman she had known for almost thirty years and would know for thirty more.

The woman said, "Oh, Nora," and put a hand on her arm, and then somebody came over to talk about parking, and the moment closed and neither of them ever reopened it. Nora thought about that hand on her arm, on and off, for the rest of her life, and considered it, on balance, sufficient.

# Chapter 24

### Two More Weeks

[H]{.dropcap}e called at nine on the exact night the answer was due.

Maya let it ring twice before she picked it up.

She had done that once before, standing in her grandmother's living room two months earlier with a piece of pandesal in her hand, and she did not notice she was doing it again.

"Hey." His voice had a careful quality, the over-control of a man who has decided in advance to stay calm about something. "I know it's tonight."

"I know."

"I'm not calling to pressure you. I want to be clear about that, because I've been sitting here for an hour working out how not to sound like I'm pressuring you." A short laugh with nothing much in it. "I just wanted to hear your voice instead of getting a text."

Maya stood at her own kitchen window with the lights off.

"Can I have two more weeks?"

The silence went on a beat longer than she had braced for.

"Maya. That's not really how deadlines work."

"I know."

"It's a program with an administrator and a start date. There's a form."

"I know it's not fair to ask."

She could hear him breathing. Below her the street was doing its evening thing, the fog coming up off the water and along the block, arriving rather than falling.

"There's a lot going on with my grandmother's things right now," she said. "I need a little more time to think it all the way through. I don't want to hand you an answer I haven't finished, just so that you have one."

That was true. Every word of it was true, and she noticed, saying it, how well it worked, and how easily it had come out, and that she had not had to construct any of it.

*She didn't go home when she was supposed to.*

Nora had said that to her in a locked thrift shop that same afternoon, sitting on a folding chair between two racks of donated coats.

*She meant to ask for the time off, and then she didn't, and then it was three months late, and then it was six.*

Maya put her free hand flat on the counter.

"Two weeks," Marcus said slowly.

She could hear him deciding. It took him about four seconds, and she could hear all four of them.

"Not three," he said. "I have to give them something concrete. I'm not being difficult, I genuinely have to."

"Two weeks. I promise."

"Marcus."

"Still here."

"I know I've been doing something. I'm not going to explain it tonight because I'd do it badly and it would come out as an excuse." She pressed her hand flatter on the counter. "But I know I've been doing it."

There was a pause on the line.

"Okay," he said. And then, carefully: "That's the most useful sentence you've said in about a month."

She hung up and stayed at the window a long time after, watching the fog come up the hill until it took the streetlight two houses down and the street stopped having a far end.

And then, for the first time since she had opened a cookbook at her grandmother's kitchen table, she let the thought run the whole way instead of stopping it three-quarters through, which she had been doing for two months without admitting it.

Somewhere in a nurses' dormitory in San Francisco, in 1971, a young woman had asked for a little more time.

And then a little more.

And every single one of those requests had been reasonable. That was the part Maya had not let herself get to before. Not one of them had been a lie. The floor was short-staffed. She genuinely did mean to file the paperwork. Each individual week had a real and sufficient reason attached to it, and the reasons kept being true, right up until the morning there were no more weeks available to ask for, and a decision had been made without anybody making it.

Maya stood in her dark kitchen with her hand still flat on the counter.

She did not know yet which of the two possibilities frightened her more: that she might turn into that woman, or that she had been her for some time already and had simply been calling it something else.

# Chapter 25

### Learning the Words

[S]{.dropcap}he started with an app, which lasted four days.

Most of them did not offer Hiligaynon at all. The one that claimed to had perhaps two hundred words in it, an unsettling proportion of which concerned hotel check-in, and a cheerful animated bird that congratulated her for learning how to ask about a swimming pool.

Roz solved it in one phone call, without being asked.

"My mother says come Thursday."

"Roz, I wasn't going to ask you to..."

"You were going to. You were building up to asking me and it was going to take you two more weeks and I decided to save us both the time." A pause. "There's one condition."

The condition, delivered directly by Mrs. Tan when Maya called to confirm, was that no instruction would take place without a meal, because in that house language and food were not separable activities and it was not up for negotiation.

There was a photograph of Roz's father in the hallway, dead nine years, in a barong, at somebody's wedding.

Maya passed it eight times over six weeks and never asked about it, which she recognized afterward as precisely the instinct she had spent an entire autumn indicting in other people.

Mrs. Tan was seventy-eight and taught the way Maya imagined she had taught her own children forty years earlier: entirely by immersion, with total confidence, and with no patience whatsoever for grammatical explanation.

The first lesson lasted two and a half hours and covered approximately eleven words, six of which were about the food on the table.

"Say it."

Maya said it.

"No. Again. Your mouth is doing an American thing at the end."

"What American thing?"

"You are putting a little hook on it." Mrs. Tan demonstrated the hook, unflatteringly. "Flat. Say it flat."

By the second lesson Maya had a notebook, which Mrs. Tan regarded with visible suspicion, as though writing things down were a form of cheating.

"Why does it change there?" Maya asked, pointing at a phrase she had copied out twice.

"Because it does. Say it again."

"But there's a rule. There has to be a rule, this is a language, languages have rules."

"There is a rule. I don't know it." Mrs. Tan pushed a plate slightly closer across the table, which Maya was coming to understand was this woman's response to any question she considered tiresome. "I have spoken this language for seventy-eight years and I have never once needed to know it. Eat. Say it again."

By the fourth lesson Mrs. Tan had begun correcting her on words she had not been taught, on the grounds that she ought to have picked them up by now.

"You've heard me say that four times."

"I've heard you say it, I don't know it."

"You've *heard* it," said Mrs. Tan, as though this settled the matter, which in that house it did.

She learned perhaps forty words over six weeks.

Nowhere near enough to read a letter. Nowhere near enough to hold a conversation, or to walk into a room in Miagao and be understood, or even to fully decode four words in a margin without help.

But something happened anyway, around week four, that she had not anticipated and could not have arranged deliberately.

The note changed in her head.

For two months and more it had been a code. An object. A thing to be cracked, photographed, run through translation software, carried to people who might decipher it.

And then one afternoon at Mrs. Tan's table, working through a phrase with an ordinary structure and rhythm, she heard the margin note in her own mouth for the first time. Not as a puzzle. As a sentence, in a real language, with a shape to it, of the kind a person might actually say.

Which meant her grandmother had, at some point, sat down at a kitchen table with a pen and *said something.* Out loud or nearly out loud. Alone.

That was all. It was not information. It changed no fact of the case.

It reorganized the entire thing.

"You know your Lola could have taught you this," Mrs. Tan said, near the end of the six weeks, not unkindly, packing food into a container Maya had not asked for and would not be permitted to decline.

"She thought English would get us further."

"Mm." Mrs. Tan pressed the lid down. "That's what we all said. It was even true, mostly. Your Roz has no Hiligaynon either, not really, she thinks she does." She handed the container over. "But I notice none of us ever said the other part out loud."

"What's the other part?"

"That a language you don't teach your grandchildren," Mrs. Tan said, "is also a language they cannot overhear you in."

She said it without weight, the way you'd note that it looked like rain, and went to find Maya a bag to carry the container in.

# Chapter 26

### An Ordinary Terrible Week

[T]{.dropcap}he following week was, by any measure, one of the worst of Maya's professional life, and none of it had anything to do with her grandmother.

Amelia Reyes had been quiet since roughly the second week of school. Maya had noticed the quiet, had logged it, and had filed it as shy, a reasonable read that turned out to be the wrong one.

On Tuesday afternoon, during the last ten minutes of the day when the room was loud with chairs going up on desks, Amelia stayed at her seat and said something to Maya that Maya would not repeat to anybody for the rest of her life except in a written statement and in one conversation in a principal's office.

She got it right. That was the one mercy of the whole week. Five years of annual training and a laminated card in her desk drawer, and when it happened she did not freeze, did not ask a single leading question, did not promise anything she could not deliver. She said *thank you for telling me,* and she said *that was a brave thing to say out loud,* and she said *I'm going to help,* and she wrote down the child's exact words while they were still exact.

Then she made a phone call.

What followed was three days of the administrative purgatory of having done precisely the correct thing and being able to tell absolutely nobody about it.

Not Roz, two doors down, who could tell within four minutes on Wednesday that something was badly wrong and who did not ask, and who left a coffee on Maya's desk during third period without a note on it.

Not her mother.

Not Marcus, who called Wednesday night and got a version of Maya so flat and so far away that he asked twice if she was angry with him. She said no. She was not able to say anything else. She lay awake afterward feeling the cruelty of a man apologizing for a wall he had not built and could not see.

The training tells you that you are not an investigator. It says this several times, in bold, on the laminated card, and it is the single most useful sentence in the entire document, because the instinct in the moment is to ask one more question and the instinct is wrong.

You are a mandated reporter. Your entire function is to hear it, write it down accurately, and hand it to people whose job it actually is.

Maya had recited that to herself perhaps forty times in three days.

She sat in the principal's office twice. She wrote the statement, and then wrote it again because the first version had one sentence in it that was interpretation rather than observation, and she knew better.

On Thursday two adults she had never met and would never see again came to the building, and Amelia was walked out of Room 14 in the middle of the morning with her backpack and her jacket, and the class asked where she was going, and Maya said that Amelia had an appointment, and then taught a lesson about the water cycle to twenty-five seven-year-olds.

Evaporation. Condensation. Precipitation. Collection. There is a diagram, and the children color it in, and there are arrows going around in a circle.

That was the job. That had always been the job. Nobody in her credential program had ever managed to convey that the job would include this, and she was not sure they could have.

She cried in her car in the staff lot at four-ten on Thursday for eleven minutes, timed, because she had committed weeks ago to dinner at her mother's and she needed to be able to walk in with a face on.

She checked the mirror. She fixed what could be fixed. She drove over.

There was a smell of onions from the hallway before the door even opened, which meant her mother had started cooking well before Maya arrived and had therefore known, from a four-word text sent at lunchtime, roughly what kind of evening this was going to be.

Liza opened the door, looked at her for about a second and a half, and said, "You look terrible."

"Work thing. I can't talk about it."

Her mother did not ask a single follow-up question.

Maya had braced for one the entire drive over. She had three deflections ready and had rehearsed the least dishonest of them at two separate red lights.

Liza simply stepped back from the doorway and said, "Then come eat."

There was a plate in front of her within four minutes. Rice, and the chicken thing her mother made when she wasn't planning anything, and a glass of water Maya hadn't asked for.

They ate almost the entire meal in silence. Not an uncomfortable one. The radio was on, low, the standards station. At one point Liza said something about a neighbor's tree and Maya made a noise, and that was the whole of the conversation.

When Maya stood to help with the dishes her mother said, "Sit down," and did them herself.

Driving home afterward, Maya thought about the thing she had been quietly building a case against for two months.

The competence instead of the conversation. The plate instead of the question. Her grandmother's whole method, and her mother's, the family instinct she had privately diagnosed somewhere around week three as avoidance dressed up as care.

It was not only that. She had missed that part entirely.

Sometimes it was precisely, exactly the correct instrument. Her mother had looked at her for a second and a half in a doorway and had understood, correctly, that her daughter did not need to be asked a single question, that she needed to be fed and left alone and not made to perform an explanation she was legally prohibited from giving.

Nobody had taught Liza that. Somebody had, presumably, shown it to her, over and over, at a kitchen table, for about fifty years.

And Maya, who had spent two months turning this family habit over like evidence, had been using it herself the entire time and had not once noticed.

# Chapter 27

### What He Noticed

[I]{.dropcap}t was a Sunday morning, four days into the second two-week extension, which she had asked for on the phone the Wednesday before in a voice she had not liked, and they were doing the crossword, a thing they did badly and competitively and which had been a fixture of their weekends for about four years.

Marcus put the pen down halfway through.

"Can I say a thing I've been rehearsing?"

"That's the second time in two months you've opened with that."

"It's the second time in two months I've needed to." He turned the paper over so neither of them could look at it, which was, she understood, deliberate. "You've told me about your grandmother's cookbook probably nine times in the last month."

"That's an exaggeration."

"It is not an exaggeration, I have thought about it specifically. Nine, maybe ten." He wasn't raising his voice. That was the part that got through. "You've never told me what the note says."

Maya set down her coffee.

"It's four words," she said. "It's not a story."

"Maya."

"It's four words in a language I can't read, in the margin of a recipe. It's not that I'm keeping it from you. There's just not much to keep."

"Okay." He nodded slowly, and did not argue with it, and then said the thing anyway. "Then tell me the four words."

The kitchen was very quiet. Somewhere below them a car alarm went off and stopped again.

She did not say anything.

"That's what I mean," Marcus said. Gently. Which was worse than if he had been angry, because anger would have given her something to push against, and instead he was simply patient, the same patience he had brought to everything for five years, and for the first time she could see the far edge of it. Not close. But visible now, out there, where it hadn't been before.

"I'm not asking because I need to know about your grandmother," he said. "I don't need to know about your grandmother. I'm asking because I've watched you carry something around for two months and hand me the outline of it every single time instead of the thing itself. And I keep thinking. If she's doing that with a *recipe*."

He didn't finish it.

He didn't have to. Maya finished it in her own head, and it sat there in her own voice, which was worse: *what's she doing with the fellowship.*

She sat with it. Ten full seconds, maybe more. He noticed, and to his considerable credit, let it happen without filling any of it.

"There was a man," she said.

Marcus went still.

"Before my grandfather. Back home, in the province. She was engaged to him. There were invitations printed." Maya heard her own voice doing something strange and kept going anyway. "She came here in sixty-nine on a two-year visa, and the two years ran out, and she just. Didn't go back. Never explained it to him. Nobody knows if she ever even wrote to him properly. She let the time run out and she let that be the answer, and then she married my grandfather and never said the man's name out loud again for fifty-five years."

"Jesus."

"And every single time I get close to telling you about it, I stop." She was looking at the turned-over crossword rather than at him. "Because the second I say it out loud, I have to notice what I'm doing. Right now. To you. With a deadline sitting on the calendar."

The car alarm started up again downstairs, briefly, and stopped.

Marcus reached over and took her hand. Laced their fingers, thumb moving slowly across her knuckles, the old habit from the first year that had never once worn down into something absent.

"Okay," he said.

"That's it? Okay?"

"What did you think I was going to do?"

"I don't know. Something. Be angry. Point out that I've had two months."

"You've had two months and your grandmother died and I'm not a monster." He tightened his grip slightly. "That's the first true thing you've said to me in about three weeks, Maya. I'm not going to punish you for it. That would be an insane thing to do."

She laughed, wetly, once.

"For the record," he said, "the fellowship is a completely separate conversation and we're not having it today. I want that on the table. Today was about the other thing."

"You rehearsed that part too."

"I rehearsed all of it. I've been rehearsing it since Wednesday." He picked the crossword back up, turned it over, and studied it as though nothing had happened. "Nine across. Six letters. 'Kept quiet.'"

"Don't."

"I'm not doing a bit. That's nine across."

She leaned over and looked, and it genuinely was, and the two of them laughed in a way that had a great deal of relief in it and finished the puzzle badly, as usual, over another hour.

# Interlude: San Francisco, 1969 {.unnumbered}

[N]{.dropcap}ora had come over on the same program, six months ahead of her, and met Perla at the airport holding a hand-lettered sign that just said VILLANUEVA in case there was any confusion among the handful of other young Filipina women getting off the same connecting flight from Manila, all of them in the same slightly too-formal travel clothes their mothers had insisted on.

"You'll cry the first week," Nora told her, matter-of-fact, hauling one of Perla's suitcases off the curb before Perla could protest. "Everybody cries the first week. Then you'll get busy, and you'll still cry, just with less time to do it properly."

She was right. The hospital ran Perla ragged in a way nursing school never fully had, real patients instead of practice mannequins, a nursing supervisor who corrected her English pronunciation in front of other staff until Nora quietly took her aside and taught her which corrections to actually absorb and which ones were simply that particular supervisor enjoying the sound of her own authority.

She wrote to Ramon every week without fail, long letters full of small details she hoped would make the distance feel smaller: the exact shade the fog turned at sunset, a co-worker's terrible cooking, a patient who called her "sunshine" because he couldn't manage her actual name and she'd decided, after the third attempt, that it wasn't worth correcting him. Ramon wrote back just as often, letters that arrived three or four at a time in uneven bursts depending entirely on the mail boat's schedule, full of the elementary school's small dramas, a particularly disastrous class play, how the sea wall looked exactly the same as it always had though he swore the water itself felt different without her sitting beside it.

The first winter she wore two sweaters under her uniform and was cold anyway, and wrote home that the fog here was not like the fog at home, that it came in off the water and got into a person's bones, and that she had begun to understand why Americans talked about the weather constantly, which she had previously assumed was a failure of conversation.

She kept every single letter in a shoebox under her narrow bed in the nurses' dormitory, and some nights, when the homesickness came on hard and specific, the particular kind that arrived not as sadness exactly but as a physical ache located somewhere just under the ribs, she would take the whole box out and read through them in order, start to finish, like a story she already knew the ending to and needed to hear again anyway, purely for the comfort of a shape she could predict.

"Two years," she told Nora, more than once, in the dormitory kitchen over cups of tea neither of them could afford to waste on anything fancier. "It'll go fast. You'll see."

Nora, who had come over a full six months earlier and had already, privately, begun to suspect that two years abroad had a way of stretching and compressing itself depending on how a person's actual life back home was faring in her absence, only nodded and said nothing at all, because there was nothing useful yet to say, and because she liked Perla too much, this early on, to be the one who planted the first small seed of doubt.

They shared a room for fourteen months. Perla talked in her sleep, in Hiligaynon, mostly nonsense about work, and Nora never told her, on the grounds that a person can do nothing about it and will only be embarrassed to learn it.

Fifty-seven years later, at a wake in a funeral home on Hickey Boulevard, standing at the back of a room full of people she mostly did not know, that was what Nora found herself thinking about. Fourteen months of a young woman muttering about medication schedules in the dark, and nobody left alive who had heard it.

# Chapter 28

### The Miagao Group

[T]{.dropcap}he group was called Miagao, Iloilo: Kabataan Kag Kadamuan, and had a little over eleven thousand members.

Its header image was the church. Maya recognized it instantly, which gave her a strange jolt, because she had only ever seen it out of focus and behind a young man's shoulder. Here it was in daylight, in high resolution, and it was more than she had understood: the entire facade worked over in carved stone, coral and volcanic rock, saints and vines and a coat of arms she could not begin to read, the whole thing looking less like a building somebody had built than something that had grown that way.

She scrolled for the better part of an hour on a Thursday night, propped up in bed with the laptop on her knees.

It took her a while to work out what she was looking at.

A woman posting about her mother's ninetieth in Winnipeg. A funeral announcement with thirty comments. A long argument about a road. Somebody selling a car, in Iloilo, to an audience that was at least half in California. Graduation photographs, dozens of them, from three continents.

Half the people in this group lived in the town. The other half had not lived there in decades and were still, plainly, of it.

And scattered through all of it, more often than she expected, the exact thing she was about to do: somebody's blurry photograph out of somebody's drawer, and a caption asking whether anybody recognized these faces.

One of them had worked. That was the one she kept going back to: a man in Alberta, a photograph of two women outside a shop, and forty-one comments underneath it culminating in somebody's niece supplying both names and a street.

The poster had written back, four days later: *that is my mother on the left. thank you. I have not seen her face at that age.*

She read four of those threads before she posted her own.

They did not all end well. One had a single comment, from the poster herself, three weeks later, saying *no luck, thank you anyway.* Maya looked at that one for a while.

Her mother did not know she was doing this.

It sat underneath the whole evening and Maya declined to look at it directly. Back in August Liza had pushed a folder three inches across a counter and said *let her keep what she kept,* and Maya had gone home and copied a phrase onto an index card.

She wrote her own caption at ten past eleven and did not post it.

She rewrote it at half past. The first version explained too much. The second explained almost nothing and read as though she were hiding something. The third mentioned her grandmother by name and she took the name out, because she was not ready for the name to be searchable in a group of eleven thousand people who might have known her.

She thought about telling her mother. She got as far as opening the message thread and typing *are you up.*

She deleted it. Liza would not be up, and would answer in the morning, and would ask what it had been about, and Maya would have to say either the truth or something else, and she did not want to find out at midnight which one she'd pick.

She told herself she would raise it on the weekend, which she did not do.

What she finally posted, a little after midnight, was this:

*Does anyone recognize this church, or this man? Photo is from around 1968 or 1969. Trying to find information for a family history project.*

The photograph. No name attached.

She closed the laptop and lay in the dark with her heart going considerably faster than the situation warranted, aware that she had just put a picture of a stranger's face in front of eleven thousand people, and that this was either the most sensible thing she had done in two months or a genuine trespass, and that she was not going to be able to tell which until somebody answered.

Posting it was a specific and modern category of act. A letter can be intercepted. A phone call can go unanswered. A photograph posted at midnight to eleven thousand people has no version in which it comes back.

She did not sleep well.

At some point around two she woke up and reached for the laptop before she was properly conscious, and there was one comment on the post, from a man in Winnipeg, and it said *nice church.*

She lay in the dark afterward composing, involuntarily, all the ways this could go badly. A relative of Ramon's arriving angry. Somebody who had known her grandmother recognizing the account name. A comment section conducting a public inquest into a dead woman on the basis of one photograph posted at midnight by somebody who had not asked her mother first.

She fell asleep again around four.

She woke to fourteen comments.

Fourteen felt like an enormous number until she looked properly and understood the arithmetic of it: eleven thousand members, an overnight post, and a photograph of a church that people in that group would comment on if you posted it every day for a year.

Most of them were about the church. Three separate people were certain it was the parish church; one man was extremely firm that it was the convento steps and not the church proper at all and had posted two photographs of his own to prove it; the ensuing discussion had generated more energy than Maya's actual question. Two comments were tagging other people. One was a prayer hands emoji, unaccompanied.

The fourth comment down was from an account whose profile picture showed a woman in her fifties standing in front of that same stone facade, squinting slightly, someone's arm around her shoulders cropped out at the edge of frame.

*this looks like it could be my father when he was young. do you have a name? I can ask my mother.*

Maya sat up so fast the laptop nearly went off the side of the bed.

She caught it. She read the comment again. She read it a third time.

*I have a name,* she typed, and had to correct two typos before she could send eleven words. *Ramon Aguilar. Does that mean anything to you?*

The reply took four hours.

Four hours during which Maya taught an entire school day, checking her phone at every break, in the hallway, twice in the bathroom, once in the supply closet, and during which Dario asked her twice with real concern whether she was in trouble, because in his experience an adult who keeps looking at her phone that way has done something.

It came through during her prep period, at ten past one, one line long.

*That was my father's name. He passed fifteen years ago. I'm Fely. Who are you?*

She did not tell anybody. Not Roz two doors down, not her mother, not Marcus that evening on the phone.

She had, in the space of about ninety seconds in a school hallway, acquired the single largest piece of information anybody in her family had turned up in fifty-five years, and she carried it around for the rest of the day alone, and did not examine what that resembled.

# Chapter 29

### The Civil Registry

[S]{.dropcap}he had tried the front door first, back in September, before Nora gave her a surname. She would leave that part out later, when she told the story, because it made her sound more organized than she had felt at the time.

For about two weeks that month she pursued the official route, on the theory that a question this size deserved more than one attempt at an answer.

She emailed the Philippine Statistics Authority's civil registry division on a Sunday night, having found the correct address after about forty minutes and two wrong ones.

The email was four paragraphs and she rewrote the second one twice, trying to explain in bureaucratically legible terms why a woman in California needed a record from a provincial municipality in 1968 and could not supply a surname.

There was no legible way to say it. She settled on *family history research* and felt the phrase do exactly none of the work required of it.

She got an automated acknowledgment inside four minutes and nothing else for eleven days.

On day twelve she called the Philippine Consulate General in San Francisco during her lunch break, phone tucked against her shoulder at her own desk, eating a sandwich with one hand.

She was on hold for nineteen minutes. She listened to the entire hold loop three times and could have hummed it by the end.

The woman who eventually picked up had the patience of somebody who fields this exact call several times a week and has long since stopped being surprised by it.

"What year are you looking for?"

"Nineteen sixty-eight, sixty-nine. Maybe seventy."

"Manila, or provincial?"

"Provincial. Iloilo, a town called Miagao."

"Ah." A pause, and Maya heard something in it that was not encouraging. "So, records from that period, from a municipality rather than the capital, are usually held at the local civil registrar's office. Not centrally. Some of them are handwritten. They predate any digitization."

"Handwritten meaning..."

"Meaning a ledger. In a filing cabinet. In an office in that town." The woman said this without any drama whatsoever, as a person stating operating conditions. "Which means somebody has to physically go and look, or you route a formal request through a local contact and wait. It can take months. And you'd need a full legal name and an approximate date for it to have much chance."

"I have a first name. Possibly a first name."

"That's difficult."

"Yeah."

There was a small silence in which the woman audibly decided to be more helpful than her job strictly required.

"You'd likely do better with the parish," she said. "Baptismal and marriage records in a Catholic town that old are sometimes better kept than the civil ones. Particularly pre-martial law. A lot of provincial registries lost material over the years, flooding, fire, or just nobody being paid to look after it." A pause. "If your family has any connection to the parish at all, that's usually faster than coming through us. I'm sorry. I know that's not what you called for."

Maya thanked her and hung up and sat at her desk with half a sandwich for the remaining six minutes of lunch.

There was something faintly absurd about it that she turned over for a while afterward. The official channel, the one with the letterhead and the phone tree and the department name, was slower and considerably less likely to work than eleven thousand strangers in a Facebook group arguing about a church.

Though on reflection, she thought, that was not absurd at all. That was exactly the gap a diaspora learns to fill for itself, over enough decades of official systems being too slow to be of use to anybody who actually needed them.

She got as far, one evening, as finding the parish itself online: a page, some photographs, a phone number with a country code in front of it.

She looked at the number for a while and did the arithmetic on the time difference and understood that calling it would mean a woman in California asking a stranger in a rectory, in English, to search a handwritten book for a name she could not fully supply, on behalf of a dead woman he had never heard of.

She closed the tab.

She never did pursue the parish. Nora gave her a surname at the end of that month, and three weeks after that a woman named Fely answered a photograph, and the formal search became abruptly, gratefully unnecessary.

But she kept the email thread. It sat in a folder in her inbox for years, unarchived, unanswered by anyone, a small artifact of the two weeks she had spent knocking politely at the front of a building while the side door stood open the whole time.

# Chapter 30

### A Different Ramon Entirely

[F]{.dropcap}ely's message wasn't the only one Maya had chased that week. It was simply the one that turned out to be real.

Comment number nine on her original post had come from a man named Boy Alvarez, no profile photo, an account that looked freshly made, insisting he was certain the man in her photograph was his own late uncle, also named Ramon, also from somewhere on Panay Island though he was fuzzy on the exact town. He'd messaged her privately within the hour, the same morning she'd first written to Fely, eager in a way that struck Maya, in retrospect, as slightly too eager, offering to connect her with cousins who might know more, asking, almost as an afterthought tucked into his third message, whether her family might be in a position to help cover the cost of tracking down old records, since apparently the local civil registry charged a fee for archival searches that a struggling nephew abroad simply couldn't manage on his own.

She'd almost sent money before something in the wording of his fourth message, a slightly too-polished request for exactly two hundred dollars, made her stop and actually look up the account itself, which turned out to have been created six weeks earlier and had, as far as she could tell scrolling back through it, posted nothing at all except variations of the same story to at least four other people's photographs of unidentified older men, each time claiming a different late uncle.

"People do this constantly in these groups," Roz told her at lunch, unsurprised, when Maya described it with some embarrassment that same week, right around the time Fely's first real reply about Ramon Aguilar was still sitting unanswered while Maya worked up the nerve to respond properly. "Preys on exactly this kind of thing, homesick diaspora people looking for family. My mom almost got taken twice looking for her own father's people. You did the right thing catching it before you sent anything."

She reported the account, which took ninety seconds and about which she felt briefly and disproportionately righteous. It was gone within a week. The four posts he had commented on remained, with his replies still under them, attached to a name that no longer led anywhere.

It bothered her for a surprisingly long time. Not the attempted scam of two hundred dollars, which had cost her nothing and which she had caught. The other thing: that for about thirty-six hours a man she had never met had known more about what she wanted than anybody in her own family did, and had known it because she had told him, freely, in the first message, the way you tell a stranger.

# Chapter 31

### What the Paperwork Actually Meant

[S]{.dropcap}he spent a Saturday at the main library downtown because she had reached the point of needing to know what the words on the page meant, rather than continuing to carry the vague secondhand weight of them.

She had the notice photographed on her phone. She had a list of terms copied out of it in her own hand. She had, by two in the afternoon, a stack of things a reference librarian named Elaine had produced for her over the course of ninety minutes with visible and increasing enthusiasm, having established early on that this was not a school project.

The program had a whole documented history. That was the first thing that landed, and it landed oddly: this was not obscure. It had a name and a founding statute and a substantial literature, and Maya had never once heard it mentioned by any member of her own family.

Postwar. Originally intended to bring foreign professionals into the country for a fixed period of cultural exchange and then return them home again, the returning being the point of it rather than an inconvenience attached to it.

Then, through the fifties and into the sixties, American hospitals discovered the thing that changed everything: the Philippines had been producing nurses on an American-designed curriculum, in English, for decades. They were, by the standards of any hospital struggling to staff a floor, the most available qualified people in the world.

Thousands of them came. Maya sat at a table with a photocopied chart in front of her and looked at the numbers for a long time.

And most of them went home.

That was the sentence that stopped her. She read the surrounding paragraph three times to be sure she had it right.

Most of the women on this identical visa, with this identical two-year clock, did the thing they had promised to do. They finished, and they packed, and they got on a plane, and they went back to a province with a credential and a story.

Which meant 1971 had not been the ordinary outcome of an ordinary process. It had not simply happened to Perla Villanueva the way weather happens to somebody.

It had been a choice. Made by one specific twenty-three-year-old, alone, under real pressure, cutting against what most of the women around her managed to do.

Maya drove home with a headache building behind her right eye and turned that over the whole way.

It did not make her think less of her grandmother. That surprised her. What it did was make the story stop being history and start being a person: not a young woman carried along by forces, but a young woman who had been given a hard thing and had failed to do it well, an entirely different and much more recognizable kind of story.

She found the other line later that week, at eleven at night, in a paper she had no business reading and did not fully understand.

It was one sentence in a section about the two-year requirement. It said that for many of these women the requirement had been less a bureaucratic technicality than a genuine site of anguish: family expectation pulling one way, an actual new life pulling the other, and no available course of action that honored both.

Maya read it three times and then closed the laptop.

Nobody had written that sentence about Perla Villanueva. Nobody ever would; she was not in any literature, she was in a cookbook on a counter in Daly City.

But something came loose in Maya's chest reading it anyway, and it took her a while to identify what. It was this: the thing her family had spent fifty-five years treating as a private and slightly shameful anomaly was, in fact, documented. Common. Studied. A known and difficult category of human decision that thousands of women had faced, and that some of them had handled gracefully, and that her grandmother had handled badly, in the specific way a frightened person handles a thing badly.

Not a scandal. A hard choice, failed at, by somebody's terrified daughter.

# Chapter 32

### Costumes

[B]{.dropcap}y the last week of October, Room 14 had achieved the condition that overtakes every elementary classroom in the days before Halloween, which is that no subtraction problem could hold anybody's attention for longer than about ninety seconds before somebody asked, again, whether they could please just talk about costumes.

Maya had tried bargaining. Five minutes of costume talk at the end of the day, contingent on the worksheet. This had worked for approximately one day and had then been treated by the class as a permanent constitutional right subject to renegotiation upward.

Dario had changed his plan four times since September.

Dinosaur in September, which had been abandoned for reasons never disclosed. Astronaut through the first half of October. Then, in the third week, an announcement delivered at carpet time with total confidence: he would be attending as a ghost, but the cool kind.

Maya had asked several follow-up questions about this over the intervening days and had not been able to establish what distinguished the cool kind from the other kind, or what the other kind was, or whether Dario himself had a clear picture of either. He remained certain and completely unable to elaborate.

There were two weeks of negotiation with the office about whether masks were permitted, resolved by a compromise that satisfied nobody: masks allowed for the parade, removed for classroom time, an arrangement that produced twenty-six children carrying masks around all afternoon and losing them.

He arrived on the day in a white sheet with two eye holes and sunglasses on over the top of it.

"Oh," Maya said. "The sunglasses."

"The sunglasses," Dario confirmed, with enormous satisfaction, and walked past her into the room.

The parade wound through every grade at eleven: a loose, cheerful procession of superheroes and princesses and, that year, an inexplicable and genuinely impressive number of dinosaurs, past the front office where the principal handed out stickers with the weary good humor of a man doing this for the fourteenth time.

Two children cried. One about a mask, one about nothing anybody could identify. A fifth grader's costume shed glitter along a hundred yards of hallway that would remain visible in the grout until roughly February.

Roz's class marched directly behind Maya's, and once the parading was done the two of them fell into step at the back and stood at the edge of the yard watching two hundred children in costume attempt to eat cupcakes without transferring frosting to anything within a three-foot radius.

They were failing. Every single one of them.

"You look better," Roz said, watching the carnage rather than Maya.

"Do I."

"Since the Facebook thing. You've had a face on since September and it's mostly gone."

"She wrote back. There's a woman in Iloilo and he was her father." Maya licked frosting off her own thumb without any dignity whatsoever. "I've been drafting a proper message to her for a week and I've deleted four of them, which is going about as well as you'd expect."

"Send the fifth one."

"You're going to be unbearable about this."

"I'm going to be accurate about it." Roz bumped her shoulder, the same easy gesture she always used to soften something true. "There's a difference between unbearable and accurate, and usually the accurate ones just feel worse going down."

Dario found them by the punch table a few minutes later, still fully committed to his own costume, whatever it technically was, and informed Maya with great seriousness that ghosts, the cool kind specifically, did not have to eat vegetables, ever, a rule he seemed to believe applied at least through the end of the school day if not indefinitely.

"I'll allow it for today," Maya told him, and he walked off satisfied, and she stood there a moment longer in the thin autumn sun, surrounded by frosting-smeared costumes and the chaos of an ordinary school holiday.

# Chapter 33

### What Marcus Is, Underneath the Fellowship

[S]{.dropcap}he still hadn't answered Fely. She had read the message perhaps thirty times, entirely unsure what she was actually asking for, or what she'd be opening if she pursued it any further, and that evening she had dinner with Marcus, on a Friday that, for once, had nothing at all to do with any of it.

He cooked, badly, on purpose, a running joke between them going back nearly two years now, in which he attempted something ambitious well beyond his actual skill and she ate it anyway without complaint, tonight a lemon pasta with far too much acid and not quite enough salt to balance it. They ate standing at the kitchen counter rather than bothering to set the small table for two people who'd known each other five years and had long since stopped needing the formality.

The second two-week extension had run out in the last week of October. Neither of them had marked it. She had noticed the date go past on a Wednesday, at school, between reading groups and lunch, and had put her phone face down on a stack of spelling tests and taught fractions, and Marcus had not called that night or the night after, and by the third night the not-calling had stopped being something either of them could raise without also explaining why they had let it go two days. So it went unraised, and became instead a small quiet object sitting on the table between them.

"You're somewhere else tonight," he said, not accusing her of it, just noting it the way you'd note a change in weather.

"I'm here."

She wasn't, not entirely, and they both understood that clearly, but he let it pass without pushing, the way he'd been letting things pass for over a week now with a patience that was beginning to cost him something visible, a tightness around his eyes that hadn't been there back in the spring.

"Chicago's got genuinely great pizza, you know. Deep dish. I looked it up, purely for research purposes, you understand, academic."

"You hate deep dish. You've told me, in exactly those words, that deep dish is a casserole with delusions of grandeur."

"I could grow. People grow, apparently, it happens all the time." He bumped her shoulder with his, and she bumped him back, and for one easy second it felt the way it used to feel before any of this, before there was a fellowship and a hard deadline and a photograph of a man she'd never met laughing at whoever was holding a camera sixty years ago.

Later, settled together on the couch, his hand found hers, fingers laced, his thumb moving slowly across her knuckles, the old habit. Tonight it went on longer than usual. Neither of them remarked on it. She let her head rest against his shoulder and thought, not about Ramon, not yet about an invitation she hadn't found, but about the plain, uncomplicated fact that she liked being exactly here, in this apartment that smelled faintly of scorched lemon, with this occasionally disastrous home cook, and that whatever answer she eventually gave him, it needed to come from somewhere inside her and not simply from a deadline doing the deciding on her behalf.

"Two more weeks isn't really about the fellowship timeline anymore, is it," Marcus said quietly, into her hair, not really a question so much as something he'd already worked out for himself and was leaving room for her to confirm.

"No," she admitted, after a moment. "It isn't."

He didn't push any further than that. He only held on a little tighter, the same slow thumb moving over her knuckles, and let her half-answer sit quietly in the room with them a while longer, which was, she understood even then, its own form of trust, one she was beginning to realize she couldn't keep asking of him indefinitely without eventually spending it all.

Later he fell asleep on the couch before nine, which he did perhaps twice a year and always denied afterward, and she sat with the weight of him against her shoulder and the television on low and thought, with no particular drama attached to it, that she would like to be doing this in twenty years and that she had never once said so out loud to him.

# Chapter 34

### Four Days

[H]{.dropcap}e called on the Tuesday, at ten past eight, which was not a time he called.

She was at the sink with her sleeves pushed up and she answered with wet hands, which meant she had the phone tucked against her shoulder and could not see his name properly, and she said hello in the bright, unguarded way you say it when you have not checked.

"The date was six days ago," Marcus said.

Maya turned the tap off.

"I know when it was."

"I want to say a thing and I'd like to get all the way through it."

"Okay."

"I've stopped being able to tell the difference between you thinking about it and you not thinking about it." He was not raising his voice. He had never raised his voice at her in five years and she understood, standing there dripping onto the floor, that this was going to be worse. "That's the actual problem. It isn't the date. I've watched you for two months and I cannot tell, from the outside, whether there is anything happening in there at all."

"That's not fair."

"It might not be. I've stopped being able to tell that either."

She said his name.

"I don't think you're coming," Marcus said. "I think you worked that out some time in October and you've been waiting for me to be the one to say it, so that when it happens it'll have been something that happened to us rather than something you did. And I've been sitting here for six days being extremely careful not to say it, because I promised you in September that I wouldn't do that, and I have kept that promise, and I would like it noted that keeping it has cost me more than you have any idea about."

The refrigerator was running. She could hear it.

"Marcus."

"Forget it," he said. "It's late. Forget I called."

And he hung up, which he had also never done, and she stood in her kitchen with the phone in her hand and water going cold on her forearms.

He did not call the next night.

He did not call the night after that either, and on the third she typed four different messages and sent the smallest one, which was about a thing his colleague had posted, and he answered it two hours later with a single line that was perfectly pleasant and closed the subject with the efficiency of a door.

On the fourth day she cooked.

She did it without examining it, the way you do a thing your hands already know, and she was most of the way there with a foil-covered dish belted into the passenger seat before she heard what she was doing.

He opened the door and looked at the dish rather than at her.

"You didn't have to do that."

"I know."

"Is this an apology?"

"It's kaldereta."

He held the door open. She went in. He got two plates down without asking whether she was eating, which she was not, and then put one back, and ate standing up at the counter in his coat, and said it was good, twice, the second time more carefully than the first.

She said the safest true sentence available to her, which was that she was still working it out. He said okay. Neither of them said Tuesday out loud, and neither of them said the word deadline, and by nine they were watching most of something with a foot of couch between them that had not been there in five years.

He put the container in the sink afterward and ran water into it.

Driving home she went through the whole evening twice looking for the place where she had actually answered him, and could not find it, and understood somewhere around the toll plaza exactly which woman's method she had just used, and on whom, and how well it had worked.

It held for eleven days.

# Chapter 35

### Two Documents, Folded Together

[S]{.dropcap}he hadn't finished the last box. She'd told herself, on both previous visits to the garage, that she was done, that she'd found whatever there was left to find, but there remained one flat box at the very bottom of the stack that she had set quietly aside both times without opening, marked in her mother's hasty hand simply OLD, a label that was, in retrospect, perhaps the least honest word Liza had written on any box in the entire clear-out.

She opened it alone on a Sunday afternoon while her mother was at the eleven o'clock Mass, which Maya had started quietly skipping without offering anyone an explanation, and then at Tita Fe's in Pacifica, where she spent most Sunday afternoons that fall.

Beneath a layer of tissue paper gone yellow and brittle with age, beneath a christening gown that had to have been Liza's own judging by its size, she found a plain hospital envelope, its return address printed in a San Francisco font style that hadn't existed on any envelope for forty years, and folded carefully inside it, pressed nearly flat by decades of other things stacked on top, two separate pieces of paper that had clearly been folded together on purpose, deliberately, and kept that exact way ever since.

The first was the exchange visitor notice she'd already found once before, whether this was a second copy or the very original she couldn't say for certain.

The second was a printed wedding invitation, formal, bordered in gold foil gone dull and slightly tarnished with age, printed with a church's name and a family name Maya now recognized instantly: Aguilar, set opposite the name Villanueva. *You are cordially invited to witness the marriage of Ramon Aguilar and Perla Villanueva.*

The date had never been filled in. A blank horizontal line sat exactly where a date should have been printed, waiting, the kind of invitation ordered well in advance so a family could plan the actual celebration around whatever date eventually got settled, the way Liza herself had once explained, offhandedly, years earlier, that weddings back home were sometimes arranged, a wedding close enough on the horizon to be real but not yet fixed to an exact day.

Perla had kept it. Not hidden away in some drawer where an accident might have stumbled upon it by chance, not thrown out entirely in whatever version of a clean, final break a different woman might have chosen instead. Folded, deliberately, inside the very document that explained precisely why the blank date line had never been filled in, and kept together that way for fifty-five uninterrupted years, through two different apartments and one entire adopted country and a whole complete life lived directly on top of it.

Maya sat on her mother's living room floor with both documents spread flat across her knees, the same worn maroon carpet beneath her that had been beneath her as a small child, and understood, fully, for the very first time, that her grandmother had not simply forgotten to go home, had not let some ordinary distraction of hospital work carry her past a deadline by pure accident. She had kept the precise, physical evidence of the exact choice she'd made. Filed it. Folded it with obvious care. And never once let a single person in this family see it.

She checked the envelope again afterward, twice, in case there was anything else in it.

There was not. Two sheets of paper, folded together, in a hospital envelope, under a christening gown, in a box marked OLD.

The thing she kept returning to, sitting there, was the physical act. Not the keeping. Anybody keeps things.

Somebody had, at some specific hour on some specific day, taken two pieces of paper that had nothing to do with one another, and put one inside the other, and folded them together as a single object.

That was a decision. It took maybe four seconds and it was completely deliberate. A woman in a small apartment on Guttenberg Street had held a wedding invitation with a blank date on it and a government letter explaining precisely why the date was blank, and had chosen to file them as one thing.

And then had carried that one thing through two apartments, a marriage, a daughter, a granddaughter, and fifty-five years, without ever showing it to a single living person.

It was, if anything, the precise opposite of forgetting. It was choosing, deliberately and for over half a century, to remember entirely alone.

# Chapter 36

### Sitting With It

[S]{.dropcap}he did not call her mother right away.

She did not call Marcus either, though she picked up the phone twice and put it back down twice, and the second time she noticed she had been about to call him to tell him about the invitation, which would have been the first fully honest thing she'd offered him since the morning of the crossword, and she put the phone down anyway.

Instead she sat on the floor of her mother's living room.

The maroon carpet was the same carpet that had been under her at four years old and at eleven and at twenty-two. She had never once, in twenty-seven years, examined it. It turned out to be worn nearly through in a path between the doorway and the kitchen, and not worn at all under the window.

She read the notice again. Then again. She read it perhaps nine times over the course of the next hour, until the phrases came apart into shapes and stopped meaning anything, the way any word does if you look at it long enough.

*exchange visitor status.*

*two-year foreign residence requirement.*

She traced the blank date line on the invitation with one finger. Slowly, the whole length of it, twice.

She would watch her mother do the same thing the next afternoon, with the same finger, and neither of them had learned it from anybody.

The light through the front window went from afternoon to the flatter gray that comes before evening in that town, which is not sunset so much as the fog deciding.

She thought about Marcus. There was a deadline sitting in her planner that had now been extended twice, by her, on the phone, in a voice she had heard herself using and had not liked.

She thought about a woman in Iloilo who had sent a stranger four short sentences more than two weeks ago and had no idea that the stranger was about to ask her to help account for fifty-five years of somebody else's silence.

Mostly, though, she thought about a twenty-three-year-old in a dormitory room in San Francisco in 1971, holding these exact two pieces of paper. Not similar ones. These. The same physical objects, fifty-five years from where Maya was sitting on a carpet, and the young woman holding them had been deciding, or failing to decide, or letting a date on a form decide for her, what the rest of an entire life was going to look like.

At one point she got up and walked the length of the living room and back, twice, because her legs had gone and because sitting still had stopped being possible.

Her mother's house at four in the afternoon in November, on a Sunday: the television off, the good glasses in the cabinet, a stack of mail on the side table that Liza would sort tonight and answer tomorrow, because that is what Liza does with mail.

An entirely ordinary room, with two pieces of paper on the carpet in the middle of it.

She did not cry.

That surprised her, sitting there. Given the size of it. She kept waiting to and it did not arrive, and what came instead was something more disorienting and much harder to name: the specific vertigo of learning that a person you have known completely for twenty-seven years was, the entire time, also somebody else. Not a different person. An additional one. A whole further continent of a woman that nobody in the family had ever once been permitted to set foot on.

Her legs went to sleep at some point. She noticed distantly and did not move.

At quarter to six her phone lit up against the carpet. Roz. A photograph first: the classroom fish tank, and taped to the glass a sticky note in handwriting Maya recognized instantly as Dario's, block capitals, THIS FISH IS HUNGRY. I CAN TELL. Then the message underneath: *found this while stealing your stapler. he cornered me at dismissal to make the case in person. cited evidence. you have built a small litigator.*

Maya laughed. Alone, on the floor of her mother's living room, with two pieces of paper in front of her that had just rearranged her entire understanding of her family, the laugh came up out of her whole and unguarded, and it felt wrong for about two seconds and then did not.

*Do not feed him extra,* she wrote back. *Kevin is on a plan.*

*already ruled on it. half a pinch. compromise is the heart of democracy.*

She sat with the phone in her hand a while after that. Tomorrow there was a vocabulary quiz she had not finished writing, and a boy who checked that tank the first minute of every morning, and a room that ran on her turning up to it. She pulled her planner over from her bag and wrote the quiz right there, the whole thing, ten words and a bonus, on the floor, in a quarter of an hour, and it was the most ordinary thing she had done all day and the only one that felt like standing on solid ground.

She called her mother a little before seven.

"I'm at the house," she said. "Are you free tomorrow afternoon?"

She did not say anything else. She had planned to and found, with the phone against her ear, that she could not do it over a phone.

Something in her voice must have carried more than she intended, because Liza did not ask a single question.

There was a silence on the line of perhaps four seconds.

"Yes," her mother said. "I'm free tomorrow."

And she said it in the tone of a woman who had just understood, with real dread, precisely which conversation was finally, after three months of everybody circling it, about to happen.

# Interlude: Ramon {.unnumbered}

[T]{.dropcap}he mail came up from Iloilo on Thursdays. By the third Thursday he had stopped walking down to the plaza to meet it, and by the ninth he had started again, because not going had turned out to take more attention than going.

Aling Terya sorted it on the table outside her store, the same table she sold cigarettes off one at a time, and she read the names out loud as she went. Everybody had complained about this for years. Nobody had ever asked her to stop.

She got through about twenty. Two for the Denanos. Something official for the municipio. A parcel that had been opened and retied by somebody along the way and that she held up for the whole plaza to consider before she put it down.

Ramon stood at the edge of it with his hands in his pockets, the way a man stands when he has come for something else and will remember what it was in a moment.

"Nothing today," Aling Terya said, without looking up.

"I wasn't waiting on anything."

"No." She squared the pile. "Nobody is."

He bought a cigarette he did not want and stayed until she had finished, on the grounds that leaving immediately would have been a kind of announcement. Then he walked back up toward the school with it unlit behind his ear.

One of his own boys came past him at a run, going the wrong way, shirt out.

"Sir. Sir, is it going to rain?"

Ramon looked at the sky, properly, for the first time that afternoon. It was doing the thing it did in that month, thickening over the water without committing to anything.

"It's going to rain," he said. "Take the short way."

The boy took the long way. Ramon watched him go and did not call after him, and thought that this was roughly the whole of what a teacher could accomplish in one afternoon, and went in to lock the classroom.

He had written once more himself and had not written again. There were several versions he composed on the sea wall over those months and never put on paper, and all of them began with a question he could not get to the end of. Whether he had done something. Whether there was somebody. Whether it was only that America was very far away and two years was a long time to ask of a woman who was twenty-three and alone in it.

Somewhere in the second year he worked out that a woman who had gone that quiet had already answered, in the only way she was going to, and that asking again would make him smaller in his own eyes without making her any louder.

The invitations stayed in a drawer at his mother's house. She raised it exactly once, holding a stack of them in both hands the way you hold something you are about to be told to throw away.

"Shall I."

"Leave them," Ramon said, and she left them, and neither of them mentioned them again, and she was gone a long time before he ever thought to wonder what she had done with them.

He met Corazon at a cousin's wedding two years later, over an argument about whether the band was playing the song wrong. He did not fall for her quickly. He fell the way a man puts weight back onto an ankle, a little at a time, testing.

He told her everything early, on the second or third real conversation, because he had decided by then that a marriage which started with something held back would go on holding it back forever.

Corazon heard the whole of it out and said she had no interest in competing with a woman on the other side of an ocean, only in building something with the man in front of her, and then asked whether he intended to keep telling her about it or whether they could eat.

He taught until he was sixty-three, and then, having retired formally and been given a plaque and a merienda, kept turning up two mornings a week for another four years on an arrangement nobody had authorized and nobody was willing to be the one to end.

He thought about Perla rarely by then, and without bitterness when he did, mostly as a distant, fond curiosity about whether the thing she had gone for had turned out to be worth what it cost her.

He died at sixty-seven without ever learning that she had kept the invitation. That is the one fact that would have told him the leaving had cost her something too, and it was six thousand miles away in a hospital envelope under a christening gown, and nobody on either side of the ocean knew it was there.

# Chapter 37

### What Liza Actually Knows

[L]{.dropcap}iza came home from work on Monday afternoon to find her daughter sitting at the kitchen table with both documents laid flat between them, side by side, and this time she didn't reach automatically for her coffee cup, didn't turn toward the sink, didn't perform any of the small domestic tasks she usually reached for when she needed somewhere to put her hands that wasn't her daughter's face.

She sat down across from Maya instead, still in her work cardigan, and was quiet for a long moment.

"Where was this," she said finally.

"Bottom box. Under your christening gown."

Liza reached out and touched the blank date line with one finger, very lightly, as though it might still be capable, after fifty-five years, of hurting somebody.

"She told me this once," Liza said slowly, the words coming out like something translated from a much further place than her usual voice, "and not when I asked. I asked her exactly once, at seventeen, and got two sentences and a closed door for it, and I never asked a second time. This was years later, when I was pregnant with you. She was at my kitchen table one afternoon, shelling peas she had brought over herself for no reason anybody had requested, and she just started talking, no warning, no question from me at all. That she'd meant to go home the whole time. That was the entire plan from the very beginning, apparently, two years and then home, marry Ramon, the wedding half arranged already before she'd even gotten on the plane to come here.
