She said the hospital floor was desperately short-staffed the exact month her two years came up, that there was nobody available to properly cover her patients, that she'd meant to formally ask for the leave and simply never got around to actually asking, and then it was three months past the deadline, then six, and by that point she said it felt somehow harder to go back and explain the delay than to just keep on not explaining it at all." Liza's finger stayed resting on the blank line. "She only told me this the once. Never brought it up again in my presence, not one single time in all the years after, and I understood even then, somehow, sitting there with a bowl of peas between us, that I'd been handed something she had no intention of ever repeating to me or to anyone."

It was very nearly what Nora had told her in the thrift shop. The floor short. The meaning to ask. Three months, then six. Nearly thirty years apart, her grandmother had given two women the same reasons in the same order, and Maya understood that by the second time it had stopped being a memory and become the version, the one that could be told.

"Did she ever say his name to you? Out loud. Once."

Liza thought about it for a long time.

"No," she said. "I heard it from her exactly once and it wasn't to me. She was on the phone to Tita Nora, in the kitchen, I was maybe twenty-two, and she said something in Hiligaynon and then a name, and she stopped talking when she noticed I was in the doorway."

"Ramon."

"I knew whose name it was." Liza said it flatly, the way she said a price. "I had known since I was fifteen. I stood in that doorway with a glass in my hand and I heard my mother say it out loud for the only time in her life, and I turned around and went back up the hall and I did not ask her one single question about it, and I was twenty-two years old and I had already decided by then that I was not going to be the one who made her say things." Liza shook her head slowly. "I have thought about that four-second piece of a phone call more since the funeral than in the thirty years before it."

"She never actually told Ramon no. She just never said anything at all."

"No." Liza looked up, and her eyes were wet now but her voice had stopped cracking, had moved past the place where it cracked into something flatter and considerably more tired. "She let the silence answer for her. For years I believed that made her a coward, plain and simple. I am fifty-one years old now, apo, and I think it more likely just made her human, and I have also come to understand that those two things are not always as different from one another as I badly needed them to be, for most of my life, because the alternative was believing my own mother was capable of doing something that genuinely cruel to a man who loved her."

"Do you think she loved Grandpa?"

"I think she loved your grandfather completely." Liza answered that one instantly, without a single beat of hesitation, the one thing in this entire conversation she seemed to hold with total certainty. "I also think there exists a version of her, a twenty-three-year-old version, six thousand miles from everything she'd ever known, with a clock running down to nothing, who made an actual choice by refusing outright to make one, and then spent the rest of her whole life being a devoted wife to a genuinely good man, partly, I now suspect, to prove to herself that the choice she'd made by not making it had been the correct one after all. Both of those things are true at once. I don't fully know how to hold them both in my hands at the same time, apo, but your grandmother apparently managed exactly that for fifty-five straight years, so." She folded the invitation gently back along its own soft, worn crease, handling it now with a careful attention she hadn't shown it earlier in the conversation. "I suppose we can try to learn how, too."

# Chapter 38

### Almost Stopping

[S]{.dropcap}he closed the laptop on Monday night and decided she was done.

It felt, at the time, like conviction. It had the exact texture of a good decision: a settling, a lightness, the sense of having finally arrived at the sensible position after weeks of thrashing.

She had a name. She had a face, laughing in front of a church sixty years ago. She had her mother's own halting version of it, given across a kitchen table with considerably more honesty than Liza had wanted to give, and she had watched her mother's hands fold the invitation back along a crease that had held for more than fifty years.

What exactly was she still chasing.

A stranger on another continent who owed her nothing. Some sense of a finished story that was not, when she looked at it directly, even hers to want finished. Both of the actual people involved had gone on to live and marry and grieve and grow old without ever requiring Maya's assistance with any of it. Ramon had been dead fifteen years. Perla had been dead not quite five months. Neither of them had ever asked to be reconciled.

And her mother was still hurting.

She kept returning to that over the following three days, because it was the strongest piece and because it was true. Liza had been carrying an invitation since she was fifteen years old. She had not asked for it to come out of the box.

So Maya stopped.

She did not open the Miagao group on Tuesday. She did not open it Wednesday either, which was harder, or Thursday morning, which was harder still.

She did not take the photographs out. She left the folder where it was, and worked, and made dinner, and slept better than she had in a month, and told herself that this was what relief felt like.

It was Roz who undid it, at the copier on Thursday afternoon, with a stack of parent conference forms and no idea she was doing anything at all.

"You seem lighter this week."

"Do I."

"You do." Roz fed a form into the tray. "Did you write back to her yet? The daughter?"

"I stopped."

Roz's hands paused on the machine.

"Why?"

"Because it isn't really mine to dig up." Maya heard herself say it and heard how good it sounded, which should have been a warning. "My mom's still hurting over what I already found. It seems cruel to keep going just because I'm curious about a dead man I never met."

Roz turned around and looked at her properly.

It was the look she used on a fourth grader who has given an answer that is technically not wrong and is concealing the real one underneath it.

"Is that actually why you stopped?"

"Roz."

"I'm asking. Is that the reason, or is it easier to call it kindness toward your mother than to say you're frightened of whatever's at the end of it?"

Maya opened her mouth.

"Because you don't have to keep going," Roz said, and there was no heat in it at all, which was what made it land. "That's fine. Nobody's making you and there's no prize. Stop if you want to stop." She turned back to the copier. "Just don't dress up scared as considerate. Your Lola deserves better than a good excuse, and honestly so do you."

Maya taught the rest of the afternoon.

She drove home. She made dinner. She sat with it for about two hours and got progressively worse at not thinking about it.

Then she opened the laptop for the first time in four days, not because she had decided anything, but because Roz's question had lodged somewhere she could not reach to get it out.

The message thread was where she had left it. Untouched since Fely's last line.

*I'm Fely. Who are you?*

Four days she had left it there this week, and had told herself she had made a decision about it, and had in fact simply been frightened for seventy-two hours and had found a more respectable word for that.

# Chapter 39

### Sunday Lunch

[T]{.dropcap}ita Fe brought the adobo, famous in the family, or infamous, depending on which member of the family you asked and whether they had been present in 2009.

There were nine of them around a table built for six. That was standard. Rico was on speaker again, propped against the gravy boat, contributing badly.

"Is that Fe's adobo?"

"It is."

"Ask her if she used the vinegar."

"He wants to know if you used the vinegar," Maya relayed.

"Tell him I have been making this dish since before he could walk," said Tita Fe, without looking up, "and that he may take it up with me directly, in person, at a location of my choosing."

"She says she loves you," Maya told the phone.

They got through the meal itself without incident. It was over dessert that Aunty Baby, who had eaten two full plates and was working through a slice of flan with visible satisfaction, said the thing she had apparently been holding for a very long time.

"You know your Lola left somebody at home before she ever married your Lolo Ernesto."

The table went quiet in a way that only a family table can, which is to say instantly and completely and without anybody putting down a fork.

"Baby," said Tita Fe.

"What. The girl's been asking." Aunty Baby licked the back of her spoon, entirely unbothered. "She's been asking for three months, at every single one of these, and everybody at this table has been talking around it like she can't hear us."

"That's not the point," Liza started.

"There was talk," Aunty Baby went on, comfortably, "before I even came over myself, mind, so this is 1974 at the latest, that she'd left somebody behind at home. Nobody ever said it to her face. You didn't, then." She gestured with the spoon. "But the old ladies at the parish knew. Those women knew everything. God rest them, every one, they were terrifying."

"You *knew*?"

Liza said it sharper than she meant to. Maya heard the edge come off her mother's voice a half-second too late to be caught.

Aunty Baby looked at her with something close to affection.

"Everybody your mother's age knew something, hija. We ate our dinner and we decided a long time ago that it wasn't ours to carry into her kitchen." She shrugged. "She built a good life here. A real one. She fed practically every person sitting at this table at some point. What is one old story next to fifty years of that."

"It's not one old story if nobody ever said it out loud."

That was Liza. Very quietly. Not looking at anyone.

There was a pause of about three seconds, which around this table was an eternity.

Then Tita Fe said the pancit needed calamansi, and Rico said from the phone that the pancit always needed calamansi and that this was a chronic issue, and Aunty Baby said she was going to have a third piece of flan and would like it noted that nobody had stopped her, and the table went back to being a table.

Maya sat through the rest of it mostly quiet.

Watching the story she had grown up inside of take one more small crack. The one about Perla who came to America and fell in love with the whole country and built a family out of nothing but will. It was not collapsing. She had not expected that. It was absorbing the crack and continuing to hold, the way a good plate takes a hairline fracture and goes right on holding soup for another thirty years.

Still useful. Still whole enough for daily use.

Just visibly, permanently, not the same plate.

# Chapter 40

### Aunty Baby's House

[A]{.dropcap}unty Baby lived alone in a small house in South San Francisco crowded with the clutter of a woman who had outlived one husband and refused to downsize for anybody's convenience, and she poured Maya a cup of instant coffee neither of them particularly wanted before she'd even asked why Maya had called ahead to visit on a Tuesday, not, as far as either of them could remember, a day they'd ever specifically visited each other on before.

"You want to know how I knew," Aunty Baby said, settling into a recliner that clearly had a permanent, worn-in shape of her already built into it. "About the fence story I told at the misa. And the other thing, the thing I let slip at Sunday lunch that your mother wanted to strangle me for."

"I want to know how everyone always seems to know something I don't."

"Because we were all young in the same country at the same time, apo, far from home in the exact same way, and women like that talk to each other even when they don't mean to." Aunty Baby wrapped both hands around her mug, not drinking from it. "I came over in 1974, five years after your Lola. Sponsored by my own sister, who'd come before me, the only way most of us managed it back then, one woman pulling the next one over behind her like a rope of paper dolls."

"Did you leave somebody too?"

The question came out of Maya before she'd fully decided to ask it, and she watched something shift behind Aunty Baby's face, not surprise exactly, more like a door she'd expected to stay shut a while longer opening slightly ahead of schedule.

"There was a young man in my own town, yes. Nothing so serious as your Lola's situation, no invitation printed, no real understanding beyond two people who liked each other well enough that everybody assumed something would come of it eventually." Aunty Baby shrugged, the shrug of a woman who had made her peace with something so long ago that revisiting it now felt more like reciting history than confessing anything. "I wrote him twice from here. He wrote back once, very short, wishing me well. That was the end of it, and I was, honestly, a little relieved, because I'd already met my Tommy by then and didn't much want to be writing love letters to a man I'd left behind while falling for another one in front of me."

"Why didn't you ever tell me that."

"Because nobody ever asked me, apo, the way you're asking now." Aunty Baby finally took a sip of the coffee, made a face at its temperature, and set it back down. "Your Lola's story got treated like a secret because there was an invitation attached to it, something official-feeling, something that made it look, from the outside, like a betrayal instead of just an ordinary thing that happens to almost every single one of us who ever left home young enough to still have somebody waiting there. Mine never needed hiding because nobody ever thought to ask if there was anything to hide in the first place. That's the difference. Not that she did something so much worse than the rest of us. Only that hers came with paperwork."

Maya sat with that for a long moment, turning her own untouched coffee cup slowly on its saucer.

"Can I ask what happened to him? Your one."

Aunty Baby waved a hand.

"He married a girl from Passi and had a very ordinary life and died in about 2011, I think, or possibly 2012. Somebody told me at a funeral." She said it without weight. "That is the thing nobody tells you about these stories, apo. Most of them end that way. Not tragedy. Just an ordinary life happening somewhere else, to somebody you used to know."

"That doesn't sound sad when you say it."

"It isn't sad. That's my point." She considered. "It was sad for about a year and a half when I was twenty-four. After that it was simply a fact about a person I no longer knew."

"Do you think she was ashamed of it? My grandmother?"

"I think she was young and scared and very far from home, the same as all of us were, and I think somewhere along the way she decided the shame of the choice was easier to carry alone than the shame of explaining it out loud to anybody, including herself." Aunty Baby reached over and patted Maya's knee once, the same gesture Maya had gotten so used to from Tita Nora that she almost expected it now from every woman of that generation. "You're the first person in this family who's ever actually gone looking for the whole truth of it instead of just accepting the outline everybody agreed to years ago."

# Chapter 41

### The Six Drafts

[S]{.dropcap}he took four days to answer, which she was aware was absurd, given that she had spent three months trying to find this exact person.

The first draft was 900 words. It had the cookbook in it, the margin note, the class photograph, the visa letter, her mother's two-second denial at a kitchen table, and a paragraph near the end that Maya reread on Saturday morning and recognized, with genuine embarrassment, as a plea for absolution addressed to a woman she had never met.

She deleted it.

The second draft opened with *Dear Ms. Aguilar,* which was wrong in a way she could not immediately name and then could: it was the register of a letter about a debt.

The third was four lines long and read like a scam.

The fourth explained too much about Perla and not enough about why Maya was writing. The fifth explained why Maya was writing and made it sound as though something was being asked for, which, she had to admit sitting on her own kitchen floor with the laptop on the coffee table, something was.

The sixth she wrote on Monday night and left overnight without sending, and read again on Tuesday, and found it acceptable, mostly because by then she had exhausted her capacity to keep finding it unacceptable.

*I'm her granddaughter. My grandmother passed away almost five months ago. I found some things of hers that made me think she and your father knew each other a long time ago, before either of them married anyone else. I don't want to cause any trouble for you or your family. I just wanted to ask, if you're willing, whether we could talk a little.*

Eleven times she read it before sending.

She was scanning for one specific thing, and she knew what it was, because she had watched herself do it in the earlier drafts: any sentence that made this stranger responsible for something. Any phrasing that arrived on a woman's doorstep six thousand miles away carrying Perla Bautista's fifty-five-year-old silence and setting it down in somebody else's kitchen to be dealt with.

She could not make the message sound like nothing. There was no version of this that was nothing.

What she could do was make sure that nothing in it asked to be forgiven.

She hit send at nine-forty on a Tuesday night.

Then she stood in her kitchen with the phone in her hand and understood immediately that she was about to check it every four minutes for the rest of the night, and that this was going to be intolerable, and that it was, additionally, none of Fely Aguilar's concern how badly Maya wanted to be answered quickly.

She opened the drawer by the sink. She put the phone in it. She closed the drawer.

Then she went to bed, at nine-fifty, on a Tuesday, like a woman with nothing else available to her, and did not get the phone back out.

She got up twice. Once for water, legitimately, and once for no reason at all, at about half past one, and stood in her own kitchen in the dark within arm's reach of the drawer and did not open it, and went back to bed.

Which was, she thought, lying in the dark and staring at the ceiling and absolutely not sleeping, the first genuinely patient thing she had done in two months.

In the morning she checked it before her feet were on the floor, which she had known she would, and there was nothing, and the day proceeded.

# Chapter 42

### Sent

[M]{.dropcap}arcus called the following evening and there was no softness in his voice at all.

Not unkindness. Something more tiring than that: a man who had used up softness as a strategy and had nothing further to spend. Neither of them had said anything about the Tuesday. It had been two weeks and it was still in the room, and it was in his voice now whether or not either of them intended to name it.

"I need an actual answer."

"I know."

"Not two more weeks. Not I'm still thinking about it." She could hear him working to keep it level, and mostly succeeding. "I told them Friday that I'd have something real. I meant it when I said it. I need you to mean it too, and I want to be extremely clear that I mean *whatever the answer is.* If it's no, tell me no. I'd rather have a no on Friday than a maybe in March."

Maya stood at her kitchen window.

The fog was doing what it did at that hour, coming up off the water and along the street in the slow way that never looked like movement if you watched it directly, only if you looked away and back.

For the first time in seven weeks she did not reach for an extension.

She noticed the not-reaching. It was almost physical, the absence of a habit, like putting your foot down on a step that turns out not to be there.

"Then let me give you a date," she said. "Not an extension. A date I pick and keep."

There was a pause on the line while he looked at something.

"Their actual cutoff is the thirtieth," Marcus said. "The Monday. I've been sitting on that number since Tuesday and not saying it to you, which I am now aware is the exact thing I promised in September I wasn't going to do."

"The thirtieth."

"Same number it was in September." A short breath that was almost a laugh and had very little in it. "Two months, and it is the same number, and I have watched you walk up to it twice." He exhaled, audibly, and she heard something come out of his shoulders on the word. "Okay. A date you pick."

"The Friday before," Maya said. "In person. I want to say it to your face and not on the phone at nine at night."

"The Friday before," he agreed.

"Marcus."

"Yeah."

"Thank you for telling me the date back in September. I don't think I said that at the time."

"You didn't," he said. "But I noticed you'd written it down."

She hung up and stood there a while longer.

On her phone, in a thread on the far side of an ocean, a message sat unanswered since the night before.

She thought about her grandmother at twenty-three. The invitation already printed and sitting somewhere in Miagao with a blank line where the date would go, waiting only on a woman coming home to fill it in. A two-year clock running down in San Francisco while she worked a floor two nurses short and told herself, every single week without fail, that she would deal with it properly soon.

Nobody had ever told Perla Villanueva the date out loud.

Maya kept coming back to that, standing at the window. There had been no phone call. No man saying *I need a real answer, and I mean whatever the answer is.* Only a piece of government paper with a stamped date on it, mailed to a hospital, and six thousand miles of ocean, and a silence that got easier to maintain every single week that it went unbroken.

Somewhere on the other side of that same ocean, a woman named Fely was deciding whether to answer a stranger.

On this side of it, Maya went and got the planner off the counter, unzipped the inside pocket, and took the index card out for the first time since August.

Her own handwriting, careful and slightly cramped, copied letter by letter three times before she got it clean.

She had been carrying it to work every day for fourteen weeks, eight inches from a deadline she had extended twice, and had not once taken it out and looked at the two of them together.

She put the card on the counter. Then she unclipped the laminated calendar page and put that beside it, and stood looking down at the two pieces of paper in her own kitchen with the fog against the window, at a quarter past nine on a Wednesday, for a considerable length of time.

# Chapter 43

### The Waiting

[S]{.dropcap}ix days is not a long time. It is one work week and one weekend. Maya had waited longer than that for lab results, for a transmission, for a security deposit from a landlord in Oakland who eventually mailed it in nickels, or so the story went by the time she told it.

It was, she discovered, an extraordinarily long time to wait for a stranger on another continent to decide whether your grandmother deserved an answer.

**Wednesday** she was fine. She had sent it Tuesday night and she was fine all Wednesday, riding a kind of tailwind, the satisfaction of a thing finally done. She taught a good lesson on subtraction with regrouping. She graded during her prep instead of looking at her phone.

**Thursday** she started calculating the time difference, which is sixteen hours, and which she recalculated four separate times that day because she kept losing confidence in the arithmetic. Sixteen hours means when you are eating lunch it is the middle of the night there. It means there is no point checking at lunch. She checked at lunch.

**Friday** she convinced herself the message had come across as demanding. She reread it a dozen times over the course of the day looking for the demanding part and could not locate it, which she found genuinely unsettling, because she was certain it was in there somewhere.

**Saturday** she reversed entirely and became convinced it had read as a scam. This felt, for about six hours, like an airtight case: a stranger, no mutual connections, a story about an old photograph, a request to talk. She had personally been messaged by a man in a Facebook group inventing a dead uncle. Of course. Of course a sensible woman would look at that and delete it.

She did not check her phone on Saturday night, which she counted as an achievement until Sunday morning when she woke at six and checked it before she was fully upright.

**Sunday** she drafted a follow-up.

Three versions. The first apologized for the original message. The second apologized for the original message and explained, at length, that she was not a scam, which she recognized upon rereading as the single most scam-like paragraph she had ever composed. The third was short and warm and said, essentially, *no pressure, take your time,* which was the worst of the three because it was a lie: it was pressure wearing a nice coat, and both parties would have known it.

She showed all three to Roz at the copier on **Monday** morning.

Roz read them standing up, in about forty seconds, with a stack of warm paper against her hip.

"Absolutely not."

"Which one?"

"All of them. Any of them. No." She handed the phone back. "You've said your piece. It was a good piece. Sending a second one before she's answered the first makes it about your comfort instead of her decision."

Maya opened her mouth.

"Her decision," Roz repeated, and went to teach fourth grade.

That sentence got her through Monday and Tuesday.

And here she was, six days into a stranger's deliberation, composing an apology whose actual function was to make a woman in Iloilo respond faster than she was ready to.

So she left it alone.

It was one of the harder things she did that entire year, and it consisted, start to finish, of not doing anything at all.

# Chapter 44

### The Photograph She Didn't Send

[O]{.dropcap}n the fourth night of waiting she took the photograph out of the folder and put it on the kitchen table under the good light.

She had thought about attaching a proper scan of it to the message, the real thing rather than the small phone picture she'd put in the group. She had gotten as far as selecting the file twice, and had removed it both times without being able to say why, and had put that down at the time to nerves.

It was not nerves. She worked that out on the fourth night.

She looked at him properly, which she had not done since the garage. A young man in front of a church, one hand up against the sun, caught mid-laugh at whoever was holding the camera.

The church behind him was the thing she kept coming back to. Even out of focus, even in a photograph the size of a playing card, you could see that every visible surface of it was carved. Stone worked over completely, edge to edge, the way you'd decorate something small, except this was the front of a building.

She looked it up, eventually, which took a few minutes and produced a name and a date and the fact that it was on a list kept by an international body, and that people traveled to see it.

The photograph changed for her then.

It was not an artifact anymore. It was a Tuesday, or a Sunday, in an actual town where an actual building stood that people still traveled to look at, and a young man had squinted into an actual sun, and somebody he trusted had made him laugh at the exact moment the shutter went.

Her grandmother had been standing on the other side of that camera. Almost certainly. That was not a fact she could prove and she was not going to be able to prove it.

She turned it over. The blank white back, which she had now looked at probably thirty times.

Here was the thing she had not let herself think all the way through in two months of carrying it around:

It was not hers.

It had lived in her grandmother's papers for fifty-five years, behind a class photograph, unlabeled, unmentioned, in a box, in a garage, in a house Perla did not live in. A woman who labeled everything had gone to sustained, deliberate, decades-long effort to keep this one object from having anything written on it at all.

And Maya had been four days from emailing it to the man's daughter.

She sat with what that would actually have been. A stranger appears in your inbox from six thousand miles away, and attached to the message is a photograph of your dead father as a young man, taken by a woman you never met, which you have only ever seen as a small, compressed picture in a public group, and which now arrives whole, without warning, on an ordinary Tuesday while you are making dinner.

*Look what I have of him.*

There was a difference, she was beginning to understand, between what she wanted to know and what she had any right to put in somebody else's hands. She had been sloppy about that distinction for three months. She had been sloppy about it with her mother in a kitchen and sloppier about it in a locked thrift shop and had been, for four days now, on the verge of being sloppy about it with a woman on the other side of the world who owed her nothing whatsoever.

She looked up the church again that night, and then a third time, and read the same three paragraphs about its construction on each occasion, which she knew at the time was not research.

She put the photograph back in the folder.

If Fely asked, she would send it in a heartbeat, scanned at the highest resolution she could manage.

And if Fely never asked, then it would stay exactly where Perla Bautista had put it, which was, in the end, precisely where Perla Bautista had wanted it to stay.

She turned the kitchen light off at eleven and left the folder on the table, and did not check her phone, and slept badly.

# Chapter 45

### Meanwhile, Ordinary Life

[T]{.dropcap}he world, unhelpfully, went right on happening throughout.

**Tuesday** the check-engine light came on halfway down Mission and stayed on, and Maya spent an entire evening on the internet establishing that it was either a loose gas cap or catastrophic and expensive, with no available middle ground.

It was the gas cap. The mechanic, a man of about sixty who introduced himself as Ruben, delivered this diagnosis with such visible delight at being able to charge her nothing that she tipped him twenty dollars out of pure relief.

"You don't have to do that."

"I know."

"I didn't do anything. I tightened a cap. I'm going to be honest with you, I turned it about a quarter turn."

"Take the twenty dollars, Ruben."

**Wednesday** at four in the morning, the smoke alarm in the apartment below hers began going off at irregular intervals for no reason anybody was ever able to establish. It continued for ninety minutes. At five-fifteen she heard her downstairs neighbor, a soft-spoken man in his thirties whom she had never once heard raise his voice, addressing the device directly, at volume, in what she was fairly sure was Portuguese.

**Thursday evening** Marcus called with the fellowship logistics packet open in front of him, working through it aloud in an increasingly alarmed voice.

"There's a section called 'What To Keep In Your Trunk.'"

"Okay."

"It has eleven items on it, Maya. Eleven items. This is a document produced by a university for incoming researchers and one of the items is a bag of cat litter and I do not understand why."

"Traction. If you get stuck on ice, you pour it under the tires."

There was a pause.

"How do you know that?"

Maya opened her mouth and then heard, about a half-second too late, what she was about to admit.

"I looked it up," she said.

"When?"

"Three weeks ago."

The silence on the line went a very particular shape.

"You looked up Chicago winter driving three weeks ago."

"I've been reading about Chicago since October," Maya said, and started laughing, caught, out of any available exit. "Winters. Neighborhoods. The public school hiring calendar, which is a nightmare, by the way. I didn't want to say anything until I'd decided, because I didn't want you to hear it as a yes when it wasn't one yet."

"Maya."

"Don't make it a whole thing."

"It is absolutely a whole thing. That is the best news I have had in six weeks and you have been sitting on it like a state secret." He was laughing now, genuinely delighted, and she was laughing too. "The hiring calendar. You looked up the *hiring calendar.*"

"It's a nightmare. It's all in February."

"I'm going to think about this all week."

"Please don't."

"I'm going to think about this constantly and at length."

For about four minutes on an entirely ordinary Thursday evening, a message that had not arrived from Iloilo simply stopped being the largest object in Maya's life.

Nobody warns you about that part of waiting. It doesn't suspend anything. There is no held breath, no hush, nothing goes still. Life goes right on being funny and irritating and full of gas caps and smoke alarms and a man in another time zone laughing at you about a hiring calendar.

And the enormous thing sits underneath all of it, patient, not going anywhere at all, waiting its turn.

On the Friday she took her class to the auditorium for an assembly about fire safety, and a man in full turnout gear let twenty-six seven-year-olds try on his helmet one at a time, and Dario asked him whether a fire could happen inside a fish tank.

The fireman considered this for considerably longer than Maya expected and then said, seriously, that he had never seen it but that he would not rule it out, which Dario received as the most satisfying answer of his academic career to date.

# Interlude: San Francisco, 1971 {.unnumbered}

[T]{.dropcap}he two-year mark fell on a Tuesday in March, which Perla knew because she'd circled it in pencil on a calendar taped inside her locker back when it still felt like a date worth planning around rather than a date she'd somehow have to survive. Three nurses on her floor had left in the last month alone, one married, two simply gone home partway through their own contracts, and the supervisor had stopped even pretending there was adequate coverage, posting the same three names on every schedule and hoping nobody noticed how thin it had gotten.

She meant to ask that week. She had the request half drafted in her head the whole shift, the words she'd use to tell her supervisor she needed to formally begin the paperwork to return home, and then a patient in room 412 coded twice in one night, and the following morning two more nurses called in sick with the flu going around the dormitory, and the asking simply didn't happen, the way an intention can fail to become an action not through any single decision but through the ordinary, grinding pressure of a job that needed her exactly where she was.

Ernesto was the one who found her crying in the stairwell that same week, not over Ramon exactly, or not only over Ramon, but over the sheer impossible math of it, the sense that she was somehow failing at the one thing she'd promised two separate countries she would manage. He was there visiting his own mother, two floors up, and had gotten lost looking for a vending machine, and instead of pretending not to notice a nurse crying on the stairs, the way most people might have politely done, he sat down on the step below her and asked, plainly, if she wanted company or wanted to be left alone, and meant it either way.

"Company," she said, surprising herself.

He didn't ask her anything else that first time, didn't push for an explanation, just sat with her until she'd stopped, and then walked her back to her floor and said, at the door, that he hoped her day got easier, in a tone so uncomplicated and undemanding that she found herself, against every intention she'd carried onto that stairwell, hoping to see him again.

She did see him again, twice more that month, both times by his mother's bedside on the floor above, and each time he asked nothing of her except whatever small kindness the moment allowed, and each time she went back to her own floor afterward feeling something she recognized, with a small private guilt she didn't examine too closely, as relief.

The formal deadline came and went in March without a single form filed, without a single conversation with her supervisor, without a letter to Ramon explaining any of it, because there was, she found, no version of that letter she could make herself write. She told herself, that whole spring, that she would write it next week. Then the week after. She was still telling herself that, quietly, the day Ernesto's mother was finally discharged and he asked, a little awkwardly, whether Perla might like to get coffee sometime, now that there was no longer a hospital stairwell requiring either of them to be there.

She said yes before she'd fully decided to, the exact same way, she would think much later, that she had let an entire deadline pass without ever once deciding, out loud, to let it.

The coffee was at a place on Geary that closed in 1988. She walked past the building perhaps four hundred times over the following decades, on the way to other things, and it was a shoe shop and then an office and then empty and then a phone shop, and she never mentioned to anybody what it had been.

# Interlude: Ernesto {.unnumbered}

[H]{.dropcap}e had grown up two blocks from the hospital where his mother eventually spent three weeks recovering from a hip replacement, the son of a longshoreman who'd come over from Cebu in the 1920s and a mother born in Stockton to parents who'd arrived even earlier, which made Ernesto Bautista, by the standards of most people he met, thoroughly, almost boringly American, a fact he found privately funny given how often strangers still asked him where he was really from.

He was not, by his own frequent admission, a romantic man. He worked the docks himself by then, following his father into it the way sons in that neighborhood did, and had dated two women seriously before Perla, both of it ending in the same quiet mutual recognition that the fit was not quite right.

He knew, from nearly the first week of coffee, that something in Perla ran deeper and more complicated than she was offering up on the surface. He wasn't a fool, whatever his own mother liked to tease him about being slow to notice things. He noticed the specific way she sometimes went quiet mid-sentence, the way certain ordinary questions about home visibly cost her something to answer, the particular practiced brightness she brought out whenever a conversation drifted anywhere near the subject of what she'd left behind. "There was someone," she told him, the night he proposed, sitting across a diner table with coffee gone cold in both their cups. "Back home. It didn't end the way it should have. I let it end by not ending it, if that makes any sense to you."

"It makes sense," he said, and did not ask a single follow-up question, then or in the following forty-three years, and it cost him something, in the quieter hours, that he never found a way to say to her and would not have said if he had.

He thought about it, though, over the years. That is worth saying, because a man who never asks is generally assumed never to have wondered.

He wondered for about a decade, on and off, mostly at odd hours. Then gradually less. By his sixties, not at all, at which point the not-asking had stopped being a discipline and had become simply the shape of the marriage.

He asked her twice, in fact. The first time was three months earlier and she said she was not ready and he said all right and did not mention it again, and did not sulk about it, which she noticed and weighed more heavily than the proposal itself.

They married within the year, a modest ceremony at St. Augustine's with maybe forty guests, and settled into the kind of life Ernesto had always assumed he wanted: a small apartment on Guttenberg Street that became, eventually, a bigger one in Daly City, a daughter born three years in whom he adored without reservation, Sunday dinners and summer barbecues and four decades of showing up, reliably, for every single ordinary thing a marriage actually required, which he had come to believe, somewhere in his forties, mattered considerably more than any grand romantic gesture ever could.

In the last decade of his life he developed an interest in the radio and would sit in the garage on Saturdays with a set he was perpetually repairing and never finished, and Perla would bring him coffee out there without being asked and go back inside without saying anything, twice every Saturday, for ten years.

He died in his sleep at seventy-eight, eleven years before Perla, of nothing dramatic, simply a heart that had done its considerable work for as long as it reasonably could and then, quietly, one ordinary Tuesday night, stopped. Perla found him in the morning, still warm, his face entirely at peace, and she would tell Liza, in the weeks after, that it was the mercy of the thing that undid her most: that he'd gone easily, without pain, without the long, grinding decline she'd watched other husbands endure on her own hospital floors for decades. It seemed, she said, exactly like him. Steady, even in the manner of his own leaving.

She did not cry at his funeral either. Her daughter noticed. Half the parish noticed.

What she did instead was cook for eleven days straight, for anybody who came to the house and several people who did not, until Liza took a pan out of her hands on the twelfth day and made her sit down at her own table and be fed by somebody else, which took a period of negotiation.

She kept his side of the closet exactly as he'd left it for nearly two years afterward, unable to bring herself to clear it out, and only did so, finally, the same practical way she did most difficult things, on an ordinary Saturday afternoon with Liza's help and very little discussion, the two of them working in a silence that held more grief inside it than either woman said out loud.

# Interlude: Raising Liza {.unnumbered}

[M]{.dropcap}otherhood arrived for Perla the way most large things in her life seemed to, without much ceremony and with an enormous amount of ordinary, unglamorous work attached to it: three a.m. feedings in a small apartment that still smelled faintly of someone else's cooking through the thin walls, a colicky infant who screamed through most of her first four months in a way that left Perla, more than once, sitting on the bathroom floor at four in the morning simply because it was the only room in the apartment where she could cry without waking anyone, Ernesto included, who worked mornings at the docks and needed his sleep in a way she'd decided, without ever discussing it with him directly, was not worth disturbing over her own exhaustion.

She was, by every account Liza herself would later give, a good mother in the practical register Perla brought to everything: meals always on time, homework always checked, a household run with the same brisk competence she'd once brought to a hospital floor, and rather less given to the softer registers of mothering, the long emotional conversations, the open displays of worry or tenderness that some of Liza's friends' mothers seemed to manage more easily. Perla showed love the way she'd learned to survive: through competence, through showing up, through making sure a child was fed and safe and capable, rather than through words she'd never quite trusted to carry what she actually meant.

She did not talk about Miagao often, in those years, and when she did it emerged in fragments rather than stories: a description of a particular fruit that didn't grow anywhere near California, a brief mention of a fiesta tradition, gone again before Liza, as a child, thought to ask a follow-up question. It was only much later, as an adult herself, that Liza would understand these fragments as the visible tip of an entire submerged continent her mother had decided, long before Liza was born, not to make anyone else find her way through alongside her.

She saved newspaper clippings of Liza's school achievements. She learned to make an American dish Liza had mentioned once, at a friend's house, and never mentioned again.

Liza's own wedding, in 1998, is a thing about which Perla said almost nothing at the time and rather more, in fragments, over the following thirty years.

She cried at it, which everybody expected and nobody thought about further.

She also, according to a cousin's photograph that surfaced decades later, spent a substantial portion of the reception standing at the edge of the room by herself with a glass of something, watching, in a manner that reads in the photograph as content and reads, if you know what was in a box at her house, as something considerably harder to name.

She was fifty-one when Liza became a mother, and she spent whole weekends at her daughter's house in those first months, not offering advice, holding an infant Maya for hours at a stretch while Liza slept, humming in Hiligaynon. Maya has no memory of any of it and recognized the sound anyway, twenty-seven years later, at a kitchen table in Daly City, without being able to say why.

"You're better at this than I was," Liza told her once, exhausted, grateful, watching her mother settle a colicky infant Maya with an ease Liza herself couldn't manage.

"I had considerably more practice by then," Perla said, which was true, and also, though Liza wouldn't understand this for nearly three decades, not the entire truth of it at all.

# Interlude: Forty Years on a Hospital Floor {.unnumbered}

[S]{.dropcap}he worked the same San Francisco hospital for eleven years before transferring to a smaller one closer to Daly City once Liza started school, and stayed at that second hospital for the remaining twenty-nine years of her career, long enough to train nurses who went on to train their own nurses, long enough to be, by the time she finally retired at sixty-one, the single most senior person on her entire floor, consulted by doctors half her age on patients whose charts had stumped everyone younger.

She was known, across four decades, for a specific reputation that outlasted every individual patient and every rotating cast of doctors: brisk, precise, unsentimental in the moment of actual care, and quietly, fiercely protective of her patients in ways that occasionally put her at odds with hospital administration. She'd been formally reprimanded twice in her career, both times for arguing too forcefully on a patient's behalf against a doctor's initial judgment, both times later proven right, a fact she mentioned exactly once at each of her own retirement parties, dryly, to general laughter from colleagues who'd worked under her for decades and understood the specific pride buried inside the joke.

She loved the work in a way she rarely articulated directly, the same reticence that governed most of her deepest feelings, but it showed in the small things: the way she never once complained about a double shift, the way she kept, until the day she retired, a small notebook of patients she still thought about years later, their names and a single line about how each of their stories had ended, a private ledger nobody else in the family knew existed.

"Nursing back home meant something different than nursing here," she told a young colleague once, decades before Maya was born, in a conversation the colleague would recount at Perla's own retirement party as one of the only times anyone had heard her speak plainly about the actual weight of having left. "Back home, it was going to be my whole future, everything I'd worked for, wrapped up in one place. Here, it became something else. Not smaller. Just different. A job I got to be excellent at, instead of a whole identity I had to carry alone." She'd paused, then, according to the colleague, and added, almost to herself: "Maybe that was easier, in its way. A job you can put down at the end of a shift. A whole identity, you can't."

She kept working for eleven months past the date she had originally given, which nobody was surprised by and which the floor manager had privately predicted to within two weeks.

The last shift she worked, in 2009, she stayed forty minutes past the end of it with a patient whose family had not arrived, and clocked out without recording the time, and drove home, and did not mention any of it at the party the following week.

They gave her a plaque. It went into a drawer in the spare room within about two weeks and was found there in August, face down under a stack of tax documents, by a granddaughter who did not know what it was for and photographed it and put it in the KEEP box.

Nobody at that retirement party understood, at the time, what she'd been describing.

Two of the nurses she trained in the eighties came to her funeral. Neither spoke to the family. Both signed the book, and one of them wrote, under her name, in the space intended for a relationship to the deceased, the single word *taught.*

Liza found that entry eight months later, going through the guest book properly for the first time, and did not recognize the name, and sat at her kitchen table for some minutes with a book open in front of her, looking at one word written by a stranger about her own mother.

# Chapter 46

### Not Knowing

[F]{.dropcap}riday night, Marcus's apartment, day three of the waiting.

She was heating up leftovers because neither of them had the energy to cook, and the microwave was doing its countdown behind her, and she was talking mostly to fill the room.

"I keep thinking about what I'd actually do. If I were her." She was watching the numbers go down rather than looking at him. "If I were twenty-three, that far from everything, and the two years just kept running out on me, and I..."

She stopped.

The rest of the sentence was right there. She could feel the exact shape of it sitting behind her teeth, fully formed, four or five words long.

She could not make herself say it.

"And you what," Marcus said.

He said it gently. He did not push at all. He simply left the space open, which is what he had been doing for weeks without complaint and which was, she was beginning to understand, harder to do than pushing.

The microwave beeped.

"Nothing." She opened the door and took the container out and burned two fingers on it, which at least gave her a reason to make a noise. "I don't know. I lost the thread."

He let it go.

She noticed him letting it go, and noticed that she was grateful, and noticed, somewhere underneath both of those, a third thing she liked considerably less: that she had counted on him letting it go. Had said the sentence at all *because* she knew he would.

Neither of them mentioned that this was the third time in ten days she had started a sentence about her grandmother and abandoned it at the precise point where it stopped being about her grandmother.

They ate. They watched most of something. It was fine, and ordinary, and she was glad to be there.

Later, in bed with the lights already off and the radiator doing its irregular work, he shifted beside her, and she knew from the quality of the shift that he was still awake.

"You know you can tell me whatever it is you keep almost telling me," he said, into the dark. "I'm not going anywhere. Whatever it is."

"I know."

And she did know it, entirely, which was the trouble. It would have been so much easier if she had doubted him even slightly. A person can justify keeping something from someone who might leave. There is no available justification for keeping it from a man lying in the dark beside you saying, unprompted, at eleven at night, *I'm not going anywhere.*

She got as far as opening her mouth.

Then Marcus's breathing changed, the way it did, all at once, the man having never in five years taken longer than ninety seconds to fall asleep, and the moment closed itself without anybody having to close it.

Maya lay awake for another hour.

She would have to say it out loud eventually, to his face, in daylight, with him fully conscious and looking at her.

Not tonight, though. It was the deal she made with herself in the dark, and she recognized the shape of the deal even as she was making it, and made it anyway.

He got up at some point in the night and she heard him in the kitchen, and heard the tap, and heard him stand there for a while not doing anything, and did not call out to him, and he came back to bed and neither of them said anything about it in the morning.

# Chapter 47

### Priya

[S]{.dropcap}he called Priya the next afternoon.

They had been friends since a dorm hallway in 2017 and had been through most things at a distance since: Priya three states away, an annual visit that had lapsed to biennial, a group text that neither of them had contributed to in months. But Priya had the usefulness of a person who loved Maya without being tangled in a single one of the family's actual stakes. She had met Liza twice. She had never met Perla at all.

"You've been weird for two months."

"Hello to you too."

"I'm serious. Two months of one-word answers and a very consistent 'busy with family stuff.' I've been waiting for you to either tell me what's actually going on or admit you weren't going to." A pause. "Which call is this?"

"Both, probably."

Maya laughed, a little helplessly, and then told her the whole thing.

It took forty minutes. It was the first time she had told it start to finish to anybody outside the family, and she found that it came out differently in that form: not as a series of discoveries but as one continuous shape, the cookbook and the yearbook and the garage and the invitation folded inside the visa letter, and the fellowship sitting underneath all of it the entire time like a second and quieter emergency.

Priya let her get all the way to the end without interrupting once.

"Okay," she said. "Two things."

"Go on."

"The first thing is that's an incredible story and I'm sorry, and I mean both of those, and if you want I'll say more about that part."

"And the second thing?"

"The second thing is you know you just described your own pattern back to me for forty minutes and never said so."

Maya did not say anything.

"Jake," Priya said. "Portland. He asked, and you took four months, and by the end of the four months it wasn't a decision anymore, it was just a slow no you never had to say out loud."

"I know."

"Do you? Because I've heard you tell the Jake story maybe six times and it always comes out as timing. Never once as a thing you did."

"I know," Maya said again. "I've been sitting with it for weeks."

"Sitting with it." Priya's voice went flat in the way it had gone flat since roughly the first week of freshman year whenever she was about to say the useful thing. "That's a nice way to spend a month while a man waits for an answer."

That landed hard enough that Maya sat down.

"So what are you going to do?" Priya said. "Not your grandmother. Not what she should have done in 1971. What are *you* doing?"

Maya looked out her own kitchen window at the fog coming up the hill, and found, to her considerable surprise, that the answer was already there, entire and fully formed, and had been for some time, and had only been waiting for somebody to ask the question in a way she couldn't get around.

"I'm going to say yes," she said. "Out loud. Before he has to ask me twice."

"Okay." She could hear Priya's whole voice change. "Okay, good."

"You could sound less relieved."

"I could not sound less relieved. I've liked Marcus since the barbecue, and I say that as somebody who did not like the previous one and told you so once and then never again." A pause, and something quieter came into it. "He's the first one who never made you make yourself smaller. You do that. You've done it since school. You did it with Jake for two and a half years and I never said anything, which I regret."

"When did you get so wise about all this?"

"I got divorced two years ago, Maya, wisdom was compulsory." She laughed, and there was very little bitterness left in it, only the specific clarity of somebody who has survived a thing and can now discuss the parts of it that were her own fault. "Go say your yes. And send me a picture of actual snow, because I don't trust anybody's description of weather that doesn't happen here."

# Chapter 48

### The Pig, the Land, and the Slap

[R]{.dropcap}oz got her own answer the following week and arrived at the copier on Monday with the energy of a woman who has waited fifteen years for a payoff and received one without warning.

"I know about the pig."

"You know about the pig."

"I know everything about the pig. Sit down."

"I'm standing at a copier."

"Then stand there and don't interrupt me," Roz said, and told it.

Her father's mother was ninety-one. She had refused this subject for four decades with a consistency that had, over time, become its own family joke. And then, at her own ninety-first birthday lunch, apparently for no reason at all, with a dozen people at the table and a plate in front of her, she had simply started talking.

The pig belonged to her own younger brother.

In 1974 it got out, as pigs do, and went onto the neighbors' land, and rooted up a substantial portion of a newly planted garden.

That part, Roz said, is undisputed. Every version agrees on the pig and the garden.

What happened next is that the brother, instead of paying for the damage, which would have cost him very little and ended it, accused the neighbors of having deliberately lured the animal over to manufacture a grievance against his family.

"Why would he do that?"

"Because he was twenty-four and stupid and it was in front of people," Roz said. "That's the whole reason. That's always the whole reason."

The accusation was, in the social arithmetic of a small barangay, close to unforgivable. It said the neighbors were liars, and it said it publicly, and the thing about saying something publicly is that afterward it cannot be quietly withdrawn.

The feud ran seven years.

It ended, or rather peaked, at a christening in 1981, where somebody from the other family said something to Roz's great-aunt about the pig, or the land, or possibly both, and Roz's great-aunt slapped her across the face in front of the entire assembled parish.

"In front of the *priest*," Roz said.

"Is the priest confirmed?"

"The priest is not confirmed and I've stopped needing him to be."

Maya laughed. Roz did not, immediately.

"Here's the thing, though." She was stacking her spelling tests now, squaring them against the machine, not looking up. "I asked her why nobody would ever tell me. Fifteen years I've been asking, at funerals, at weddings, and every single time somebody put food in front of me instead."

"What did she say?"

"She said it was never really about the pig."

Roz set the stack down.

"It was about my great-aunt losing her temper where everybody could see it. In a place, in that generation, where that particular kind of thing followed a woman for the rest of her life. She was thirty-four. She lived to seventy-four and she never once got out from under it." Roz shook her head. "So the family agreed on the pig. The pig is the version you can tell. The pig is funny. Nobody has to say the other sentence out loud, which is that a woman got pushed past what she could hold, on a Sunday, in front of everyone she knew."

Maya did not say anything for a moment.

"You've gone quiet," Roz said.

"I'm thinking about my grandmother."

"Yeah." Roz picked the tests back up. "I thought you might."

Because it was the same fuel. That was the thing Maya turned over for the rest of that day and several days after. Not the same event, nothing like the same event: an invitation and a visa letter instead of a slap at a christening, a silence instead of a scene.

But underneath both of them, the identical unbearable thing. Being watched at the exact moment you stop managing to hold it all together.

"Do you wish you hadn't asked?"

"No." Roz did not hesitate. "I wish somebody had told me at about nineteen, when I could have asked my great-aunt directly instead of having to receive it fourth-hand at ninety-one removes. She was alive until I was twenty-six, Maya. I could have sat in a room with her."

"Would she have told you?"

"Absolutely not." A short laugh. "But I could have sat in the room."

"Small towns don't forget anything," Roz said, heading for the door with her stack. "They just agree on which parts everybody's allowed to keep saying out loud."

She had no idea she was finishing a sentence a seventy-eight-year-old woman had started for Maya in a locked thrift shop two months earlier.

# Chapter 49

### Fely Writes Back

[T]{.dropcap}he reply came on a Tuesday afternoon during recess duty, while Maya stood at the edge of the blacktop half watching a chaotic game of four square and half watching her phone in her jacket pocket, and when it finally buzzed she very nearly didn't get to it in time, one hand already raised to blow her whistle at a kid climbing determinedly up the wrong side of the slide.

*Sorry it took me a few days. I talked to my mother first, I hope that's alright. She's really the only one who knew the details, my father never talked about it much himself, but he apparently told her early on, before they married, he wanted her to know everything up front, that's just who he was, she says.*

*He did know a nurse before he ever met my mother. A girl from a good family in town who'd gone off to America for work. He told my mother there had been an understanding between them, a wedding half planned even, and that it simply didn't end up happening, the girl never came back home, and after a while he stopped waiting for her and my mother came into his life not too long after that, and he never once, not one single time in their whole marriage, made her feel like she'd been some kind of second choice.*

*My mother says he used to tell her, some things just work out different than you'd planned, and you can spend your whole life being angry about that fact, or you can go build something else entirely instead. He married my mother in 1974. They were married thirty-seven years, until he passed. He had the three of us, six grandchildren after that. He taught at the elementary school in town for more than forty years, everybody in Miagao still remembers him, the mayor himself came to his funeral. He was, by every single account I've ever heard including my own memory of him, a happy man.*

*I don't say all that to make you feel better necessarily. I say it because it happens to be true, and I had a feeling you probably needed to hear that it was true.*

*I don't know if that's what you were hoping to hear. I hope it helps you anyway.*

She read it four times standing up.

The first time she got about halfway and had to start again, because her eyes had gone ahead of her comprehension and picked up *thirty-seven years* out of order, before the rest of it.

The second time she read it properly.

The third time she read only the last two lines.

*I don't say all that to make you feel better necessarily. I say it because it happens to be true, and I had a feeling you probably needed to hear that it was true.*

A woman she had never met, six thousand miles away, whose father had been left waiting by Maya's grandmother, had gone to her own mother, asked, listened, written it out carefully over what was plainly more than one sitting, and then added two sentences at the end whose only purpose was to be kind to a stranger.

Maya stood at the edge of the blacktop with the whistle hanging loose around her neck.

Recess went on. Somebody's ball went over the fence and there was a negotiation about it. Two girls came past holding hands and neither of them looked up. The fog was doing its usual slow burn-off at the far end of the yard.

She cried without making any sound about it at all, which is a specific and learnable skill, and which she had in fact learned in this exact profession, in staff bathrooms and parked cars, over five years.

Because it was the best and worst possible answer, delivered in the same paragraph, and she had not understood until that moment that those could be the same thing.

Nobody's life had been destroyed. That was the best of it. Her grandmother had not broken a man beyond repair. There was no wreckage. There was a schoolteacher in Miagao who had been loved by his wife for thirty-seven years and had six grandchildren and had been carried out of a church with the mayor in attendance.

And the worst of it was exactly the same fact, from a different angle: that Perla Villanueva had spent fifty-five years keeping something folded down to almost nothing, and had done it for no reason at all. There had been nothing to protect anybody from. The catastrophe she had organized an entire interior life around had simply never occurred.

Two months of not knowing, and the not knowing had cost her more than the truth did.

Maya let the bell ring out its full cycle. Then she wiped her face with the back of her hand, checked it with her phone camera for about a second and a half, and blew her whistle at the kid who was still, throughout all of it, climbing determinedly up the wrong side of the slide.

# Interlude: Fely {.unnumbered}

[S]{.dropcap}he had almost scrolled past the photograph entirely, that first night, one more blurry old picture among dozens posted to a group she checked more out of habit than genuine hope of finding anything, but something about the set of the young man's shoulders had stopped her thumb mid-scroll, a familiar tilt of the head she recognized without being able to say from where at first.

Her mother had confirmed it within the hour, holding the photo up to the kitchen light with the care of a woman handling something considerably more fragile than a phone screen. "That's him," she'd said. "Before I knew him. Before any of us knew him." She'd sat with it a long moment, then added, gently, without any bitterness at all in her voice: "He looks so young there. Younger than I ever got to see him."

Fely had known the broad outline of her father's story for most of her adult life, the nurse who'd gone to America, the engagement that hadn't ended cleanly, all of it delivered by her mother decades ago in the same matter-of-fact tone her mother used for most difficult subjects, love it, own it, and move forward without drama. She had never expected, in all that time, to hear from anyone connected to that old story, and when the granddaughter's message first arrived, her first instinct had been a small, private flare of something she didn't examine too closely, not quite resentment, closer to protectiveness, an old, automatic impulse to guard her father's memory from anything that might complicate the simple, happy story she'd always told herself about him.

Talking to her mother that same evening had settled it. Her mother, almost eighty now and long past any need to protect a marriage that had already proven itself sound for thirty-seven years, had simply laughed, a little sadly, and said that the girl deserved to know the truth, whatever shape it turned out to take, because carrying an unanswered question that size for two months, over a woman who'd never even gotten the chance to ask it directly, sounded like exactly the kind of quiet suffering nobody in their own family needed to cause a stranger.

Fely wrote her reply carefully, over two separate evenings, wanting to get the tone exactly right: honest about what had actually happened, generous about how her father's life had turned out, careful not to either excuse or condemn a woman neither of them had ever met. She thought, writing it, about her own father's steady kindness, the way he'd never let his own old disappointment curdle into bitterness toward anyone, and understood, finishing the message, that whatever she said next belonged, in its own small way, to his memory as much as to Maya's need to hear it.

She never told Maya that she cried a little herself, sending it, not from sadness exactly, but from the complicated fullness of finally getting to say her father's whole story out loud to someone who genuinely needed to hear it, a fullness she suspected, though she never said so directly, that Maya's own grandmother might have understood completely, had anyone ever given her the same chance to say the whole of it plainly, just once, before it was too late.

She read the granddaughter's message about the garlic again, out loud, in a hospital corridor eight months later, waiting on the results of her mother's third scan, and laughed out loud in a place where people do not laugh, and had to explain herself to a nurse.

She did not tell Maya that either.

Her mother died six weeks after that scan. In the last two weeks, when Corazon was mostly sleeping and the family had settled into shifts, Fely sat by the bed in the afternoons and read aloud whatever happened to be on her phone, the way you do.

On one of those afternoons she read out the messages from the American granddaughter.

Her mother, who had not opened her eyes in some hours, said, distinctly: "Too much garlic."

They were the last words Fely ever heard her say. They were about a dish, and they were, as far as Fely was concerned, the correct last words for that particular woman to have chosen.

# Chapter 50

### What Maya Does With It

[T]{.dropcap}here was a night in between, which she spent badly.

She had been given, in a single message, everything she had wanted since August, and the effect was not relief. It was a kind of vertigo: the search had had a shape and the shape had been holding her upright, and now there was nothing in front of her at all.

She understood, lying awake, that she had been enjoying it. Not the grief. The looking. Nearly four months of a problem with a solution somewhere in it, which is a more comfortable object than an ordinary Tuesday with a dead grandmother in it.

She had a list.

She had actually written it down, on the back of a receipt, on the Tuesday afternoon after Fely's message came in, sitting in her car in the school lot with the engine off.

*Photo of him?*

*Did he know she married*

*Did he ever ask about her*

*Was there a letter*

*Did he say her name*

She looked at that list for a long time on Tuesday evening and again on Wednesday morning, and worked out, gradually, what every item on it had in common.

Every single one was a question about whether Maya's grandmother had mattered to a man who died fifteen years ago in a town she had never seen.

Not one of them was a question Fely would enjoy being asked. Several of them, phrased any way at all, amounted to: *please establish for me that my grandmother was important to your father.* One of them, the last one, was worse than that. It was asking a woman to confirm that a dead man had, in some private moment, said another woman's name.

She threw the receipt away on Wednesday afternoon, in the staff room, in the bin under the coffee machine, which she was aware was a slightly ceremonial way to dispose of a receipt and did anyway.

What she wrote instead took four sentences, and she wrote it in about six minutes.

*Thank you for telling me all of that. It helps more than I can explain.*

*I do have one more question, if you don't mind, and it has nothing to do with any of the hard parts.*

*What did he used to cook?*

*My grandmother kept a recipe I think might have been his originally, and I'd love to know whether it's the same one your family still makes.*

She sent it and then went and did something else on purpose, a technique she had acquired over the preceding two months and had become good at: put the phone in another room and give yourself a task with an end point.

She cleaned the inside of the refrigerator. It took thirty-five minutes and it had needed doing since roughly August.

The reply came in under two hours, and it was noticeably longer than anything Fely had written before, and it read as though it had been fun to write.

*He made a torta with far too much garlic. My mother's words, not mine. Way more than any written recipe calls for.*

*He said his mother taught him to make it that way and he wasn't going to change it for anybody. He was extremely annoying about it. He would say it every time, every single time, like it was new information.*

*We still make it that way. My sister makes it for New Year.*

*Funny that you'd ask about that specifically.*

Maya read it twice.

Then she got up, and took the cookbook off the counter, and opened it to the page that had started all of it in August.

The paper had a soft dent in the top corner now, from being opened to this page more than any other over the preceding weeks. A book tells on you like that. Fifty years of a woman's cooking had gone slack in the spine at three places, all of them dishes she made constantly, and here was a fourth that was entirely Maya's doing.

The torta recipe. The ingredients in her grandmother's small forward-leaning hand. And underneath, in the margin, pressed harder into the paper than anything around it, four words and one letter underlined hard enough to nearly go through.

She sat down on the kitchen floor with the book open on her knees, not as a decision, only where she ended up.

She had been reading it wrong since the first day.

She had read it as an elegy. Everybody had. She had carried it to Roz's copier and to a parish hall and to a locked thrift shop and had presented it, every single time, as the private grief of a woman who had lost something, four words of quiet mourning left in a book where nobody would find them.

It was not that.

It was an argument.

Roz had heard it correctly at the copier, months ago, and neither of them had understood what she was hearing. *No more of this one.* Not a lament. A ruling.

It was precisely, exactly the same thing Perla Bautista had written in the margin of every other page of that cookbook for fifty years. *Too much vinegar, next time half.* *Ernesto says needs more garlic. Ernesto is wrong.* *Neighbor says less butter. Neighbor is wrong.* A woman who had never in her life let a recipe have the last word, arguing, in pen, with someone who wasn't in the room.

Except this argument had been interrupted.

Not ended. Interrupted, mid-sentence, by an ocean and a stamped date on a government form and a silence that ran fifty-five years without either party getting to finish the point.

Maya turned back two pages, to the kaldereta, and the third crossed-out sauce ratio with 2011 written beside it.

She had been carrying the wrong date for two months. She had pictured a young woman in a dormitory in San Francisco with something to grieve, because that was the version in which the note was an elegy, and she had never once thought to do the arithmetic.

Fely had said fifteen years.

Which meant that at some point, in some year, the news had crossed six thousand miles and arrived at a kitchen table in Daly City in front of a woman who had asked nobody for it. How, Maya was not going to find out. There was no letter in any box and no name and nobody left to ask, and she understood, sitting on the floor with the book open on her knees, that this was going to be one of the ones she did not get.

The page insisted on the rest of it, though. The hand on this line was not a young woman's hand. It was the hand from the last third of the book, the one Maya had spent months learning to read on sight: slower, and pressed harder, because by then pressing harder had started to be necessary.

So: alone, at a kitchen table, in her sixties, having found out. And she had gone back to the page and written the only thing there was left to write.

*Not this one anymore.*

Not a mourning. A concession, of a sort, from a woman who never conceded anything.

He had been right about the garlic. She had known it for fifty-five years and had never once said so out loud, and she had written it down where nobody could read it, in a language nobody in her house had been taught.

# Chapter 51

### Marcus's Mother

[H]{.dropcap}e called Ohio the same week, on speaker, the two of them sitting on his apartment floor with their backs against the couch because he had already packed one of the chairs.

His mother's name was Deborah. Maya had spoken to her exactly twice before, both times briefly, both times pleasantly, and had formed the impression of a woman who was kind and who would not be managed.

The congratulations came first, at length. Then a pause, and then the actual question, delivered with the particular bluntness available to a woman two thousand miles away on a speakerphone.

"And Maya's coming with you."

It was not phrased as a question. It sat there in the room being not-a-question.

"Ma."

"I'm asking, Marcus."

"You're not asking, you're telling."

"Because I love you, baby, and I have heard this exact story from you before." There was a photograph of Deborah on the wall behind Marcus in every video call: a woman in her late sixties on a porch, squinting, holding a very large dog by the collar with visible effort.

Maya had looked at it a hundred times and had formed, from it, an entirely accurate impression of the person now speaking.

Deborah's voice did not rise at all. "The wonderful opportunity. So excited. And what it turned out to mean was leaving somebody important behind, and you didn't say a word to her about it, and I watched what that cost you for about two years afterward."

Maya sat very still.

Marcus had gone very still beside her on the floor.

She had the answer. That was the thing. She had had it since the phone call with Priya, whole and finished, and it was sitting in her mouth and all she had to do was lean six inches toward a speakerphone and say it to a woman in Ohio.

And she could not do it, because she had not said it to Marcus yet, and there was no version of this in which he found out from his mother's kitchen.

The pause went on about two seconds.

"We're talking about it, Ma," Marcus said.

He said it easily. He covered for her without a flicker, in real time, in front of his own mother.

And then his hand found hers on the floor between them and gripped it, considerably harder than the moment appeared to warrant, hard enough that Maya turned and looked at the side of his face and understood, all at once and for the first time with any clarity, exactly how much of this specific fear had been sitting underneath everything he had said and not said for two months.

He had never told her about Boston. Not properly. She had thought his carefulness was patience.

Some of it was patience. Some of it was a man who had done this badly once and was quietly terrified of discovering he was the kind of person who did it twice.

"Talking about it," Deborah repeated, in a tone conveying precisely what she thought of that.

"Yes."

"Mm."

"Ma."

"I've said my piece. I'm not going to say it again." A rustle, the sound of a woman sitting down somewhere. "He does this, Maya. He gets so far inside the work he stops noticing what he's standing to lose. His father was identical. Brilliant man. Could not see a thing that was directly in front of him."

"I notice things fine," Marcus said, mildly wounded.
