"You noticed a fellowship offer six full weeks before you noticed you had to ask her an actual question, instead of hoping she'd work out what you wanted from the general vicinity of your anxiety."

Maya laughed before she could stop herself.

"Thank you," said Deborah.

"I'm being ganged up on in my own apartment."

"You're being *loved* in your own apartment, there's a difference." Then, more quietly, and directly: "Maya. I don't know what any of this looks like from where you're sitting and I'm not going to ask. I'll only say that whatever you decide, I'd like it to be something you decided. That's all. He'll survive either one. He won't survive being managed around."

"I understand," Maya said, and found her voice had gone strange.

"Right." Brisk again, the conversation concluded by executive decision. "Now. Marcus. What is happening about a winter coat, because what you own is not a coat, it's a windbreaker with ambitions."

After they hung up, Marcus sat quiet for a moment, still holding Maya's hand, working through something.

"She's not wrong. About the last time." He said it carefully, like a door he wasn't sure he wanted to open but had decided, finally, to open anyway. "There was a job in Boston, after college, years before I met you. Somebody I was seeing then didn't want to go, and I didn't ask her properly either, just sort of assumed the work mattered more and let the relationship end quietly instead of actually fighting to keep it or actually asking her to come. I told myself for years it was mutual. It wasn't, really. I just didn't want to be the one who had to hear her say no out loud, so I never gave either of us the chance to say anything at all."

Maya let it sit.

"That's sort of the whole point of every single conversation we've had for two months." He turned to face her fully, something open and slightly frightened in his expression that she didn't often get to see from him. "I didn't want to just assume you'd come, or assume you wouldn't, and build some quiet story afterward about how it was mutual. I wanted an actual yes or an actual no. I still do. Whichever one it ends up being."

Maya sat with that for a moment.

"Friday," she said. "I'm not stalling. I want to say it properly and I want to say it to your face."

"Okay."

"It's not a no."

"I know it's not a no." He almost smiled. "I've known since about ten minutes into my mother's phone call. You went completely still and you didn't say anything, and if it were a no you'd have said it right then to get me out of the room with her."

"That's very annoying."

"I'm a scientist," Marcus said. "I observe things." He picked his coat up off the chair and hung it properly, finally, on the hook. "Noticing is easy. Working out which noticed things to say out loud is the part that takes five years."

# Chapter 52

### What Chicago Actually Looks Like

[W]{.dropcap}ednesday night, two days out from the answer she had promised him, Marcus called with his laptop already open.

"I want to show you something and I want to be clear up front about what it is and isn't."

"Okay."

"It isn't pressure. It's information. You've been deciding about an idea for two months and I think the idea is worse than the actual thing, because the idea is just a word, and the word is *Chicago*, and it's doing a lot of work in your head that it doesn't deserve."

He shared his screen.

The first listing was a one-bedroom in a neighborhood called Logan Square. Exposed brick, which she had opinions about. A fire escape. A street lined with trees that were, in the photograph, bare, and which he informed her turned a color in October that she would not believe.

"They actually do that."

"They do that. I've seen photographs. I'm as suspicious as you are."

"I've lived my whole life somewhere the leaves just stay green and then fall off in a slightly disappointing way."

"Then you're in for a genuinely stupid amount of foliage."

Rent was less than she was currently paying. That took a second to absorb, and then took another second, because she had been carrying a vague assumption that everything would be harder in every direction and this was the first data point suggesting otherwise.

"There's a park two blocks down. And I want to show you one more thing and I want you to know I checked it this week, not in October, I'm not producing an old screenshot to close a deal."

A district job board. A posting, dated four days earlier, for a second grade position at a public school two miles from the apartment.

Maya looked at that one for a while.

"You've been apartment hunting without me."

"I've been apartment hunting *in case,*" Marcus said. "Not instead of asking. Those are different and I want credit for the difference, because I've been extremely disciplined about it and it's been killing me."

He clicked through two more.

He narrated them in a way that gave him entirely away: a kitchen with real counter space, because she complains about counter space; a laundry room in the building rather than the machine down the block she has hated for five years; a two-block stretch of the neighborhood that he said reminded him, faintly and on a smaller scale, of the parts of the Mission she likes.

There was a long stretch in the middle of the call where neither of them said anything much and the screen just sat there showing a kitchen in a listing photograph, empty, staged, with a bowl of lemons on the counter that nobody had ever eaten.

He had a spreadsheet. Of course he had a spreadsheet.

"Please don't show me a spreadsheet."

"It's a small spreadsheet."

"Marcus."

"It's four columns and it took me twenty minutes and it answers three of the questions you've asked me twice, so you're going to look at it and then thank me," he said, and shared it, and she looked at it, and it did, and she did.

They talked for over an hour.

Not about whether. About the unglamorous specifics, for the first time: the credential transfer, a real process with a real form and a real fee. Whether her lease could be broken and what it would cost. Whether the fellowship stipend covered two people if her own work took months, and what they would do if it did not.

Whether her mother would actually visit or would say she would and then not.

"She'll come," Maya said. "She'll complain about the flight for a full year afterward, and she'll come."

Near the end, his voice changed.

"I don't want you saying yes to an idea of me in a city you've never let yourself picture," he said. "I want you to say yes to the actual apartment with the actual fire escape, and the actual winter, which is going to be genuinely hard, and actual me on the days I'm not good company. Not a version you've been avoiding looking at closely enough to be properly frightened of."

After they hung up she sat with the laptop and went back through the listings on her own.

She let herself do the thing she had been declining to do since September: put herself in the rooms. A kitchen with counter space. Snow on a fire escape. A second-grade classroom in a building she had never seen.

It was less frightening with edges on it. That was the discovery. Not unfrightening. But an actual apartment on an actual street is a smaller object than the word Away, which is what she had been carrying around instead for two months and calling a decision.

# Chapter 53

### Thanksgiving

[L]{.dropcap}iza hosted, which meant a turkey and, on a separate table nobody had ever discussed making optional, lumpia and pancit and rice, plus cranberry sauce from a can that every adult present professed to dislike and which was, by nine o'clock, gone.

Marcus came for the first time.

He had prepared. Maya found it almost unbearably endearing, and did not tell him. He had looked up which dishes took a fork and which were meant for hands, and he arrived with a working theory, and the theory survived roughly ten minutes before three separate aunties began correcting him warmly and continuously for the rest of the afternoon.

"Not that one."

"He can have that one, Fe."

"He can have it, I'm saying he shouldn't put it on the *rice.*"

"Why not?"

"Because." Tita Fe had no further argument available and did not require one. "Give me the plate."

He gave her the plate. She fixed it. He said thank you. She was pleased with him for the remainder of the day and told Maya so twice in the kitchen, in a stage whisper audible from the next room.

Rico attended by video, propped against the gravy boat at an angle that gave the table an unflattering view of his ceiling, and spent twenty minutes describing traffic he would have encountered had he driven up, which he had not.

Tita Fe brought the adobo. She also, over dessert, told the entire Rodel Fernandez story herself, at volume, laughing harder than anybody.

"Fifty years of family gossip," she said, wiping her eyes. "Fifty years. And I sat this girl down in my own kitchen and gave her somebody else's scandal entirely."

"With total confidence, Tita."

"With *complete* confidence. I have never been more certain of anything in my life." She was delighted with herself. "Vallejo. I sent her to *Vallejo.*"

And Liza laughed.

Maya noticed it and kept it, the way you keep a small unexpected object. Not a polite laugh. Her mother put her hand flat on the table and laughed until she had to stop and start again, at a joke whose entire subject was her own mother's hidden engagement.

In August that subject had been a folder shoved three inches toward a trash can.

Later they did the dishes together while the rest of the family conducted an argument in the front room about a football game, the rules of which nobody could satisfactorily explain to Marcus, who kept asking increasingly precise questions in the manner of a man conducting a literature review.

"She wrote back, then," Liza said, to the sink. "The woman you told me about on the phone. Fely."

"A week. I'd decided about four times that she wasn't going to."

"She was being careful with it." Liza handed over a wet plate to dry, her voice steady in a way that felt, to Maya, like an actual vote of confidence rather than empty reassurance. "People who take that long over something this big usually aren't ignoring you, apo. That's different."

"How do you know that?"

"Because I took four weeks to answer your grandmother, the one time she gave me anything real to work with, back when I was pregnant with you." Liza smiled, a small, private smile Maya hadn't seen from her in a long while. "Sometimes careful just looks like slow from the outside."

"She'd have wanted to know," Liza said, to the window over the sink. "All of it. She'd have pretended she didn't and she'd have wanted to know."

"You think so?"

"I know so. She asked me about Danilo for five years and never once asked me directly." Liza handed over a plate. "That was how she asked. She'd say, how is the house, and she meant how are you, and everybody in that generation understood each other perfectly and it drove the rest of us insane."

They finished the dishes without saying much else, the comfortable quiet of two women who had, over the last few months, worked their way into a version of honesty with each other that neither of them fully had the words for yet, and Maya went home that night, Marcus's hand warm in hers the whole drive, thinking that whatever she said to him on Friday, this particular Thursday, turkey and lumpia and a mother who'd finally learned to laugh about the whole thing, had already been enough on its own.

# Chapter 54

### Answering

[F]{.dropcap}riday arrived the way ordinary Fridays do, right up until it stopped being one.

She brought takeout. That was deliberate. There was a version of this evening where she cooked, and where the cooking gave both of them something to do with their hands and their eyes for forty minutes, and she had decided somewhere around Wednesday that she was not going to allow herself that.

He opened the door with his coat still on, having gotten in about ninety seconds ahead of her.

"Yes," Maya said.

Marcus stopped with one arm out of a sleeve.

"I want to come with you," she said. "I want to be clear about why, because it matters to me that you hear the whole thing and not just the word."

"Okay."

"It's not because it's easy, and it's not because I've got it worked out, because I don't. I have no job there. I don't know how the credential transfers. I have looked at the winter and it frightens me." She had rehearsed none of this and was aware it was coming out badly and kept going. "It's because I have spent two months watching what happens to somebody who lets a clock make a decision for her. I've read the paperwork. I've held the invitation with the blank line on it. And I am not doing that."

He got the other arm out of the sleeve, very slowly, in the manner of a man who has been handed something and does not want to drop it while removing a coat.

"Say the first part again."

"Which part."

"The yes part. I want to hear it once more while I'm actually paying attention, because I think I missed most of it the first time."

"I'm choosing you," she said. "Out loud. On purpose. Before the thirtieth, and not because of the thirtieth."

The coat went on the back of a chair rather than the hook. He put the takeout bags down on the counter slowly, the way a person's hands move when they need a task while the rest of them catches up.

"Yes like moving-to-Chicago yes."

"Yes like that."

"You're sure."

"Marcus."

"I have to ask once. I'm asking once and then never again, I promise, I just." He stopped and started over. "I've spent two months trying very hard not to lean on you about this and I need to know I didn't."

"You didn't." She was already moving toward him. "You told me the date and then you left me alone with it, which is the single most decent thing anybody has done for me all year."

He crossed the rest of the kitchen in about a second and a half.

The kiss was not careful. That was the whole of what was different about it. For two months everything between them had been careful, both of them holding a small distance out of a mutual and unspoken agreement that neither one should be the one to press, and it closed all at once, her hands fisted in the front of his shirt, his palm warm at the back of her neck, the takeout going cold on the counter behind them and neither of them caring in the slightest.

Later, on the couch, her legs across his lap, the food eventually eaten at nine.

"I was so scared you'd say no," he said, into her hair.

"I was so scared I'd say nothing." She felt him laugh, once, without sound. "Which would have been worse. That's the part I understand now that I didn't in September. Saying no would have been a real thing. I'd have given you a real thing to work with."

"You'd have wrecked me."

"You'd have survived it."

"I'd have survived it," he agreed. "Eventually. Badly." His thumb was moving across her knuckles, slow, the old habit. "I want to say something and then I want to never bring it up again."

"Go on."

"I think you should tell your mother tomorrow rather than next week."

Maya lifted her head.

"Because next week turns into the week after," Marcus said, "and I've watched you do that particular thing twice now, and I'd rather be the person who says it out loud than the person who waits and hopes you notice."

She looked at him for a moment.

"That's a very annoying application of my own material," she said.

"It's an extremely annoying application of your own material and I've been sitting on it since Wednesday."

She called her mother in the morning.

# Chapter 55

### What Liza Does With It

[S]{.dropcap}he told her mother on Sunday, at the same kitchen table where the invitation had come out of hiding three weeks earlier, and this time she told all of it, in order, including everything she had skipped over at Thanksgiving.

The Facebook group. Boy Alvarez and his invented uncle. The six drafts. The night the phone spent in a drawer.

Then Fely's reply, which Maya read aloud off her phone rather than summarizing, because it seemed important that her mother get it in the woman's own words.

*He did know a nurse before he ever met my mother.*

*…a wedding half planned even, and that it simply didn't end up happening…*

*He married my mother in 1974. They were married thirty-seven years, until he passed. He had the three of us, six grandchildren after that. He taught at the elementary school in town for more than forty years, everybody in Miagao still remembers him, the mayor himself came to his funeral.*

*He was, by every single account I've ever heard including my own memory of him, a happy man.*

Liza listened to all of it with her hands flat on the table.

When Maya finished she did not say anything for a while.

"Forty years at the school," she said eventually.

"Forty years."

"Your grandmother taught nobody. She nursed. But she'd have liked that he taught." Liza's mouth did something complicated. "She had opinions about teachers. She'd have had opinions about him."

She did not cry. Whatever crying she had needed to do about this, she had done at some point in the preceding weeks, privately, in a manner Maya had not witnessed and understood she was not owed a witness to.

What she did instead, Maya had not expected at all.

She reached across the table and picked up the invitation.

It had been sitting there in front of them the entire conversation, folded, the way it had been folded for fifty-five years, along the same crease, so worn now that the paper had gone soft and slightly furred at the fold line.

Liza opened it.

She did it slowly, both hands, all the way flat, and then went over the crease with the side of her thumb, twice, pressing it out.

It did not lie flat. Fifty-five years is long enough that paper stops being able to; the fold had become part of the object rather than something done to it, and the two halves kept lifting very slightly at the edges, and Liza kept smoothing them, and it kept not working. The gold foil border caught the afternoon light off the kitchen window. The blank line sat where the date would have gone, still blank, still waiting for absolutely nothing.

"She kept it folded because she couldn't look at it head-on," Liza said, mostly to herself, thumb still moving.

"Ma."

"I think I can look at it head-on now. For her." She set it down flat between them. "We should have this framed."

"Framed."

"Not out in the living room where people would see it and ask. Somewhere private." Liza was still looking at it. "I just don't want it living folded up in the dark anymore, like it's a thing to be ashamed of. She wasn't a thing to be ashamed of, apo. She was a woman who got badly frightened once, when she was twenty-three years old and six thousand miles from her mother, and then spent fifty-five years being brave about absolutely everything else instead."

Maya looked at her mother.

"You're not angry at her anymore."

"Oh, I'm angry at her." Liza said it immediately, without heat, the way you'd confirm the weather. "I expect I'll be a little angry at her for the rest of my life, and I've decided recently that that's allowed as well."

She looked up. Something in her face had loosened, not repaired exactly, nothing so tidy, only loosened, the way a knot goes slack the moment you stop pulling against it.

"Both at once," she said. "I told you. I'm still learning how to carry them at the same time."

Then she got up and went down the hall.

Maya heard the closet door, and the particular sound a shoebox makes coming off a high shelf, which is a sound with no other source in a house, and then nothing for long enough that she thought about going after her and did not.

Liza came back with a photograph in her hand and put it on the table face up, next to the invitation, and sat down and folded her hands and did not look at it.

"That's my mother at about twenty-five."

It was a Polaroid, small, the white border gone the color of weak tea. Perla in a kitchen Maya did not recognize, half turned, laughing at something outside the frame, in a sleeveless dress and no lipstick, with her hair down, which Maya had never in her life seen it.

It was not the woman on the credenza and it was not the woman in the class photograph on the steps at Iloilo.

It was somebody's friend, at a party, in 1973.

"Where did you get this."

"Her dresser. I was fifteen." Liza's hands stayed folded. "The same drawer, apo. The same week. I took the invitation out and put it back and I took this one out and did not put it back, and I have had it for thirty-six years and I have never shown it to one living person, including your father."

Maya did not touch it.

"Why?"

Her mother took a while.

"Because it was the only part of her I ever had that nobody else did." She said it evenly, in the voice she used for a plumber's estimate. "You had her as a grandmother, which is a much easier thing to be given. Tita Fe had her as a woman from home. The parish had her as a person who cooked. I had her as a mother, and a mother is mostly a set of rules and a plate arriving, and this was the only thing I ever got that was just hers, and I kept it, and I want to be extremely clear with you that I knew exactly what I was doing the entire time."

"Ma."

"I have spent two months being angry at a woman for keeping something folded in a box." Liza's chin did something and steadied. "And I have had this in a shoebox on a shelf for thirty-six years and I did not once, in two months, think to mention it. Not to you. Not when you were on your knees in my garage. Not when I sat in this kitchen and told you to leave it alone."

The kitchen clock did what it does.

"You could keep it," Maya said.

"I know I could keep it."

"Nobody would ever know."

"That's right," her mother said. "That is exactly right, and that is the entire problem, and I have had it explained to me at some length this year by a woman who is dead."

She pushed it across the table.

She did it the way she had pushed the folder away from herself in August, the same motion, three inches of laminate, the same hand. It was, Maya thought, watching it come toward her, the only gesture her mother had for this, and she had now used it in both directions.

"Take it to Illinois," Liza said. "I don't want it in the box anymore and I don't want it on a wall either. I want it somewhere it can be picked up."

Maya picked it up.

Her grandmother went on laughing at whatever it was, on a kitchen counter in 1973, in a room nobody alive could name, at somebody nobody alive could name either.

"You're allowed to want it back," Maya said.

"I'm aware of what I'm allowed." Liza stood and took both coffee cups to the sink and turned the water on, and then off, and stood there a second with her back to the room. "Ask me about it sometime. That's all I want out of this. Not that you keep it carefully. That you can ask me about it and I'll answer, and that I can ask you, and you'll answer, and neither of us has to wait until one of us is in the ground to find out what the other one was carrying around the whole time."

"Okay."

"Okay." Liza dried her hands. "Now go home, it's late, and leave the invitation here, I want to think about the frame."

# Chapter 56

### Framing It

[T]{.dropcap}he framing shop in Pacifica had a hand-lettered sign, a bell on the door, and a proprietor named Arturo who did not rush anybody, which turned out to matter, because Liza spent twenty-five minutes on the mat.

"Not white."

"White's the standard," Arturo said, without any investment in it.

"White makes it a museum piece. Like it's finished, and it's behind glass, and that's the end of it." She had four sample corners fanned out in her hand and was holding them one at a time against the invitation, which lay flat on the counter under the good light. "I want something warmer than that. Something that still looks like it belongs in a house."

"Cream, then. Or the oatmeal."

"Show me both against it."

He showed her both. She looked at both for a long time, and Maya, standing slightly behind her mother, understood that she was watching a woman make a decision that had almost nothing to do with matting.

"That one," Liza said finally. The deep cream.

"Frame?"

"Plain. Wood. Nothing carved, nothing gold, nothing that's trying." She glanced at Maya. "It's got enough gold on it already."

Arturo took the invitation up carefully, in both hands, and looked at it properly for the first time. He was perhaps seventy. He had, by the look of the place, been doing this a long while, and had presumably had all manner of things brought to him across that counter by people who were not entirely composed about it.

"The date's not filled in," he said.

"No."

He did not ask.

Maya thought about that for a long time afterward. There was a whole conversation available to him, a widow and her daughter and a nearly sixty-year-old invitation with a blank line where the date belonged, and he declined all of it.

"The foil's tarnished along the border here," he said instead. "I can clean that up, if you want. Takes about an hour and it comes back nearly new."

"No."

Liza said it fast, then softened it.

"No, thank you. Leave it exactly as it is." She reached over and touched the edge of it, briefly. "It's nearly sixty years old. That's part of it now. Making it look new would be its own kind of lie."

Arturo nodded, and wrote something on the ticket, and said it would be ready Thursday.

It was ready Thursday, as promised.

Liza collected it herself and carried it home flat across the back seat with a towel over it, and did not open the paper until she was inside with the door shut, which Maya only learned about later, secondhand, and which she thought about for a long time: her mother alone in a hallway at fifty-one, cutting tape on a package, with nobody there to see her face when the paper came off.

It went on the wall of Liza's bedroom, opposite the bed. The first thing she saw most mornings and close to the last thing most nights: a wedding invitation for a marriage that never happened, printed for a woman who went on to have an entirely different and full life, the blank line still blank.

Not hidden. Not folded. Just up there on a wall, the way a fact sits in a room once you have stopped being afraid of what looking straight at it might cost you.

"Your Lola would have hated this," Liza said, standing back to see it hung for the first time. "Having it out where she'd have to look at it every day."

"Or she would have liked finally not having to hide it," Maya offered. "We'll never actually know which."

"No," Liza agreed, quiet. "We won't. I think that not knowing is just going to be part of loving her from now on." She reached up and straightened the frame half an inch, though it hadn't been crooked, an old habit neither of them commented on. "I can live with that. I think, honestly, it's better than the alternative."

# Chapter 57

### Telling Everyone Else

[T]{.dropcap}he wider family found out about Chicago the way the wider family found out about everything, which is to say all at once, over food, at a gathering convened for an unrelated reason.

Liza hosted a dinner the following weekend, nominally for Tita Fe's birthday, and used it, with Maya's agreement, to put the news in front of everybody simultaneously rather than conduct a dozen separate phone calls she had no intention of making.

She waited until the plates were down and people had started eating.

"Maya's moving to Chicago."

Forks paused.

"With Marcus. In January."

Then it all arrived at once, in the overlapping way of a table with nine people at it.

Maya had prepared for noise. What arrived first was about a second of silence, which made the news larger than she had wanted it to be.

Then it broke.

Aunty Baby, immediately and with total practicality: "How many bedrooms?"

"One."

"One." A pause of visible disapproval. "Is it safe? The neighborhood."

"It's safe, Aunty."

"Is there a Filipino store?"

"There's one about forty minutes away."

"Forty *minutes.*" Aunty Baby put down her fork to convey the gravity of this. "So you'll go once a month and buy too much and half of it will go bad. That's what happens. That's exactly what happens."

Rico, on speaker from Sacramento: "Good. Finally."

"I've been waiting for this call since the summer and I want everybody at that table to know I have said nothing."

"You have said several things," said Liza.

"I've said them to *her.* That's different."

Somebody wanted to know about the winter. Somebody else wanted to know whether Marcus's job was permanent, and Maya said it was two years, and there was a small silence around that which she let pass without filling.

It was Tita Fe, later, over dessert, who said the quieter thing.

"You're really going."

"I'm really going."

"Leaving all of this behind." She gestured with her spoon, meaning the table, the house, the fog outside, the whole arrangement.

"Not leaving it behind." Maya heard the sentence arrive and understood, halfway through it, that she had been building toward being able to say it for about four months. "Adding somewhere else to it."

Tita Fe considered that.

"Your Lola never said it that way," she said.

"No."

"She said she came here. That was the whole word she used, for fifty years. *Came.* Never once said she'd left anywhere." Tita Fe went back to her flan. "I noticed it. I never said anything, because what would I have said."

Down the table Aunty Baby had moved on to whether Maya had a doctor lined up there, had established that she did not, and had begun to lay out an appropriate timeline for arranging one, and was still laying it out when the table drank to something else entirely, over the top of her, and wished it known that she had not finished her point.

# Interlude: Liza and Danilo {.unnumbered}

[S]{.dropcap}he had never told Maya how she met Danilo Reyes, mostly because the story had never seemed worth the telling: two young Filipino-Americans at the same community college dance in 1995, both there because their mothers had insisted, both privately intending to leave early, and both staying until the lights came up because neither wanted to be the one who admitted the night had been fun.

They married three years later. It was, by any honest accounting, a good marriage, and unremarkable in exactly the way Liza had always wanted a marriage to be, after growing up in a house where she had sensed, without ever being able to name it, some private weather system moving through her mother that nobody else appeared to notice. Danilo worked at the port. He came home the same person he had left as that morning, which she had come, over two decades, to understand as a gift rather than an absence of excitement.

He died on a Tuesday in March, a heart attack at his desk at the shipping office, fast enough that the doctors told her with that particular gentle bluntness that he had likely not suffered. It was meant as comfort and mostly landed as one, though she had spent more nights than she would admit to anybody doing the useless arithmetic of exactly how fast fast is.

Maya was twenty-two that year, weeks from her credential and months from her first classroom, and Liza made a deliberate decision in the weeks after the funeral not to lean on her only child any harder than necessary.

Nobody had warned her that the third year would be the hardest one.

The first year had come with machinery attached, and other people to help operate it: the funeral, the nine nights, a first Christmas and a first birthday and a first anniversary, each hard in an obvious way that other people understood well enough to call about. By the third year the machinery had dismantled itself. Friends stopped asking how she was holding up. Maya was twenty-five and settled into her own apartment and her own troubles and called every Sunday and had, reasonably, a full life the rest of the week. Liza went to work, came home, ate at a table built for four, and found a quality to the quiet that the first year's structured grief had never actually required her to sit inside.

She took up gardening that year, not out of any love of it but because it gave her hands something to do on the stretch of Saturday afternoon that had belonged to Danilo puttering in the garage while she cooked. She grew tomatoes badly and green beans slightly better and told herself most weeks that a woman who kept a garden alive was managing fine.

She dated once, a widower from the parish, kind and steady and entirely uninteresting to her in a way she felt guilty about, and let it end after four dinners with an excuse about not being ready that was true and that she suspected even then would go on being true for longer than either of them wanted to say.

She kept Danilo's number in her phone. She has never deleted it. Twice a year at most, never on any date of significance, she scrolls past it on the way to somebody else and stops for a second, and then goes on to whoever she was actually calling.

Somebody else has the number now. That occurred to her around year six and she has declined to think about it further.

In the fourth year she took his coats to the parish thrift shop, all six, on a Wednesday. Nora was working. Neither woman said anything about it beyond what the transaction required.

Nora hung them on the rack herself after Liza had gone. Two sold that same month. She has never mentioned it to anybody in the family and does not intend to.

# Chapter 58

### The Resignation Letter

[S]{.dropcap}he wrote it on a Sunday afternoon in early December and then sat looking at it for two hours.

It was four sentences long. Five years of gratitude, notice through winter break, an offer to help with the transition in whatever way was most useful. She had written harder emails about field trip permission slips.

What took the two hours was that hitting send would make it real in a way that saying yes to Marcus, somehow, had not.

It surprised her. She had said yes to him in his kitchen with takeout on the counter and had meant it with her whole chest, and it had felt, in the moment, like the largest decision she had ever made out loud.

And then here was a four-sentence email to a district office that would not even be read until Monday, and her hand would not move.

She read the four sentences perhaps thirty times.

She changed *five wonderful years* to *five years*, on the grounds that the district had not asked for her opinion. She cut a line about gratitude for the opportunity, which read, on the page, as something generated rather than meant.

What she could not put in was the actual content: *I do not want to leave and I am leaving anyway, and both of those are true, and there is no field on this form for that.*

Five years in the same building.

She had taught the younger siblings of children she had already taught, a seven-year-old walking into her room in September with a last name she recognized and a face that was a slightly different arrangement of a face she knew.

Twice now she had been recognized in the grocery store on Mission by a former student working a register, and both times the kid had said *Ms. B?* with real delight, and both times Maya had gone out to her car afterward and sat for a second before starting it. That was, she had decided years ago, the highest honor available in elementary education. Nothing else came close.

She had taught under her mother's mother's name since her first classroom. Nobody had ever asked her about it and she had never worked out what she would say if anybody did.

She sent it at four-fifteen.

The principal called her at four-fifty-two, on a Sunday, which she had not expected at all and which made her stand up out of her chair to answer.

"Marlon. It's Sunday."

"It is Sunday. I read it anyway." A pause on the line. "I'm not going to try and talk you out of it."

"Okay."

"I want to be clear that I thought about it. I got about as far as opening a reply." He laughed a little. "I'm calling to say I read it twice, and I'm sorry, and that if you ever want to come back, this district would take you in a heartbeat. I'll put that in writing tonight if it's useful to you for anything at all. Reference, whatever. Say the word."

"That's very kind."

"It's not kind, it's accurate." He cleared his throat. "Second grade's hard to staff and good second grade is nearly impossible. You know that."

She got off the phone and cried for about four minutes, standing up, in her own kitchen, which she had not seen coming.

Because that was the thing she had been quietly bracing against for weeks without letting herself name it.

Not the city. Not the fog. Not her mother, who would be fine, who would be more than fine, who had Tita Fe and Aunty Baby and a parish that fed itself.

The job.

She was good at this job. She had been good at it for five years, in a building where the custodian knew her coffee order and the office manager knew which parents to route directly to her and a man named Marlon would call on a Sunday afternoon to tell her so.

And in January she would be a substitute in a district where nobody knew her name, teaching other people's classes, out of other people's cupboards, with no idea whatsoever where anything was kept.

# Chapter 59

### Telling Room 14

[S]{.dropcap}he told the class in December, two weeks before break, because Roz had been extremely firm about the timing.

"Not before Thanksgiving."

"That feels like I'm hiding it."

"You're not hiding it, you're managing it. Seven-year-olds have no functional concept of a month." Roz was grading at the time and did not look up. "You tell them in October and they will grieve you for eight consecutive weeks while you are standing directly in front of them, alive, handing out worksheets. Two weeks is exactly right. Long enough to say goodbye properly, short enough that it stays real."

She practiced it in the car. Twice.

Both versions were terrible, and she abandoned the second one halfway through, and arrived having decided to simply say it and manage whatever came.

Maya told them on a Monday morning at carpet time, when anything of consequence got told in Room 14, and it went approximately the way she had expected, which is to say it went sideways immediately.

"Are you in trouble?"

"I'm not in trouble."

"Did you get *fired*?"

"Nobody fired me. I'm moving to a different city, which is a thing grown-ups sometimes do, and I'm going to miss all of you very much."

"Which city?"

"Chicago."

This produced a wave of noise, because two children had been to Chicago and both wished to establish that fact immediately and at length, and a third had an aunt there, or possibly in Michigan.

"Will the new teacher be mean?"

"The new teacher will not be mean."

"How do you *know*?"

"Because Mr. Ocampo is not a mean person, and because I would fight him," Maya said, which got exactly the laugh she had aimed for and bought her about forty seconds.

Dario had his hand up and had been holding it up, rigidly, through all of it. When she called on him he did not lower it.

"Who's going to feed Kevin."

"Mr. Ocampo will feed Kevin."

"Does he know how much? Because it's not a *lot.*" Dario's voice had gone up. This was the first thing all morning that anybody in that room was genuinely afraid of. "It's a small amount. It has to be the small amount, Ms. B, or he gets fat and it's *bad* for him, and if somebody doesn't tell him then..."

"Dario."

"Somebody has to tell him."

"I'm going to write it down," Maya said. "On paper. The exact amount. And I'm going to tape it to the tank so it's right there where anybody can see it, and I'm going to tell Mr. Ocampo out loud as well, so it's written down and said."

Dario considered this for a long moment.

Then he put his hand down.

What she had not expected was the follow-up question, which came from Marisol and which was worse than Dario's.

"Will you remember us?"

The room went completely quiet for the second time in ten minutes.

"Yes," Maya said. "I remember every class I've ever had. I can tell you the name of every single kid I taught five years ago."

"Prove it."

"Marisol."

"You could be lying."

So Maya stood up and said fourteen names from her first year, in order, around the carpet as it had been arranged in 2021, and got to the fifteenth and could not produce it, and stood there with twenty-six seven-year-olds watching her try.

"Anthony," she said, eventually. "Anthony. He had a cast for most of October."

The class accepted this as sufficient evidence and moved on.

She did write it out that same afternoon, in marker, in her clearest print, and taped it at a child's eye level rather than an adult's, which she did not explain to anybody. Mr. Ocampo left it up for the rest of the year, and the following year, and Roz eventually sent her a photograph of it in the following March, curling slightly at one corner, still there.

It was the last question of the morning that she was not ready for.

A girl named Sofia, who sat near the window and volunteered almost nothing and had once written a four-sentence paragraph about her grandmother that Maya still had in a folder at home, put her hand halfway up.

"Are you sad?"

The room went quiet in the way rooms do when a child asks the actual question.

Maya thought about lying. It would have been easy, and in the short term it would have been kinder, and she had lied to children about smaller things a hundred times to get through a Tuesday.

"Yes," she said. "I'm really sad about leaving you. And I'm really excited about where I'm going." She sat back on her heels on the carpet.

Sofia nodded slowly.

"Okay," she said, and went back to picking at the carpet, entirely satisfied, the matter closed.

Somebody else immediately asked whether it snowed in Chicago and whether Ms. B would send a picture, and the morning got away from them, and they never did get to the reading block.

# Chapter 60

### What She Gave Away

[T]{.dropcap}he December purge turned out to be an education in what five stationary years will quietly accumulate in a one-bedroom apartment.

A bread machine, used four times.

A yoga mat, used fewer than four times, still bearing the crease of its original packaging.

An entire cabinet of mismatched mugs arrived at through no process she could reconstruct, including two she was fairly sure belonged to a woman she had shared an apartment with in 2021 and had not spoken to since.

Six bags of clothing went to donation. Three boxes of books survived a triage that involved holding roughly forty individual titles and asking each one whether it was coming to Illinois, a conversation she had out loud, alone, over the course of an entire Saturday.

The paperwork from her first two years of teaching went in the trash, and she was surprised by how good it felt. She had been dutifully moving that box from apartment to apartment for five years. Nobody had ever asked to see a single item in it.

She did it in the wrong order, which she recognized about a third of the way through and could not by then undo.

The correct order is: decide, then sort, then pack. What she actually did was open every container in the apartment at once, on a Saturday, with no system, on the theory that a system would emerge under pressure.

By two in the afternoon there was no floor.

She sat down in the middle of it around four and looked at an entire life laid out across four hundred square feet in an arrangement with no logic to it whatsoever, and thought of her mother in August with her three piles and her Sharpie, whose system she had privately considered fussy at the time.

She gave the bread machine to Roz.

"I don't want this."

"I know."

"I want to be clear that I'm accepting a bread machine I do not want, and that I'm doing it because I love you, and that it's going in my garage."

"That's all I'm asking."

The hardest category was the one she had not anticipated, because she had never in her life gathered it into one place.

Things her grandmother had given her.

They had been distributed across five years and four hundred square feet without Maya ever consciously grouping them: a wooden spoon in the crock by the stove, worn asymmetrical from decades of somebody else's stirring. A saint's medal on a thin chain, given at her confirmation, never once worn. A crocheted blanket in a combination of oranges and browns that Maya had privately considered hideous since she was nineteen and had used every winter anyway.

And the Polaroid, which had been on her refrigerator under a magnet shaped like a pineapple since November, where three separate visitors had asked about it and had been told, and where it had spent two weeks being an ordinary object in an ordinary kitchen rather than the bottom item in anybody's shoebox.

She put them all on the bed together and looked at them.

It was not a large pile. That was somehow the worst part: fifty years of a woman, a spoon and a medal and a blanket and a cookbook.

She kept every one of them. She did not spend even a minute deciding.

And then, taping the box shut, she had the thought properly for the first time.

Liza, keeping a Polaroid at the bottom of a shoebox for thirty-six years.

Perla, keeping an invitation folded inside a visa letter for fifty-five.

Nora, keeping a whole private reason to herself for five decades and volunteering nothing.

Maya, at twenty-seven, on her knees on a bedroom floor, taping a box shut on a dead woman's spoon.

She sat with that for a minute.

And then went to the hall closet and found the box she had moved through two apartments without ever once unpacking it, and took out the old phone, and plugged it in, and sat down on the floor beside the outlet while it decided whether it was going to come on.

It came on. It always had. She had checked twice in five years and had never needed to know the answer for any reason except this one.

Ninety seconds, in a folder, dated in the March her father died. A man explaining that the dentist had moved the appointment to the Thursday and that this was, in his considered opinion, an improvement.

She did not play it.

She had understood for years what the not-playing was, and had been comfortable enough calling it a kind of care, in the way a person is comfortable with anything she has never once had to say out loud to somebody else.

So she said it out loud.

"There's a voicemail from my dad on this," she told Marcus, later, with the phone in her lap and the boxes at her back. "Ninety seconds. It's about a dentist appointment. I've had it five years and I've never played it."

He put down the tape gun.

"Do you want to?"

"Not tonight."

"Okay," he said, and asked nothing else, and went back to the tape, and Maya sat on the floor of an apartment she was leaving and understood that she had braced, out of pure habit, for a question that was never going to come, and that the bracing had been the entire cost of the last five years, paid nightly, to nobody.

She put the phone in the box going to Illinois.

Then she worked out the actual distinction.

The eleven sealed bank statements passed through her hands somewhere in the middle of it, the rubber-banded brick of them from the early nineties, the ones she had asked her mother for the week before without explaining and been handed without a single question. She held them a moment. She could open them right now, on this floor, in about forty seconds, and learn whatever eighteen months of a dead woman's money had to say for itself.

She put them in the box unopened.

Underneath them, at the bottom of the same folder, was a notebook she had not seen before.

It was small and cheap and had a rubber band around it too, a different one, gone soft rather than brittle, which meant somebody had replaced it at some point. The cover said nothing. She turned it over twice looking for a label, on the reasonable grounds that this was a woman who labeled things, and there was none.

Inside, in the small forward-leaning hand, four decades of names.

One line each. *Mrs. Abraham, 1981. Went home to her sister in Fresno and sent a card at Christmas for nine years and then did not.* *Danny R., 1994, nineteen. His mother came every day and slept in the chair and I never got her to eat anything.* *The man in 6 with no people. Tuesday. I stayed.*

There were, at a rough count, two hundred and forty of them.

Maya sat back on a bedroom floor in a half-packed apartment and read for the better part of forty minutes, which she had not budgeted for and which put the whole afternoon behind.

She kept waiting for the thing to turn. For a page where the handwriting changed, or a line that was not about a patient, or an initial underlined hard, or anything at all folded into the spine.

There was nothing. It was exactly what it appeared to be, all the way to the last entry, dated 2009, which said *Mr. Villaflor. Family did not make it. Forty minutes. Did not clock it.*

She sat with that.

Her grandmother had kept a private record of two hundred and forty patients for forty years, had told nobody in the family it existed, and there was no secret in it anywhere. It was a woman writing down what a day had contained, in a book she kept in a drawer, for nobody, in exactly the way she wrote in the margins of a cookbook she kept on top of a refrigerator where anyone could reach it.

Which meant the compression on the torta page was not how Perla Bautista handled a heavy thing.

It was the only time she ever had.

Maya put the notebook in the box with the statements and the spoon.

If anyone who loved her ever picked them up and asked, she could say exactly what they were and exactly why she had kept them sealed. That was the whole test, and she was beginning to understand that it ran in both directions.

She found a marker.

She wrote KITCHEN, MISC, LOLA on the top of the box, in capitals, large enough to read across a room, and put it with the pile going to Illinois rather than the pile staying behind.

# Interlude: The Eleven Letters {.unnumbered}

[S]{.dropcap}he started eleven letters between March and September of that year.

She knows the number because she counted them, once, decades later, and then destroyed all eleven in the kitchen sink in Daly City, on an ordinary afternoon, without telling anybody she was doing it.

She wrote to Ramon exactly once more after the deadline passed, a short letter, shorter than any she'd sent him in two years, telling him only that she'd stayed on longer than planned, that the hospital needed her, that she didn't know yet when she'd be able to say more. She mailed it on a Thursday and then spent every following Thursday for the next several months at the dormitory mailbox, waiting for a reply that never came, and told herself, each time it didn't, that the mail boat was simply slow, that Miagao was simply far, that there were a hundred ordinary reasons a letter might not arrive on schedule.

No reply ever came at all. She would wonder, for the rest of her life, whether he'd written back and the letter had genuinely gone astray somewhere between two countries, or whether he'd understood everything her short, evasive letter hadn't said and had decided, with more grace than she'd given him credit for at the time, not to make her say it twice.

Ernesto proposed a year later, at a diner near the hospital, over coffee that had gone cold in both their cups. He said that whatever was in her past belonged to her, and that he was asking anyway. She told him the one true thing she had decided somebody in this country should know, in four sentences, without the name, and he said it made sense and never asked a follow-up question, then or ever again, which she would come to understand, over forty-three years of marriage, was itself its own kind of vow.

She kept the invitation. She never fully understood, herself, why she couldn't bring herself to throw it away, only that some nights, unpacking a box in their first small apartment together, she'd come across it and feel something close to physical pain, and rather than face that feeling directly she folded it, that same evening, inside the exchange visitor letter that explained, in its flat official language, exactly why the date on the invitation had never been filled in. Two documents that told the same story from two different angles, filed away together where no one else would ever think to look, and she carried them, folded that same way, through every move of the next fifty-four years, taking them out perhaps four or five times in all that span, always alone, always for just long enough to remember, and never once long enough to let the remembering turn into regret she'd allow herself to keep.

# Chapter 61

### Back Into Use

[R]{.dropcap}ico came through town the following Sunday for a work conference, and Liza did what Liza always did when family passed through, cooking for roughly twice the number of people expected.

"He's one person, Ma."

"He's one person who drove four hours."

"He flew."

"He flew and then he drove from the airport," Liza said, "which is still driving," and took out the large pan.

Maya offered to make the torta.

She said it while looking in the refrigerator rather than at her mother, and there was a pause behind her of about a second and a half, and then Liza said, "There's ground pork in the freezer," and that was the whole of the conversation.

She used her grandmother's recipe as written, except the garlic, which she did Ramon's way: more than any written recipe calls for.

It was, in the end, an unremarkable Sunday afternoon. Nobody gathered in the doorway. Nobody said anything about what the dish was or whose it had been. Liza chopped onions at the counter beside her without being asked and complained about the knife, not the good knife, because the good knife had gone to Tita Fe in August. In the next room Rico conducted a loud and increasingly personal argument with a parking app.

"It says the space is available. I am *looking at the space.* There is a car in it."

"Nobody can help you," Liza called.

"I'm aware of that, Auntie."

The kitchen smelled the way it had always smelled. Maya had not been braced for it. Garlic going soft in oil is not a metaphor and does not care what year it is; it simply smells the way it smells, and it had smelled that way in a second-floor apartment off Mission for forty years, and it smelled that way now.

She cracked an egg one-handed against the rim of the bowl.

She noticed it about half a second after she'd done it. The shell came apart cleanly, the yolk went in whole, and her thumb was already reaching for the second egg before her brain caught up with what her hand had just performed without being asked.

Nineteen years, she had been failing to do that. Standing on a kitchen chair at eight. Fragments in the batter every time, laughed at, permitted to continue. She had assumed she never learned it.

Apparently she had learned it. She had simply learned it so slowly, over so many afternoons, that there had never been a day she could point to.

She stood there holding the second egg for a moment.

Not a ghost. That was the thing she could not have explained to anybody in that house, and did not try to. Not a wound either. Just a woman in a kitchen doing something with her hands that another woman's hands had taught her, over years, on purpose, without either of them ever once naming it as instruction.

She cracked the second egg. It went fine.

Rico ate three helpings and talked with his mouth full through most of the meal.

"Tastes different than I remember."

"More garlic."

"Huh." He considered this seriously, fork paused. "It's better."

"It is better," Maya agreed. "Turns out that was always the correct amount. Somebody told me. It took me a while to believe them."

Rico nodded and went back to his plate and asked whether anyone had found the serving dish, and Liza said that the serving dish was gone, and Rico said that the serving dish was not gone, it was somewhere in this house, and the argument resumed exactly where it had been left in September.

The serving dish turned up the following March, in the garage, in a box marked KITCHEN, MISC in Liza's own handwriting, having been packed by Liza personally in a state of considerable distraction on the day they cleared the apartment.

She telephoned Rico to report that it had been located and that this changed nothing, and the two of them stayed on the phone for fifty minutes.

# Chapter 62

### A Letter That Isn't a Request

[S]{.dropcap}he wrote to Fely that night, and this time it took four minutes rather than four days, because this time she was not asking for anything and that turned out to be the entire difference.

She had learned something over the six drafts back in November, and she applied it now without having to think about it: write the thing that is true, and then check whether any sentence in it is secretly a request. There was only one rule and it was that simple.

*I made the torta today,* she wrote.

*Using your father's amount of garlic. My grandmother wrote in the margin of her cookbook, years ago, that she thought he was right about it. She never said so to him. She never said so to anybody. She just wrote it down in a book she kept on top of the refrigerator, where anybody in the house could have picked it up, and nobody ever did.*

*I'm not asking you for anything. I want to be clear about that because I know the last time I wrote to you I was.*

*I only wanted you to know the recipe is still in use on this side of it too. And that whatever else happened, your father's opinion about the garlic has finally won.*

She read it twice, nine fewer times than the first message, and sent it at ten past eight on a Sunday.

Fely wrote back within the hour.

*That made me laugh out loud, in my kitchen, by myself.*

*He always insisted he was right about the garlic. Every single time it came up. My mother used to say that arguing with him about that dish was the only time he ever got stubborn about anything in thirty-seven years.*

*Thank you for telling me. It's a strange feeling. Good, but strange. Two families who never met each other, on opposite sides of an ocean, having the same argument about the same dish for fifty-five years and neither one knowing the other one was having it.*

Maya read that on her couch with the plate still in the sink and the kitchen still smelling of oil and garlic.

She did not write back. There was nothing that needed to go back.

She washed the pan by hand rather than leaving it, which she did not usually do, and which she was aware at the time she was doing to have something to do with her hands.

She sat there a while afterward, holding the phone in her lap, and thought about the fact that neither of them had asked the other for a single thing in the entire exchange. Not a photograph. Not a phone call. Not a promise to stay in touch, the thing people say at the end of exchanges like this and rarely mean.

Then both of them had stopped, and neither of them had needed to discuss it.

# Chapter 63

### The Winter Concert

[R]{.dropcap}oom 14's contribution to the winter concert was "Jingle Bells," on recorders, performed by twenty-six seven-year-olds who had rehearsed it for three weeks and had achieved, in that time, a level of unity best described as approximate.

Maya stood in the wings with her hands pressed together, willing individual notes into existence.

The first eight bars were, genuinely, excellent. She had not expected the first eight bars. Somewhere around bar nine, one child at the end of the row lost their place, recovered by starting again from the beginning, and pulled two neighbors with them, so that from that point onward the class was performing the piece in two separate keys and two separate tempos, both of which were internally consistent.

Dario was doing neither. Dario was a full beat ahead of everybody, and had been from the downbeat, and remained there for the entire duration with an expression of total serene confidence, a boy who had privately concluded that the rest of the ensemble had fallen behind and that the correct professional response was to hold his line.

There was a moment near the end where the two keys briefly converged, entirely by accident, and the parents applauded before the song was finished because they thought it had been finished.

It had not been finished. There were eleven more bars.

"That," Liza announced in the parking lot afterward, still wiping her eyes, "was the best thing I have seen in a year."

"It was a catastrophe."

"It was a *masterpiece,* Maya. That boy in the middle never wavered."

"Dario."

"Dario never wavered. Everybody else fell apart around him and he simply held the line." Liza had driven down specifically for this and had brought a small bunch of flowers to give to nobody in particular, and was now carrying them around the parking lot. "Your Lola would have loved every second of that."

"Would she?"

"She used to say the best part of any recital was the kid who got it wrong with complete confidence. She said it told you more about how somebody's life would go than any perfect performance ever did." Liza laughed. "She said it about you, once. You were nine and you played the wrong piece."

"I did not."

"You absolutely did. You played the piece from the previous concert. The entire way through. Your teacher's face."

"Did she come to those? I don't remember her being there."

"Every single one." Liza sounded surprised she'd been asked. "She sat at the back so she wouldn't embarrass you by clapping too loudly, and then she clapped too loudly."

Maya thought about that in the car.

She had, in fact, no memory of her grandmother at any school concert. She had a memory of looking for her mother in a crowd of parents and finding her, and no memory whatsoever of the second woman who was, on every one of those evenings, sitting several rows further back, clapping too loudly. Not one. Which meant Perla Bautista had attended perhaps thirty of them across a decade, sat at the back, clapped too loudly, and arranged the whole thing so successfully that her granddaughter had no recollection of her being there at all.

"I used to think she just liked getting out of the house," Liza said, later, at the light on Mission. "I think now she also liked watching a room full of children get to be exactly, loudly, imperfectly themselves in front of everybody."

She did not add the second half of it.

She didn't need to. Maya drove the rest of the way with Dario's serene, wrong, unwavering recorder line still going in her head.

# Chapter 64

### The Last Wednesday

[T]{.dropcap}he thrift shop had put up decorations, which meant one string of lights along the window frame with a third of it out and a paper snowflake taped to the till at an angle nobody had corrected since the first week of December.

Nora was on coats again. She was always on coats. Maya had asked once whether she ever got put on anything else and had been told that the other volunteers could not be trusted with sizing, delivered as a technical assessment rather than a complaint.

"You're leaving in January," Nora said, before Maya had the door shut.

"How do you know that?"

"Your Tita Fe told nine people in one week and got the month wrong for five of them." She shook out a parka and looked at the lining without approval. "She has you going in March. I corrected her."

"Thank you."

"I corrected her twice." Nora hung the parka. "She has not adjusted."

Maya took the folding chair without being offered it this time, which she noticed herself doing, and which Nora did not remark on.

"I came to tell you something and then I'm going to go, because you don't like company that stays."

"That is a slander your grandmother started and I have never been able to get out of the parish."

"Is it inaccurate?"

"It is not inaccurate." Nora sat down on the second chair. "Go on."

So Maya told her.

It took about four minutes and she did it in order, which she had practiced in the car and which turned out to be the right call: the group, the photograph, the woman who answered, the name. Then the rest of it. Married in 1974. Thirty-seven years. Three children and six grandchildren. Taught at the elementary school for more than forty years, and the mayor came to the funeral, and by every account anybody had he was a genuinely happy man.

Nora listened to all of it with her hands on her knees and did not interrupt once.

When Maya finished she did not say anything for a while, and the shop made the small sounds a shop makes, and outside on the sidewalk somebody's car alarm considered going off and thought better of it.

"He was funny in his letters," Nora said, finally.

"You told me."

"I know I told you." She was looking at the coat rack. "I have been carrying that around as the only thing I knew about him for fifty-five years, apo. That a man I met twice was funnier on paper than in a room. That was the whole of my file."

She adjusted a hanger that did not need adjusting.

"And now I know he taught small children for forty years, which I would not have guessed, and that a mayor went to his funeral, which I would have guessed even less, and that he did not spend his life angry at my friend." Her hands went back to her knees. "That is a great deal to be handed on a Wednesday."

Maya waited. She had gotten better at that this year and knew where she had learned it.

"I said nothing," Nora said. "You know this, I told you. I watched her stop mentioning him and I decided it was not my business and I said nothing, and I have had a very long time to look at that."

"You told me you decided it was both. Respect and cowardice."

"I did say that. It was a good answer and I have used it for about forty years." Nora's mouth did something dry. "I would like to revise it slightly, now that somebody has finally brought me the ending."

"Go on."

"If I had asked her, that first year, in the kitchen, one time, plainly, the way you have been asking everybody in this family since the summer." She stopped and started again, which Maya had never once seen her do. "She would not have told me. I want to be clear about that. She would have changed the subject and put a plate in my hand and I would have let her, and nothing about her life or his would be one bit different than it is."

"Then what's the revision."

"The revision," said Nora, "is that she would have known somebody asked."

She got up and went back to the rack, because she had been sitting for longer than she did.

"That is not nothing. I have decided this year that it is not nothing. It is in fact most of what any of us can do for each other and I spent six decades under the impression it was a small thing." She pulled a coat off and held it out at arm's length, appraising. "You have been very annoying since the summer, apo, and I want you to understand that I mean that as the highest available compliment."

She was reaching for her bag when she remembered, which was not the same as deciding, and she took the phone out instead.

"One more thing and then I'll go."

"You have said that twice."

Maya found the folder and turned the screen around. Seven photographs, taken on her knees on a garage floor in September, of a paperclipped stack she had never worked out what to do with: three women in a kitchen, at a table, once on a beach in coats, over what must have been a decade.

Nora put her glasses on properly, which she had not done for the margin note or for anything else that year.

"That's the Guttenberg apartment," she said. "That's before Daly City."

"That's you."

"That is me at about thirty and I want it noted that I was wearing that coat because it was the only coat." She scrolled with one finger, slowly. "Beach is Pacifica. It was cold. It was always cold. We went anyway because Remedios had a car."

Maya waited about four seconds.

"Who's the third one."

"Remedios," Nora said, without any pause at all. "Remedios Punzalan. Worked nights on the same floor for ten years and then transferred to Oakland and then died in ninety-four, which I only found out because your grandmother told me at the parish and then would not discuss it further." She handed the phone back. "She could not cook. Not one thing. Your grandmother fed her for a decade and complained about it for a decade and cried when she went to Oakland, which she also would not discuss."

Maya stood in a thrift shop with a phone in her hand and a name in her ear that she had read once, in August, on a Mass card in the top drawer of a dresser, and had put on top of the dresser rather than in any box, and had asked her mother about, and had been told *no.*

Kept for thirty-two years in the drawer with everything Perla had touched.

"Nobody knew who she was," Maya said. "I asked. There was nobody left to ask."

"I was left." Nora went back to the rack. "I have been on this Wednesday since nineteen seventy-something, apo. Everybody knows where I am."

She said it entirely without reproach, which made it considerably worse, and she did not look up while she said it, which Maya understood by then was the only way that woman had ever said anything that cost her.

"My grandmother trained a woman in 1991 who said the same thing about her."

"She would have said it about me first." Nora hung the coat. "Send me a picture of the snow. Not on the telephone." She squared a hanger. "A card, with a stamp on it. And when the rest of them start asking you for one, which they will, I would like it understood that I asked first."

"That's petty, Tita."

"I am seventy-eight years old and I have earned the right to a small amount of it," said Nora, and turned back to the coats, and that was the conversation.

Maya was most of the way to the door when she stopped.

"Tita. Did anybody ever ask you?"

Nora's hands did not stop moving on the rack.

"You just did," she said.

# Chapter 65

### The Last One Here

[L]{.dropcap}iza did everything exactly the way Perla had always done it.

The same dishes in the same order. The pancit before the lumpia, which nobody outside this family did, and which had originated at some point in the 1980s for a reason nobody could reconstruct. Rice on at nine. The ceramic rooster on the counter beside the stove.

The rooster had been in the donate pile in August. It was chipped along the comb and it was not attractive and it had been in that pile with Liza's own marker on the box. At some point between August and December it had come back out, and neither of them had mentioned it then or since.

"Next year you'll be doing Chicago Christmas," Liza said, moving it two inches to the left.

"I don't know what that looks like."

"Snow through the window, probably. Marcus's family driving in from Ohio." Her hands kept working. "His mother will want to do something with a ham. I could tell that about her in four minutes on the phone."

"You could come, Ma. Both things can be true."

"I know that."

"You could come every year."

"I know." Liza wiped the counter, which was clean. "I'm not complaining, apo. I'm not doing that. I'm saying a thing out loud that I'm still getting used to."

She put the cloth down.

"This is the last one here. That's all. Last one in this kitchen, with this rooster, before all of it turns into something we visit instead of something we're just inside of."

The tree was up, which surprised her.

Her mother had not put up a tree since 2020 and had said so, several times, in the tone of a woman who has retired from an activity. It was up. It was the small artificial one from the garage and it had been placed in the corner where Perla's had always gone rather than where Liza's own used to go, and neither of them said a single word about any of that.

They ate at nine, the correct hour since before Maya was born, and there was too much food for two people in a way that neither of them commented on.

Liza made the pancit slightly wrong. Maya noticed and said nothing. Her mother noticed her noticing and said, to the stove, "It's the noodles they had. It isn't the same brand," and that was the entire exchange.

Midnight Mass at St. Augustine's, the same pew.

Perla had claimed it by some unspoken parish arrangement sometime in the eighties and had held it for four decades without anybody ever formally granting it to her. Liza sat down in it without discussion. Two people who had presumably been sitting somewhere else all year adjusted around them without comment.

The church was full in the way it is full exactly twice a year, which meant a substantial population of people neither of them recognized and who did not know the responses, and Liza's face throughout the first fifteen minutes was a study in a woman declining to comment on this.

Maya got through the readings fine.

It was a hymn that took her, one she had sung every Christmas of her life without once attending to a single word of it, and she found herself crying quietly in the middle of the second verse, holding the book, not really from sadness.

It was closer to fullness. Standing at the exact place where an ending and a beginning overlap and being unable to say which part of the feeling belonged to which.

Liza noticed. She did not turn her head or look over or say anything.

She reached across and took her daughter's hand and held it, steadily, for the rest of the hymn, and then let go when everybody sat down.

Fifty-five years earlier, on a hospital stairwell, a man had sat down one step below a young woman crying about a deadline she could not bring herself to face, and had asked her no questions about it.

Maya would never know that story. Nobody alive did.

She only knew that her mother had reached over without looking, and that it had been exactly right, and that nobody in this family had ever needed to be told how to do that.

They stayed for coffee after Mass in the parish hall, the same room that had hosted the misa in August, decorated now with tinsel instead of leche flan trays, and Aunty Baby found them both by the coffee urn to say, with real warmth, that she expected postcards from Chicago, actual paper ones, not just phone pictures, because a woman her age deserved something she could hold in her actual hand.

The parish hall after Mass was three-quarters full at one in the morning, which Maya had forgotten was normal and which struck her, that year, as one of the genuinely strange and good things about the place: two hundred people who had all agreed, without discussion, that the correct hour to eat pancit was one a.m. on the twenty-fifth of December.

"I'll send you the first snowfall," Maya promised. "Actual postmarked proof."

"See that you do." Aunty Baby patted her cheek once and wandered off toward the dessert table, already deep in some new piece of parish gossip before she'd made it halfway across the room.

# Chapter 66

### The Cemetery

[S]{.dropcap}he went to Colma the day after Christmas, alone, without telling her mother.

The fog was thick enough that the headstones surfaced one at a time as she walked, resolving out of the gray about ten feet ahead and going back into it behind her, so that the whole cemetery existed only in the small circle she was standing in.

There were two other cars in the whole cemetery and a man on a bench about thirty yards off, alone, doing nothing at all.

He had been there when she arrived and was still there when she left, and neither of them acknowledged the other in any way, which felt like the correct etiquette of the place. Everybody there is doing the same thing. Nobody wants to be observed doing it.

She had not brought flowers.

She had thought about it in the car and had decided somewhere on the freeway that flowers were the wrong object. They were for the front of a stone, arranged, visible to whoever came past next. They were a thing you left in order to have left it.

What she had instead was a container of the torta, made that morning, still faintly warm through the dish towel.

The grave was six months old. The grass had come in unevenly over it, patchy at one end, and there was a small hard ridge along one side where the ground had settled and not quite finished. Somebody had been out here before her: a Christmas wreath, cheap, wired, propped against the base. Her mother, presumably, or Tita Fe, or both, separately, neither mentioning it.

Maya sat down on the wet grass beside it.

Not in front of it. Beside it, the way you sit down at a table next to somebody rather than across.

She did not say anything for a long time.

The things she might have said were all available and she looked at each of them and put each of them down. *I found him. I know his name. He was happy. He never blamed you.* All true. All of them things she had come here holding, the way you carry a report to somebody's desk.

And every single one of them, she understood, sitting there getting damp through her jeans, would have been for her own benefit. There was nobody here to be relieved by any of it. Her grandmother had died knowing Ramon Aguilar had lived a good life. She had found it out somehow, in some year, and had told no one, and had gone back to a cookbook and written four words about garlic.

So Maya sat with her.

That was all. Fog, wet grass, a badly wired wreath, the hum of the 280 somewhere off past the fence.

At some point she took the lid off the container and set it down on the grass at the base of the stone.

It made no sense as a gesture. Nobody would eat it. It would be gone by morning, taken by birds or a groundskeeper, and there was no version of this in which it accomplished anything at all.

She left it anyway. Lid off.

That was, if anything, the point: a dish exists in order to be eaten by somebody, and if the somebody is a crow then it is a crow.

The wreath had a card on it. She had not seen it going in.

It said, in her mother's handwriting, *Merry Christmas, Ma,* and nothing else, and it had been rained on at some point and the ink had run at one corner, and Liza had not mentioned coming out here to anybody.

Then she got up, brushed the wet off the back of her jeans, and walked out through the fog toward the car, and on the way she thought about the specific, useless, ordinary fact that she had spent an hour that morning making a dish for somebody who could not eat it.

# Chapter 67

### Leaving

[B]{.dropcap}efore any of that, on the last day of school before winter break, Room 14 threw her a party she hadn't asked for and couldn't have stopped if she'd tried, twenty-six kids and a cake somebody's mother had ordered that said MS. B WE'LL MISS YOU in slightly crooked blue icing, and Dario, appointed by nobody in particular as the class spokesperson, stood up in front of everyone and announced, with total seven-year-old solemnity, that Chicago was allowed to have her but only if they promised to take good care of her, as though the entire city were a single entity capable of receiving and honoring that instruction.

"I'll email pictures of snow," she told them. "Actual snow, not the fake kind in movies."

"Is it cold enough to freeze your eyeballs," Dario wanted to know, with genuine scientific interest, and she told him she'd find out and report back, and he seemed satisfied with that answer in the way only a child fully invested in a stranger's future weather could be.

The staff gave her a card, which she read in her car and could not finish.

It was signed by everybody including two people she had spoken to perhaps six times in five years, and the custodian, Ernie, had written four lines in small careful print about the year she had helped him find his glasses, an event she had no memory of whatsoever.
