Roz cried a little at the copier on the actual last day, unashamed about it, blaming it loudly on the toner fumes in a way neither of them believed for a second. "You better actually call," she said, pulling Maya into a hard hug amid the smell of warm paper. "Not the kind of promise where you mean it in the moment and then life happens. The actual kind."

"The actual kind," Maya promised, and meant it.

The last thing she did in Room 14 was the walls.

She had not thought about the walls. She had thought about the boxes and the files and the handover notes for Mr. Ocampo, and had done all of that efficiently over two afternoons, and then had come in on the final morning to a room that was still entirely hers from the waist up.

Five years of things taped to things. The alphabet strip. A poster about the water cycle that had been up so long the tape had gone amber. Twenty-six self-portraits from September. A photograph of a class from 2021 in which four children were making the same face.

It took two and a half hours to take it all down, and it was, by an enormous margin, the worst part of the entire month.

She left the fish instructions, and the water cycle poster, on the grounds that the tape was older than several of the children and would take paint with it.

The last thing she did was write her name on the whiteboard, in the corner, small, the way you do, and then look at it for a second and rub it out with the side of her hand.

The apartment came apart faster than she expected once she started, five years of a life folding down into boxes over one long weekend, Marcus flying out ahead of her to start setting up the Chicago place while Liza came over every evening to help and, more than once, to simply sit on the floor amid half-packed boxes saying nothing at all, which Maya had come to understand, over the last several months, was one of her mother's actual love languages, showing up to occupy space rather than filling every silence with words.

Liza brought the recipe box over on the second-to-last evening, without being asked, and set it on the counter between them the way you put down something you have decided not to make a speech about.

"It was in the garage. It's been in the garage since August."

Maya looked at it. Tin, cream-colored, a hinge gone at one end so the lid sat slightly proud. It had lived on top of the refrigerator so long that the paint behind it had stayed a different shade, and Maya had walked past it four hundred times and had, in five months of turning her grandmother's whole life over, never opened it.

She opened it now, standing up, with her mother watching.

Recipe cards. Three or four dozen, in three different hands, mostly not Perla's. A pancit from somebody named Lita. Two versions of the same leche flan, one of them crossed out with a single line and no comment, which is its own kind of comment.

And behind the divider, in the section for things that were not recipes, what an eight-year-old had once watched a card go into: a coupon, forty cents off, expired in 1996. A Mass card. A slip of paper with a phone number on it and no name, in a hand neither of them recognized.

That was all.

Maya went through it twice to be sure, and then a third time holding the box up to the window, because there is a specific stubbornness that gets into a person after a certain number of months and it does not switch off on schedule.

"It's a coupon," Liza said.

"It's a coupon."

"What did you think was going to be in there?"

"I have absolutely no idea," Maya said, and started laughing, and could not stop for a while, and her mother stood in a half-packed kitchen and let her.

The other torta card, the one Perla had taken out of an eight-year-old's hand that afternoon at the counter and put back in the wrong section, was not in the box and had not been in the box for a long time. It is not anywhere. Somebody threw it out, at some point, in some year, along with a great many other pieces of paper, and nobody noticed, and nobody ever will.

Maya took the coupon out and put it in her wallet, where it still is, and gave the box to her mother.

The cookbook came last, wrapped in a dish towel and cushioned in the very center of a box marked KITCHEN, the same careful handling Perla herself had once given a folded invitation and a visa letter for fifty-five years.

"You'll bring it back to visit," Liza said, the night before the movers came, not quite a question.

"Every summer. I'm going to tell whoever hires me that I need July off, every year, no matter what."

"Good." Liza looked around the half-emptied apartment, boxes stacked at odd angles in every corner, and something crossed her face that wasn't quite sadness, closer to a kind of settled, complicated pride. "Your grandmother would have liked this part, I think. Not the leaving. The choosing. She never really got the choosing part for herself, not out loud, not until it was almost too late to matter to anyone but her. You're getting to do it at twenty-seven, and out loud, and to somebody's face."

"I wouldn't have gotten here without her, though. Without all of it."

"No," Liza agreed. "I don't suppose you would have." She reached over and squeezed Maya's hand once, brief and fierce. "Go pack your kitchen, apo. The movers come at eight."

# Chapter 68

### Marcus Goes First

[H]{.dropcap}e flew out in the first week of January, three weeks ahead of her, to start the fellowship and make the apartment habitable before her things arrived.

She had not expected the size of those three weeks.

That caught her. She had been braced for the move, for the cold, for the job hunt. Nobody had mentioned that the hardest stretch might be the one where she was still in her own apartment, in her own city, with her own furniture, and simply alone in it, twenty minutes from her mother, waiting.

They had never done distance. Five years in the same city, twenty-five minutes apart, and the longest either of them had gone without seeing the other was eleven days when he was at a conference in Vancouver.

He called every night at seven her time, nine his.

The first week was logistics and complaint. The building's radiators, which had two settings, off and Caribbean. A bed frame he assembled wrong twice before establishing that one of the side rails had been packed upside down. A neighbor's dog with what he described as "a real gift for projection."

The second week he started sending photographs.

An empty room with the light coming in at an angle. The same room with a chair in it. The chair moved eighteen inches. A photograph of the chair in its original position with the caption *no. wrong. I was wrong.*

"You're doing the thing where you make the apartment into a project so you don't notice you're by yourself in it."

"I am absolutely doing that."

"Okay. As long as you know."

"I know exactly what I'm doing and I intend to keep doing it until the twenty-second," Marcus said. "At which point I will stop, because you'll be here, and the apartment will have to survive on its own merits."

"Good."

"Also I found a grocery store two blocks over that has decent soy sauce, which I did not expect, and which I have already told four people about, including my landlord."

The Filipino grocery came in the second week too. He led up to it badly, with obvious pride.

"So there's a thing I want to tell you and I want you to know I found it by myself."

"Okay."

"There's an actual Filipino grocery. Not an aisle. A store. Forty minutes away, so it's a whole outing, but it exists." A pause. "I bought you patis."

"You bought me fish sauce."

"I bought you fish sauce and it's sitting on the counter and honestly it's making its presence known in this apartment. It's like a small pungent housewarming gift for a person who isn't here yet."

"Marcus."

"I've been saving up small specific gestures for three weeks in case you needed convincing, is what I'm telling you."

"I don't need convincing."

"I know. I've been doing it anyway." His voice changed. "It's strange being here without you. Good-strange, mostly. The place feels like it's waiting for the rest of itself."

The last night in the apartment she slept on a mattress on the floor with everything else gone, and lay awake listening to a building she had lived in for five years making sounds she had stopped hearing about four and a half years ago and could hear again, perfectly, now that there was nothing in the room to absorb them.

Her mother drove her to the airport, which took forty minutes and during which they discussed, exclusively: the traffic, a cousin's new job, whether the flight would be full, and a restaurant on Mission that had closed.

At the drop-off Liza put the car in park, which you are not supposed to do there, and hugged her daughter for slightly longer than the arrangement of the front seats comfortably allowed, and said, "Call me when you land," and then, as Maya was getting out, "and don't call me tonight, call me tomorrow, you'll be tired."

She flew out on the twenty-second.

The cold arrived at the jet bridge door and got inside her coat before she had gone six steps. She had been warned about it in specific terms, repeatedly, and had read about it since October, and none of that turned out to be transferable.

The flight itself was four and a half hours during which she did not sleep, did not read, and watched roughly forty minutes of a film about a heist before losing the thread of it entirely.

Somewhere over what she assumed was Nebraska, on a clear night, she looked down at an enormous dark expanse with a scattering of small orange grids on it, and thought about a twenty-one-year-old woman on a considerably longer flight in 1969, going the other way, with two printed invitations at the bottom of one suitcase underneath her uniforms so they would not crease.

He was at baggage claim in a scarf that had plainly belonged to his mother, holding nothing, having arrived far too early.

"Welcome home," he said, and took the scarf off and put it around her neck before she had gotten her bag properly onto her shoulder.

The word landed oddly in her chest. Home, applied without hesitation to a city she had been to twice, one of those times for a wedding.

She stood there in an airport in a borrowed scarf that smelled faintly of somebody else's laundry detergent and understood, with unusual clarity for someone who had been awake since four in the morning, that home was not the apartment and was not the city.

It had become, at some point in the preceding several months without her noticing the day it happened, this person, standing in a terminal, early, waiting.

# Chapter 69

### First Weeks

[C]{.dropcap}hicago in January introduced itself with a cold she had no prior frame of reference for, the kind that made the inside of her nose hurt on the four-block walk from the train, and that turned the lake, when she finally worked up the nerve to go and look at it, into something vast and slate-gray and faintly terrifying in a way the Pacific, for all its own moods, had never managed to be.

The first week she did not leave the apartment except with Marcus, which she recognized as unlike her and did nothing about.

The building had twelve units and she met nobody. In Daly City she had known the name of every person on her floor, and the dog on the ground floor, and the schedule of the man who parked badly. Here she rode the elevator four times with the same woman in a green coat and neither of them said anything beyond a nod.

She was not unhappy. It took her a while to find the word for what she was, and the word turned out to be *unrecognized*: a person who had been legible for twenty-seven years walking around a city with no way of reading her at all.

She cried twice in the first two weeks. Once because the grocery store did not carry a brand of soy sauce she had never in her life had to think about buying. Once, harder, at two in the morning, from a homesickness that arrived with nothing attached to it.

Marcus found her awake the second time, sat up without complaining about it, and did not try to fix anything. He held on until it wore itself out, and in the morning made the coffee too strong the way he always did, and did not mention it unless she did.

The substitute work came slower than she had hoped, the credential tangled in exactly the bureaucratic slowness he had warned her about, and for a month she filled the days with unpacking and long walks in a coat that was not equal to the situation.

Liza called every Sunday, and by the third call had a habit of asking, before anything else, what Maya had cooked that week.

"I made the adobo," Maya told her in February. "From memory. No recipe. It was almost right. Not quite Lola's. Not quite yours either."

"That's how it's supposed to go, apo. Every generation gets it a little bit wrong in its own direction."

Roz made good on the actual kind of promise in March, flying out for a long weekend under the transparent pretext of a teaching conference which happened, conveniently, to be held in Chicago that particular year.

They did almost nothing all weekend, which was the point.

What Maya had not understood she was missing, until it was sitting across a table from her, was the absence of preamble. Two months of new colleagues, all of them perfectly kind, and every conversation of any depth had required laying foundations first: who her mother was, why the move, what the cookbook was, why a recipe carried what it carried.

Roz needed none of it. Roz had been there for all of it, in real time, at a copier.

"How's your mom really?"

"Cooking for a crowd that isn't coming. Same as ever."

"Dario asks about you every week, by the way." Roz worked at a piece of crust. "Whether you've frozen your eyeballs yet. I told him I'd get photographic evidence either way."

"Tell him it's been close a couple of mornings."

"He'll be thrilled. He's been rooting for frozen eyeballs since September." Roz put the crust down. "He asked Ocampo in January whether teachers are allowed to have favorites, and then told him the answer before he could get to it. Second grade is a very intense ten months and then it's over and they go on to somebody else. You know this. You've done it five times."

"It's different from this side."

"It's exactly the same from this side," Roz said. "You've just never been on this side before." She turned her glass around on the table. "Ocampo left the fish instructions up, by the way. The tape's gone yellow. He won't take it down."

Maya had to look at the table for a second.

The other thing came out in April, by accident, four weeks after it started.

"What's that noise?"

"That's nothing."

"Ma, it's running water."

"I'm washing a brush," Liza said, and then, after a pause in which she visibly considered several exits, "I've been taking a class."

Watercolor, Wednesday evenings, at the community center on Hillside, taught by a retired art teacher named Dolores who had, according to Liza, opinions about edges. There were nine people in it and Liza was the youngest by fifteen years, a fact she reported with satisfaction on three separate Sundays. Her first attempt was the view from her own kitchen window and came out, in her own words, like the ocean had had a disagreement with a lemon.

She described this laughing at herself, in a register Maya had heard perhaps four times in twenty-seven years, and Maya sat on a couch two thousand miles away and said nothing for a second because her throat had gone tight.

"I like that I'm bad at it. I have not been bad at anything on purpose since I was nineteen years old, apo, and it turns out it's restful."

Three weeks after that, and considerably more reluctantly:

"I'm having dinner with Ben Tolentino on Friday."

Maya put down what she was holding.

"Ma, that's the man you turned down."

"That's the man I said no to three years ago, yes, and he has decided to try again, which at fifty-one shows a persistence I have chosen to find admirable." Liza's voice had gone brisk, which meant she was uncomfortable and would like to be finished. "It's a dinner. He wants to go to the place by the pier that everybody says is overpriced."

"It is overpriced."

"It's extremely overpriced. I'm going anyway."

"Why?"

The pause went on long enough that Maya checked the screen.

"Because I used to close a door before I'd properly looked through it," Liza said. "I've been doing it since your father died and I was doing it before that, if I'm honest, which I have been trying to be lately. If it's nothing then it's nothing and I'll have eaten some overpriced fish. But I'd rather find that out by going than by deciding it in advance and never knowing." She cleared her throat. "Painting badly on Wednesdays seemed like a reasonable place to start."

# Chapter 70

### One Year

[T]{.dropcap}he framed invitation hung in the same spot in Liza's bedroom seven months on, when Maya flew back for the July visit she had promised and kept.

The house looked different. That was the first thing. Not the furniture, which was identical. Something about the arrangement of it, a lamp moved, a stack of unfamiliar paperbacks on the side table where there had never been books before.

Also a watercolor, framed, in the hall. Not a good one. Recognizably a bowl of something.

"Dolores made us frame one," Liza said, when she caught Maya looking. "She says the frame is part of the practice. I think she has an arrangement with the shop."

"It's a bowl."

"It's a bowl of *lemons,* and I'm aware of the quality, thank you."

"Did you do it from life?"

"I did it from a photograph of a bowl of lemons that Dolores put on the table, which is not the same and which she made a point about." Liza straightened it half an inch on the hook. "Next term is faces. I intend to be extremely bad at faces."

"I like it."

"You don't have to like it. That's not what it's for."

They stood in front of the invitation the second morning with coffee. Her mother walked to it without appearing to decide to, which was how Maya worked out it was a ritual in the first place.

"I dreamed about her last week," Liza said. "First time since the funeral. Not sad. She was in the kitchen making the torta and telling me I was chopping the onions wrong."

"Did she say anything? About any of it?"

"No. She said my knife technique was an embarrassment to the whole family." Liza laughed, easy, the unguarded laugh Maya had only started hearing from her this year. "I don't need her to apologize to me in a dream. I needed to be able to picture her whole again, doing something ordinary, without the invitation sitting underneath it. And I can."

"I found out who the third woman was," Maya said. "In the photographs. From the garage."

Liza put her coffee down.

"Remedios Punzalan. Tita Nora knew her the whole time. She worked nights on Lola's floor for ten years and then went to Oakland and died in ninety-four."

"The Mass card."

"The Mass card."

Her mother looked at the wall for a moment.

"I said no," Liza said. "You asked me and I said no and I meant it, I had never heard the name in my life." She picked the coffee back up. "And Nora was four miles away on a Wednesday."

"I know."

"I know you know. I'm saying it out loud because I have decided I am going to say things out loud now." She drank some of it. "Write it on the back of the photograph. All seven. Her name and the year, in pen, so that whoever gets them next does not have to run around this town asking seventy-eight-year-olds."

Tita Fe came for dinner and brought the adobo and told the Rodel Fernandez story again, embellished slightly further, in the way the whole family had come to accept as its permanent shape now.

Maya called Rico on the morning of her flight, from the rental in her mother's driveway with the engine running and Liza still inside looking for something, and asked him the thing she had not asked in ten months.

"You told me once you had something folded up. You said ask again."

There was a stretch of Sacramento on the line. A television. A dog. One of his kids losing an argument in another room.

"I did say that."

"So I'm asking."

"It's still a real maybe."

"That's fine."

"I want to be clear it isn't a family maybe."

"I know the difference now. You're the one who taught me the difference."

He laughed, once, and then didn't, and the noise of his house went on behind him.

"Ask me again," he said. "Not in a few years. Sooner. I'll have gotten somewhere with it by then, probably."

"Okay."

"Maya."

"Yeah."

"Nobody in this family asks twice."

She sat in the driveway with the phone in her lap before she went back in for her bag, and thought that it had cost her under two minutes.

# Chapter 71

### Her Own Classroom

[T]{.dropcap}he substitute year taught her things she would not have chosen to learn.

Chiefly: that a classroom is not a room. She had spent five years believing she loved teaching, and the substitute year established that what she had actually loved was teaching *twenty-six specific children she knew the names of,* in a room she had built, in a building where the office manager knew which parents to send directly to her.

Without those, the job was recognizable but hollow, like a photograph of a meal.

She took every assignment she was offered from February to June. She was good at it, because she was good at the underlying craft, and it did not make her happy at all, and she stopped apologizing to Marcus for saying so out loud somewhere around March.

She interviewed at four schools between March and June.

She did not get the first two.

The third called on a Thursday and offered her fifth grade, and she said thank you, and asked how long she had, and was told a week.

It was a good school. That was the first problem with it. Twenty-two minutes from the apartment against forty for anywhere else she had a hope of, a principal who had asked her two questions nobody else had thought to ask, a salary step that credited all five of her California years instead of three. Fifth grade rather than second, which she had never taught, and which everybody kept telling her was the same job with taller people in it.

She did not tell Marcus for two days.

She understood, on the second night, standing at the sink with the water running over a plate she had already washed, precisely what she was doing and exactly what it was called, and it did not stop her doing it for another twenty minutes.

Then she turned the tap off and went into the other room and told him.

"Okay," he said, and put the laptop on the floor. "What do you want to do?"

"I don't know."

"That's allowed."

"It isn't, actually, that's sort of the entire problem." She sat down on the arm of the chair. "I've got until Thursday. And what I noticed tonight is that I have a very good version of this where I take six days to think about it, and on the sixth day I call them and it's already gone to somebody else, and then it isn't something I did. It's just something that happened to a woman with a busy week."

Marcus did not say anything for a second.

"Do you want fifth grade?"

"No."

"Say the rest of it."

"I want a second-grade room in a building where somebody's grandmother waits at the fence an hour early." Her voice did something on the way through that and she let it. "And I have no evidence at all that such a job exists here, and I have been substituting since February, and if I say no to this I have nothing, and I might have nothing in August, and I might have nothing in January again."

"That's true," Marcus said. "All of that's true."

"You're supposed to argue with me."

"I'm not going to argue with you, I'm going to sit here." He was, in fact, sitting there. "You know what you want. I've watched you not know what you wanted and this isn't what that looks like."

She called them on the Monday, three days early, from the car in a parking lot with the engine off, because the alternative was calling them on the Thursday and she knew what the Thursday would do to her.

The woman on the other end was gracious about it, and said they would keep her file, and meant it in the way people mean that.

Maya sat in the car afterward with the phone in her lap for a while.

It did not feel good. That was the discovery, and nobody had warned her about it either: that saying a true thing out loud on purpose, in time, to a person who is inconvenienced by it, feels almost exactly like making a mistake, and goes on feeling that way for some weeks, and is not one.

Marcus did not once suggest she should have taken it. She noticed that, and kept it.

She got the permanent position the following August: second grade, a school forty minutes south of the apartment, in a neighborhood where the pickup line ran in four languages and one of them was Tagalog, which she had not expected to find here and which caught her sideways the first time she heard it through a car window.

She got it in the second round of interviews, in June, in a windowless conference room, from a panel of three who asked her a question about differentiated instruction that she had answered in her sleep for five years.

The question that actually decided it, she suspected afterward, was the last one, and it was not on the form.

"Why here?"

And Maya had said, without preparing it: "Because I heard Tagalog in your pickup line, and I have not heard it here in six months, and I would like my life to have that in it."

Two of the three panelists wrote something down.

Room 9 had a radiator that leaked, a whiteboard carrying the permanent ghost of somebody's cursive from a previous decade, a window that opened but did not close, and twenty-four children she did not yet know in any way at all.

She had the week before the first day to make it hers, and she used every hour of it.

She arranged the desks in pods and hated it. She arranged them in rows and hated that more. She put them in a horseshoe on Wednesday and left them there for a full day before admitting the horseshoe did not work for a room this shape, and went back to pods, slightly differently.

She taped the alphabet along the top of the board. She found the previous teacher's labeling system in a cupboard, entirely intact, incompatible with her own, and spent two hours peeling it off.

She wrote twenty-four names on twenty-four cards, in her best print, and stuck one to every desk, and then stood in the doorway looking at a room full of names belonging to children she had never met, which is a particular feeling she had experienced exactly once before, six years earlier, and had assumed she would never get to have again.

Terror and joy in roughly equal proportion. It had not changed at all.

On the wall behind her desk, above the filing cabinet, she hung one thing that had nothing to do with second grade.

It was a photocopy, enlarged to about twice actual size, of a single page from a green spiral-bound cookbook. A recipe in small forward-leaning handwriting. Amendments in the margins in the same hand, in three different pens. And at the bottom, underneath the ingredients, four words in a language nobody in that building could read, with a single letter underlined hard enough that the pen had nearly gone through, and the photocopy had caught that: you could see the pressure in it.

A student asked in the second week, later than Maya had expected.

Her name was Josephine and she was one of those children who reads every word on every wall of a room, systematically, over a period of days, and then begins asking about them in order.

"What's that one?"

"That's my grandmother's handwriting."

"Is it a recipe?"

"It is. It's for something she used to make. Egg and pork and a lot of garlic. I make it too now."

Josephine studied the page for a while.

"What does the part on the side say? The little part."

"It's a name," Maya said. "Part of one, anyway. Somebody she loved, a long time ago, in a different country, before she came here."

"Did she marry him?"

"No. She married somebody else and she was very happy. And he married somebody else and he was very happy too."

Josephine took this in with the total gravity children bring to adult information they have no framework for whatsoever, and then went back to her seat without saying anything further about it, and asked about the periodic table poster the following Tuesday.

In May, near the end of that first year, she brought in a photograph of her own grandmother, unprompted, in a plastic sleeve, and put it on Maya's desk without explanation and went to lunch.

Maya kept it there for the rest of the week and gave it back on the Friday, and neither of them ever discussed it, and it remains one of the three or four best things that has ever happened to her in a classroom.

# Chapter 72

### The Second Letter

[I]{.dropcap}t came in October, almost a year after either of them had last written, on a Tuesday evening while Maya was grading and Marcus was at the lab, and she saw the name on the screen and had to sit down before opening it.

*I hope this isn't strange.*

*My mother passed last month. She was eighty. It wasn't sudden, so we had time, which I understand now was its own kind of gift even though it did not feel like one while it was happening.*

Maya put the phone down for a second, then picked it up again.

*I am going through her things, which you will know something about.*

*I found something I think you should have.*

*She kept a notebook of my father's recipes. He never wrote anything down himself, not once in his life, so she used to stand in the kitchen and watch him cook and write it out afterward from memory. There are about thirty in there. Some of them just say things like "more than you think."*

*The torta is in it.*

*At the top of the page, in her handwriting, she has written: R's, from his mother. Too much garlic. He is right about it and I will never tell him so.*

Maya read that line, and then read it again, and then sat entirely still in her own kitchen for a while.

*I laughed for about ten minutes,* Fely wrote. *Alone, in her house, surrounded by her things, laughing like a lunatic.*

*Then I cried, which I hadn't expected, because I'd been doing all right until then.*

*Two women who never met each other, six thousand miles apart, writing almost exactly the same joke about the same man and the same dish. Neither one of them ever knowing the other one had written it.*

*I don't know what to do with that except tell you. You're the only other person alive who would understand precisely why it's funny.*

Maya got up.

She took the cookbook down from its rectangle of counter and opened it to the torta page, and set the phone beside it, and looked at the two of them together.

*R's, from his mother. Too much garlic. He is right about it and I will never tell him so.*

And on the page beside the phone, under Perla's own torta, in a language Maya could now read perhaps forty words of, in a hand she had spent a year learning to recognize on sight, four words and a single letter underlined hard enough to nearly go through the paper.

Same dish. Same man's mother's recipe. Two women, each of them conceding it in writing, in permanent ink, and neither of them ever once saying so out loud.

She sat with the phone in her lap for a while first, in a kitchen in Illinois, with two pages side by side on the counter behind her.

She wrote back that night.

*It's not strange. I'm so glad you wrote.*

*I'm very sorry about your mother. I know what the going-through-her-things part is like and I'm sorry you're doing it.*

*Thank you for telling me about the notebook. I've had the two pages side by side on my counter for an hour.*

*I think they would have liked each other. Which is a ridiculous thing to say about two women who spent their entire lives not knowing the other one existed. But I think it's true and I wanted to say it to somebody.*

Fely answered in the morning, Maya's time.

*I think so too.*

*I think they would have argued about the garlic for about twenty minutes and then agreed about everything else for the rest of their lives.*

They have written perhaps nine times since. Birthdays, mostly, and once when Fely's eldest had a baby, and once when Maya sent a photograph of snow on a fire escape with no message attached at all because none was required.

Neither of them has ever suggested meeting. Neither of them has ever mentioned not suggesting it.

Maya kept the two pages side by side on the counter for three days before she put the cookbook back.

Marcus noticed on the second evening and did not move it, and did not ask, and worked around it while cooking, which required him to use a different section of counter and which he did without comment for three consecutive days.

# Chapter 73

### The Blank Page

[S]{.dropcap}he started her own margin notes in earnest that autumn.

The book had acquired a smell by then, which undid her the first time she noticed it, standing at the counter with her hands full. Fifty years of kitchens and the paper had taken it up: oil, garlic, the ghost of a hundred Sundays. It had been in Illinois since January and at close range it still smelled like an apartment in Daly City that no longer existed. She had stopped opening it near the stove for that reason. It seemed important not to add anything new to it too quickly.

Not the polite kind. She had been writing the polite kind for a year without noticing it was a different activity: *less butter next time,* *oven runs hot,* *doubled this, worked fine.* Useful. Impersonal. The kind of note anybody could have left.

The other kind took longer to start, because the other kind required deciding that a day was worth writing down.

*Marcus cannot make rice. I have accepted this. It is the only thing he is bad at and I have decided it is important to me that he stay bad at it.*

*First real snow, October 30th. I cried, a little, in the parking lot at school, and then went in and taught a full day and nobody knew. Do not tell Roz. She will make it a whole thing.*

*Called Ma. She has a dinner on Friday with the man from the parish. She pretended not to be excited about it. She was extremely excited about it. She talked about his car for four minutes.*

*Second graders here say "pop" instead of "soda." I have decided to die on this hill.*

*Downstairs neighbor taught me a thing with dill. Neighbor says less butter. Neighbor is wrong.*

*Josephine asked about the page on the wall today. Told her the truth, most of it. She said "that's not how it works." Give her thirty years.*

*Bad week. Making this because it takes eleven minutes.*

She stopped when she wrote that one down. Sat looking at it for a while with the pen still in her hand, because she had not planned it and had not remembered, consciously, where the sentence came from until it was already on the page in her own handwriting.

She left it. It seemed important not to cross it out.

She has read that entry perhaps forty times since. It has never once told her anything about the week it refers to and it has never stopped being the most interesting sentence in the book.

She has never dated it, either, which means that anybody reading it eventually will have exactly what she had: a bad week, unspecified, in a life otherwise recorded in some detail.

She thought about that for a while and decided she was at peace with it. Not everything has to be recoverable. Some things are entitled to be a fact about a person that nobody gets the rest of.

The distinction she had arrived at in December still held: it is not whether you keep a thing. It is whether somebody who loves you could pick it up and ask, and get an answer.

Anybody who finds this page can ask her. That is the whole of the difference, and it is not a small one.

She was aware that she was doing something. She was less clear on what. It was not a message to anybody, which had taken her a while to get right: for a few weeks in September the notes had come out slightly performed, written with some imaginary future reader looking over her shoulder, and she had recognized the tone immediately because she had spent an entire year reading a woman who never wrote that way.

Perla Bautista had not been writing to a granddaughter. That was the whole thing. She had been a woman with a pen and a book she opened every week, putting down what a day had contained, and the fact that somebody eventually read it fifty years later was an accident of the book surviving.

So Maya stopped performing, and after that it got easier.

She thought sometimes about who would find it, in the ordinary way you think about that, without any particular weight attached. A child, if that happened; they had started talking about it in the vague, careful register of two people who both wanted to and were both waiting for the other one to say so first. A niece. Some cousin's kid she had never met.

Or nobody. That was a real possibility and it did not trouble her as much as she would have expected.

Somebody, eventually, clearing out an apartment with a roll of packing tape and a relative standing in the hall saying *the donate box, everything goes in the donate box, we are not keeping a bread machine.*

Whoever it turned out to be, they were going to find the whole of her in there.

Every argument about garlic. Every ordinary Tuesday. The bad weeks, written down. Every large thing she had been frightened of and had gone ahead and said out loud anyway, and the two or three she hadn't, written down as the failures they were.

Nothing folded inside anything else. Nothing filed at the bottom of a box underneath everything, unlabeled, for somebody to find in a cold garage sixty years from now and have to guess at.

Just a woman's actual life, in the margins of the only book she reliably opened, left out on the counter where anyone who loved her could pick it up.

She put the pen down and closed the cover and pushed it back into its rectangle of counter space.

Outside it had started doing the thing it does there in November, which she had been assured for a year was snow and which she had come to accept was snow, though it still did not look to her like anything she had been shown in a film.

She went to see about dinner.

There was rice on, and there was a thing in the pan that would be finished in eleven minutes, and it was a Tuesday, and it was entirely ordinary, and the book was on the counter where anybody who came into that kitchen could pick it up.

# Interlude: Daly City, eleven years before the funeral {.unnumbered}

[S]{.dropcap}he was sixty-seven the year her hands first started giving her trouble, arthritis settling into her knuckles the way it did eventually into most old nurses' hands, a small, unglamorous betrayal after a lifetime of good service. She'd taken to sitting at the kitchen table most afternoons now that Ernesto was gone, going through the cookbook slowly, correcting old recipes in the margins the way she always had, since a woman who stopped arguing with her own recipes was a woman who'd stopped paying attention to her own life.

She reached the torta recipe on an ordinary Tuesday and sat with it a long while, pen uncapped and resting against the page, before she let herself write anything at all.

She had never once, in forty-four years, said his name to anybody in this country who did not already know it. Not Ernesto, who had never asked twice. Not Liza, who had asked exactly once, at seventeen, and been given two sentences, and who had been given the rest of it years later over a bowl of peas, everything except the name. Not her granddaughter, who knew nothing at all, who thought of her, she suspected, the way grandchildren generally thought of grandmothers, as a woman who had simply always existed exactly as she currently appeared, with no real life of her own before them.

She wasn't writing it down for any of them. She understood that clearly, pen finally touching paper. She was writing it because word had reached her, three years earlier now, through a cousin still living in Miagao who kept up with everyone's news the way small towns always produced at least one person willing to do, that Ramon had passed the year before, that he had lived, by every account reaching her secondhand, a full and genuinely happy life, that he had never spoken ill of her to anyone, not once, in forty years. Perla had read that letter on this same kitchen table and had cried for the better part of an hour, not from grief exactly, or not only grief.

She had a pen in her hand for some time before she used it. Arthritis makes that a longer operation than it used to be, and she had also, she would have admitted if anybody had been there to ask, no clear idea what she intended to write.

She wrote four words in the margin, then his initial beneath them, underlined once, hard, and did not write anything else, because there was nothing else that needed saying, not to herself, not to anyone. Not this one anymore. A woman, at sixty-seven, closing an argument that had run, unresolved, for forty-four years.

She made the dish that evening. That is a detail nobody ever knew and nobody ever will: that having written the note, at sixty-seven, alone, she then got up and made the torta with the correct amount of garlic and ate it by herself at her own kitchen table, and washed one plate, and went to bed.

She closed the cookbook, set it back in its place between the chipped rooster and the missalettes, and went on, for another eleven ordinary years, being exactly the woman her whole family already knew her to be: sharp-tongued, generous, and endlessly particular about garlic.

# The End {.unnumbered .unlisted .the-end}

The End
